Ch 01 — The Whale and the Virus: How Scientists Study Evolution

44 questions
Q001
p15Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: E
Like Einstein's General Theory of Relativity, evolutionary theory is a set of overarching mechanisms and principles backed by decades of evidence, examination, experimentation, and testing. A scientific theory is not a 'guess.' Evolution has been directly observed in lab and nature, and its evidence spans geology, genetics, geography, and more. Incorrect options: (A) 'Theory' colloquially means 'guess,' but to scientists it carries weight. (B) There is abundant evidence, from Darwin to the Modern Synthesis. (C) Science is not about 'belief'; it follows evidence. (D) Evolution HAS been observed directly in lab and field.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p15Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: D
A, B, and C are each individually correct; D (All of the above) is the best overall answer. Understanding evolution informs biodiversity/extinction threats, antibiotic resistance and cancer, and the evolution of our own genome.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p15Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: E
None is true. The fossil record is always incomplete yet evolution is well supported by many lines of evidence. Evolution does not require increasing complexity; mutations are random but selection is not; life is a branched bush, not a ladder. (A) 'Missing links' is a misnomer; key transitional fossils (e.g., Tiktaalik) exist. (B) Complexity may increase OR decrease. (C) Selection and other non-random forces shape evolution (note convergence of whales and fishes). (D) Every organism has adaptations; humans are not the 'top' of any ladder.

(D) Wrong - natural selection favors variants that maximize transmission, not only those targeting the weakest.

Q030
p22Go the DistanceShort Answer (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key
Thewissen and colleagues used embryological evidence (dolphin embryos briefly develop hind-limb buds that are subsequently resorbed), molecular/developmental evidence (expression patterns of limb-patterning genes such as Sonic hedgehog [Shh], Hand2, and Tbx4), and paleontological evidence (fossil whales like Pakicetus, Ambulocetus, Rodhocetus, and Dorudon documenting progressive hind-limb reduction).
📷 Show original page
Q031
p22Go the DistanceShort Answer (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key
They integrated small-scale evidence (loss of Shh expression in dolphin hind-limb buds, correlating with truncated development) with large-scale evidence (fossil sequence showing gradual hind-limb loss across early cetaceans). By linking a specific regulatory/gene-expression change to the observed anatomical transition in the fossil record, they proposed a developmental-genetic mechanism for the evolutionary loss of hind limbs in whales.
📷 Show original page
Q032
p23Delve DeeperShort Answer
✓ Answer: Scientists can use observations of the present as indirect evidence of events that occurred in the past. For example, they can examine skeletons of living organisms and how those animals move in their environments and infer that ancient animals must have used structural elements similarly.
Scientists use present-day observations as indirect evidence for past events. By examining skeletons of living organisms and how they move in their environments, they infer that ancient animals with similar structures likely used them in similar ways. This comparative-functional reasoning, combined with the fossil record, lets scientists reconstruct the past.
📷 Show original page
Q033
p23Delve DeeperShort Answer
✓ Answer: No. Evolution does not produce a peaceful balance, nor does it strive to. The influenza virus evolves quickly, and with the right combination of mutation and reassortment, it can wipe out huge numbers of individuals. Even more benign strains attack the young, the elderly, and the weak. In addition, predators hunt and kill prey, and parasites devour their hosts from the inside out. Survival and reproduction are part of the natural process of evolution.
No. Evolution does not strive for balance. Influenza evolves rapidly; mutation and reassortment produce strains that kill many. Predators kill prey; parasites consume their hosts from within. Survival and reproduction are inherent to the evolutionary process.

Q041
p24Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: E
All of A-D are true. Reassortment combines strains; within-host evolution continues after infection; single mutations can dramatically change infectivity; and viruses can circulate in animal reservoirs for years before a mutation enables host jumping.
📷 Show original page
Q042
p24Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: A
Genetic drift is a random process that changes allele/gene frequencies in a population from generation to generation. It does not create variation (mutation does that) and it is a genuine evolutionary force, particularly in small populations.
📷 Show original page
Q043
p25ContemplateShort Answer (Reflection)
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key
Yes. Detrimental mutations can persist through: (1) genetic drift in small populations (harmful alleles may rise or fix by chance); (2) recessive inheritance (heterozygotes mask the harmful effect); (3) heterozygote advantage (e.g., sickle-cell allele persists because heterozygotes resist malaria); (4) linkage/hitchhiking with beneficial alleles; (5) pleiotropy (one allele is harmful for one trait but beneficial for another); (6) late-acting effects that manifest after reproductive age, so selection is weak; and (7) recurrent mutation input balancing selection (mutation-selection balance).
📷 Show original page
Q044
p25ContemplateShort Answer (Reflection)
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key
No. Embryos are not miniature adults. Early embryos of related vertebrates look strikingly similar, reflecting shared developmental programs inherited from common ancestors. They may show transient ancestral features (e.g., pharyngeal arches in mammalian embryos, hind-limb buds in cetacean embryos) that are later modified or lost. Modern developmental biology (evo-devo) rejects Haeckel's strict 'ontogeny recapitulates phylogeny'; instead, embryos follow species-specific trajectories that share deep homologies with relatives.
📷 Show original page

Ch 02 — From Natural Philosophy to Darwin

25 questions
Q001
p26Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
How would you define biological evolution?
  • A)A gradual process in which something changes into a different and usually more complex or better form
  • B)Any change in the frequency of heritable traits within a population from one generation to the next
  • C)A process of slow, progressive change
  • D)Any kind of change over time
  • E)A theory that humans have their origin in other types of animals, such as apes, and that the distinguishable differences between humans and apes are due to modifications in successive generations
✓ Answer: B
Chapter 1 introduces the concept of biological evolution and its effects on organisms as small as viruses and as big as blue whales. Why the other options are incorrect: A) Incorrect. Evolution does not have a purpose; it does not necessarily move from simple to complex or from worse to better forms. Misconceptions about what evolution is or how it acts are pretty common, so each chapter of the study guide includes an Overcoming Misconceptions section designed to help you understand evolutionary theory. C) Incorrect. Biological evolution is not necessarily slow, let alone progressive. Evolution can occur quite rapidly (within months in viruses and other short-lived organisms; see Section 1.2), and it does not have a purpose. D) Incorrect. Change over time does not necessarily reflect the heritable changes that are necessary for biological evolution. A whale can grow and develop, changing over time from a small, young calf to a large adult, but this change occurs within an individual's lifespan. Biological evolution is the change in the frequency of heritable traits within a population from one generation to the next. E) Incorrect. Biological evolution certainly can explain the historical relationship between humans and apes, but that is not its only implication. Biological evolution affects every living organism. In Dobzhansky's essay "Nothing in Biology Makes Sense Except in the Light of Evolution" (1973), he emphasized the important role evolution plays in understanding everything from fossils to the diversity of life.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p26Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
Why are mutations important in evolution?
  • A)Because mutations are always deleterious, and organisms with these deleterious mutations do not survive and reproduce
  • B)Because mutations only occur in viruses
  • C)Because mutations are random, and evolution is a random process
  • D)Because mutations create the variation among individuals on which other mechanisms of evolution can act
✓ Answer: D
Mutations affect the genetic sequences that generate an organism's phenotype. So, even when the effect on the phenotype may not be readily apparent to evolutionary biologists, mutations can lead to genetic variation among phenotypes—the raw material for evolution through both natural selection and genetic drift (see Chapter 1). Why the other options are incorrect: A) Incorrect. Mutations can often be deleterious, but they can also be beneficial, or even neutral. Mutations provide the variation among individuals on which the mechanisms of evolution can act. Beneficial mutations may lead to greater survival or reproduction through their effects on the characteristics, or phenotype, of an organism. B) Incorrect. Mutations can occur in any organisms during the process of DNA (or RNA) replication or when these molecules are damaged. Because viruses reproduce at such a rapid rate, lineages can evolve rapidly, and this evolution can be observed both in natural settings (e.g., with flu viruses) and in the lab. C) Incorrect. Mutations are random, but evolution is not necessarily a random process. In fact, natural selection is decidedly not random. Natural selection acts on mutations that affect the success or failure of the individual—those individuals with mutations that increase survival or reproduction will do better. This non-randomness is clear in adaptations that have converged on the same form, such as the streamlined shape of whales and fishes.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p26Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
What kinds of evidence have scientists used to study the evolution of whales?
  • A)Evidence from DNA
  • B)Evidence from fossils, such as their anatomical traits
  • C)Evidence from geology
  • D)Evidence from living species
  • E)All of the above
✓ Answer: E (All of the above)
Scientists have used DNA, fossils, geology, and living species to examine the evolution of whale lineages (see Chapter 1). This evidence for evolution is presented throughout Making Sense of Life. A) DNA from living mammals provided evidence that whales were related to artiodactyls. Scientists compared snippets of DNA to determine patterns and understand historical relationships. These patterns led to the hypothesis that whales were most closely related to hippos. B) Although Darwin proposed that whales descended from land mammals, very few fossil whales had been discovered to test that hypothesis. As more and more fossil whales were discovered, scientists tested Darwin's hypothesis about whale evolution. C) Geology provides important information about the origin and ages of different rocks. For example, Pakicetus fossils were found in rocks that formed from the sediments of shallow streams that flowed seasonally through hot, dry landscapes—not in rocks formed in ocean habitats. D) Evolutionary biologists often use evidence from living species to understand the past. Patterns of DNA from cows, goats, camels, and hippos strongly suggested that cetaceans were indeed related to artiodactyls—they shared a common ancestor.
📷 Show original page

Match the term "Adaptations" to its definition from the list below.
  • A)Groups of organisms that a taxonomist judges to be cohesive units, such as species or orders

  • C)Inherited aspects of an individual that allow it to outcompete other members of a population that lack the trait (or that have a slightly different version of the trait). They are traits that have evolved through the mechanism of natural selection
  • D)The study of prehistoric life
  • E)The permanent loss of a species. It is marked by the death or failure to breed of the last individual
✓ Answer: c
Adaptations are inherited aspects of an individual that allow it to outcompete other members of a population that lack the trait (or that have a slightly different version of the trait). They are traits that have evolved through the mechanism of natural selection.

Q015

Match the term "Uniformitarianism" to its definition from the list below.

  • B)An overarching set of mechanisms or principles that explain a major aspect of the natural world
  • C)Inherited aspects of an individual that allow it to outcompete other members of a population
  • D)The study of prehistoric life
  • E)The permanent loss of a species
✓ Answer: l
Uniformitarianism is the idea that the natural laws observable around us now are also responsible for events in the past. One part of this view, for example, is the idea that the Earth had been shaped by the cumulative action of gradual processes like sediment deposition and erosion.

Q016
p34Overcoming MisconceptionsMultiple Choice
Which of the following is a misconception about evolution?
  • A)The fossil record will never be a complete record of life on Earth.
  • B)Evolution is entirely random.
  • C)Evolutionary biologists are searching for the beginning of life.
  • D)Scientists have never observed evolution directly.
✓ Answer: B (Evolution is entirely random.)
Mutation is random, and genetic drift is random. But evolution through natural selection is not a random process. It occurs because mutations with beneficial effects spread through populations, while harmful mutations are removed. Complex adaptations evolve as beneficial mutations accumulate over time. People who oppose evolution often present it as an entirely random process to argue that structures like the eye or molecules like hemoglobin could not have arisen this way, but this misunderstands the non-random filtering action of natural selection.
📷 Show original page
Q017
p35Delve DeeperShort Answer
What is the difference between how scientists use the word "theory" and how it is used in everyday language?
✓ Answer: (Short answer)
Scientists use the word "theory" to describe an overarching set of principles that explain how the natural world works. The concept is very powerful and at the root of most scientific work. Scientists use theories to develop hypotheses and make predictions. Theories organize the way scientists think. In everyday language, the concept is much less powerful—the word generally refers to a guess or a hunch. Television crime dramas may use the word to summarize evidence that supports a crime—the theory of the crime—however, that use is still very different from its use in science.
📷 Show original page
Q018
p35Delve DeeperShort Answer (Discussion / Fill-in Table)
Consider the following discussion question and fill in the boxes. Should Carolus Linnaeus be considered the father of modern taxonomy? Fill in arguments for Yes and No, then state your Conclusion.
✓ Answer: (Discussion / Sample answer)
Yes (arguments in favor): 1. He developed a system of naming and classifying organisms. 2. He recognized that organisms could be assigned to a nested hierarchy based on their pattern of shared traits. 3. The hierarchical system he developed is still in use today. No (arguments against): 1. Many of the groupings he proposed are not used today. 2. He believed that the pattern of his system reflected a divine plan. 3. His assumption that the overall patterns of life's diversity had not changed since the biblical creation of the world was incorrect. Conclusion: Students should draw their own conclusion based on weighing these points. Linnaeus made foundational contributions (the nested hierarchical, binomial system still in use today) but operated under pre-evolutionary assumptions that have since been overturned.
📷 Show original page
Q019
p36Test YourselfMultiple Choice
In which order would humans be classified according to Linnaeus?
  • A)Hominidae
  • B)Homo
  • C)Animalia
  • D)Mammalia
  • E)Primates
✓ Answer: E (Primates)
Per the answer key (1. e). In the Linnaean hierarchy, humans belong to the order Primates. Hominidae (A) is a family, Homo (B) is a genus, Animalia (C) is a kingdom, and Mammalia (D) is a class.
📷 Show original page
Q020
p36Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Who first proposed that life could change over time?
  • A)Charles Darwin
  • B)Georges Buffon
  • C)Carl Linnaeus
  • D)Alfred Russel Wallace
✓ Answer: B (Georges Buffon)
Per the answer key (2. b). Georges-Louis Buffon proposed that new varieties of a species could arise in response to new habitats—an early proposal that living things could change over time, though he did not believe that species themselves could arise this way. Darwin and Wallace later articulated the mechanism of natural selection, but they were not the first to suggest that life could change over time.
📷 Show original page
Q021
p36Test YourselfMultiple Choice
What evidence did James Hutton use to reason that the Earth must be vastly old?
  • A)The slow transformation of landscapes caused by erosion
  • B)The rich diversity of fossil species
  • C)William Smith's maps of fossil strata
  • D)Volcanoes that radically transformed the Scottish landscape
  • E)The patterns of fossils of different ages found throughout England
✓ Answer: A (The slow transformation of landscapes caused by erosion)
Per the answer key (3. a). James Hutton envisioned a world with a deep history shaped by gradual transformations of landscapes through imperceptibly slow processes (uniformitarianism). By reasoning that the very slow rates of erosion and sediment deposition he observed would require immense spans of time to produce the landscapes visible today, Hutton concluded that Earth must be vastly old.
📷 Show original page
Q022
p36Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Why did Georges Cuvier reject Lamarck's idea that species were not fixed?
  • A)Because he and Lamarck were competitors at the National Museum of Natural History in Paris
  • B)Because Lamarck did not believe in extinction
  • C)Because his experience indicated that huge gaps existed among major groups of animal fossils with no intermediate fossils
  • D)Because his experience with fossils indicated that all species were created at the same time
  • E)Because he believed the Earth was not old enough for species to change
✓ Answer: C (Because his experience indicated that huge gaps existed among major groups of animal fossils with no intermediate fossils)
Per the answer key (4. c). Cuvier was a pioneer in comparative anatomy and paleontology. In his work with fossils, he observed that the major groups of animals appeared distinct, with no fossils showing transitions between them. From this pattern he argued that species could not gradually transform into other species (as Lamarck proposed)—because if they did, such intermediate forms should be plentiful in the fossil record.
📷 Show original page
Q023
p36Test YourselfMultiple Choice
How do you define a scientific theory?
  • A)A guess based on a few facts that scientists try to prove as correct
  • B)An educated guess based on some experience that allows scientists to test evidence
  • C)A set of laws that define the natural world
  • D)A testable set of mechanisms or principles that explain a major aspect of the natural world
  • E)A belief that scientists try to prove as fact
✓ Answer: D (A testable set of mechanisms or principles that explain a major aspect of the natural world)
Per the answer key (5. d). A scientific theory is an overarching set of testable mechanisms or principles that explain a major aspect of the natural world. Theories are far more powerful than guesses or hunches—they organize scientific thinking and generate testable hypotheses. This is distinct from the casual, everyday use of the word "theory" as a synonym for "guess."
📷 Show original page
Q024
p36Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Why did Thomas Malthus' arguments in "An Essay on the Principle of Population" inspire Darwin and Wallace's concept of natural selection?
  • A)Because Darwin and Wallace realized that poor people could not get enough food no matter how hard they tried
  • B)Because Darwin and Wallace believed that populations get what they need so even humans would not live up to their full reproductive potential
  • C)Because Darwin and Wallace needed some support for their ideas about human populations and social engineering
  • D)Because Darwin and Wallace realized that one generation of any organism can produce far more individuals then can actually survive and reproduce in the next
✓ Answer: D (Because Darwin and Wallace realized that one generation of any organism can produce far more individuals than can actually survive and reproduce in the next)
Per the answer key (6. d). Thomas Malthus proposed that the increase of the human population is necessarily limited by the means of subsistence. Darwin and Wallace applied this insight broadly: because organisms produce far more offspring than the environment can support, there must be a struggle for existence. In that struggle, individuals whose heritable traits make them better suited to their environment leave proportionately more offspring—this is natural selection.
📷 Show original page
Q025
p37Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Is natural selection the only mechanism that can lead to biological evolution?
  • A)Yes, because natural selection operates on the needs of organisms in their environment
  • B)Yes, because Charles Darwin invented natural selection as part of his theory of evolution
  • C)No, because evolution is entirely random
  • D)No, because evolution can occur through other mechanisms such as genetic drift
✓ Answer: D (No, because evolution can occur through other mechanisms such as genetic drift)
Per the answer key (7. d — note: the answer key lists "6. d" as the final entry, indicating question 7 follows the pattern; natural selection is not the only mechanism). Biological evolution can occur through multiple mechanisms. Natural selection is one mechanism, but genetic drift—random changes in the genetic composition of a population from one generation to the next—is another important evolutionary mechanism. Other mechanisms include mutation (the source of genetic variation) and gene flow (migration of alleles between populations).
📷 Show original page

Ch 03 — What the Rocks Say: How Geology and Paleontology Reveal the History of Life

38 questions
Q001
p38Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
Why is the study of stratigraphy important to understanding evolutionary theory?
  • A)Because stratigraphy provides evidence for the relative age of fossils
  • B)Because Nicolaus Steno recognized that rocks occurred in layers even though he didn't believe in evolution
  • C)Because stratigraphy characterizes the process of fossilization
  • D)All of the above
  • E)None of the above
✓ Answer: A
Stratigraphy is the study of layering in rock (stratification) as a method for reconstructing the past. It was first developed by Nicolaus Steno in the mid-1600s, before the development of evolutionary theory. Stratigraphy was instrumental in helping scientists examine change over time (see Chapter 2).
📷 Show original page
Q002
p38Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
What was Georges Cuvier's contribution to evolutionary theory?
  • A)He strongly objected to Jean-Baptiste Lamarck's ideas about life evolving from simple to complex, drumming Lamarck out of the scientific establishment.
  • B)He invented paleontology.
  • C)He recognized that some fossils were both similar to and distinct from living species, and many fossil animals no longer existed.
  • D)He was the first to organize and map strata according to a geological history.
  • E)He rejected the idea that species evolved, instead believing that life's history was a series of appearances and extinctions of species.
✓ Answer: C
Cuvier studied the fossil remains of elephants and compared them to living elephants. He discovered that some characters of the fossils, such as the shapes of teeth, were distinct from living elephants, and provided some of the first compelling evidence for extinction (see Chapter 2).
📷 Show original page
Q003
p38Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
What is a scientific theory?
  • A)A belief that scientists try to prove as fact
  • B)A set of laws that define the natural world
  • C)A set of mechanisms or principles that explain a major aspect of the natural world
  • D)A guess based on a few facts that scientists try to prove as correct
  • E)An educated guess based on some experience that allows scientists to test evidence
✓ Answer: C
A scientific theory is different from the word theory used in everyday language. Scientific theories are not just guesses; they are overarching sets of mechanisms or principles that explain and provide testable predictions. Darwin contributed the first overarching set of mechanisms for the theory of evolution as natural selection and sexual selection (see Section 2.3).
📷 Show original page
Q004
p40Identify Key TermsMatching
Match the term "Archaea" to its correct definition from the list below. ── DEFINITION BANK ── a) Molecular evidence of life in the fossil record. They can include fragments of DNA, molecules such as lipids, or specific isotopic ratios. b) Vertebrates with four limbs (or, like snakes, descended from vertebrates with four limbs). Living members include mammals, birds, reptiles, and amphibians. c) Layered structures formed by the mineralization of bacteria. d) Microorganisms lacking a cell nucleus or any other membrane-bound organelles. These microorganisms comprise two evolutionarily distinct groups, the bacteria and the archaea. e) One of the two prokaryotic domains of life. This domain includes organisms such as E. coli and other familiar microbes. f) The third domain of life, characterized by traits that include membrane-enclosed cell nuclei and mitochondria. This domain includes animals, plants, fungi, and protists (a general term for single-celled members). g) A Lagerstätte in Canada that preserved fossils from the Cambrian period. h) A lineage of tetrapods that emerged 300 million years ago and gave rise to mammals. The lineage can be distinguished from other tetrapods by the presence of a pair of openings in the skull behind the eyes, known as the temporal fenestrae. i) A technique that allows geologists to estimate the precise ages at which one geological formation ends and another begins. j) Extinct marine arthropods that diversified during the Cambrian period and gradually died out during the Devonian period. k) Members of a diverse phylum of animals that includes the vertebrates, lancelets, and tunicates. As embryos, chordates all have a notochord (a hollow nerve cord), pharyngeal gill slits, and a post-anal tail. Many present-day animals lose or modify these structures as they develop into adults. l) Sites with an abundant supply of unusually well-preserved fossils—often including soft tissues—from the same period of time. m) A group of animal species that existed during the period just before the Cambrian, between 575 and 535 million years ago. These animals included diverse species that looked like fronds, geometrical disks, and blobs covered with tire tracks. n) Flexible, rod-shaped structures found in the embryos of all chordates. These structures served as the first "backbones" in early chordates, and in extant vertebrates the embryonic structure becomes part of the vertebral column. o) A group including humans as well as all species more closely related to humans than to chimpanzees. Within this group, humans are the only surviving members. p) A lineage of bony fish that comprises most living species of aquatic vertebrates, including goldfish, salmon, and tuna. They can be distinguished from other fishes by unique traits, such as the mobility of an upper jawbone called the premaxilla. q) One of the two prokaryotic domains of life. Members of this domain superficially resemble bacteria, but they are distinguished by a number of unique biochemical features.
✓ Answer: q
Archaea: One of the two prokaryotic domains of life. Members of this domain superficially resemble bacteria, but they are distinguished by a number of unique biochemical features.

This domain includes organisms such as E. coli and other familiar microbes. f) The third domain of life, characterized by traits that include membrane-enclosed cell nuclei and mitochondria. This domain includes animals, plants, fungi, and protists (a general term for single-celled members). g) A Lagerstätte in Canada that preserved fossils from the Cambrian period. h) A lineage of tetrapods that emerged 300 million years ago and gave rise to mammals. The lineage can be distinguished from other tetrapods by the presence of a pair of openings in the skull behind the eyes, known as the temporal fenestrae. i) A technique that allows geologists to estimate the precise ages at which one geological formation ends and another begins. j) Extinct marine arthropods that diversified during the Cambrian period and gradually died out during the Devonian period. k) Members of a diverse phylum of animals that includes the vertebrates, lancelets, and tunicates. As embryos, chordates all have a notochord (a hollow nerve cord), pharyngeal gill slits, and a post-anal tail. Many present-day animals lose or modify these structures as they develop into adults. l) Sites with an abundant supply of unusually well-preserved fossils—often including soft tissues—from the same period of time. m) A group of animal species that existed during the period just before the Cambrian, between 575 and 535 million years ago. These animals included diverse species that looked like fronds, geometrical disks, and blobs covered with tire tracks. n) Flexible, rod-shaped structures found in the embryos of all chordates. These structures served as the first "backbones" in early chordates, and in extant vertebrates the embryonic structure becomes part of the vertebral column. o) A group including humans as well as all species more closely related to humans than to chimpanzees. Within this group, humans are the only surviving members. p) A lineage of bony fish that comprises most living species of aquatic vertebrates, including goldfish, salmon, and tuna. They can be distinguished from other fishes by unique traits, such as the mobility of an upper jawbone called the premaxilla. q) One of the two prokaryotic domains of life. Members of this domain superficially resemble bacteria, but they are distinguished by a number of unique biochemical features.
✓ Answer: o
Hominins: A group including humans as well as all species more closely related to humans than to chimpanzees. Within this group, humans are the only surviving members.

Q021
p46Interpret the DataShort Answer
Which species had the lowest ratio of ¹³C to ¹²C, and when did it live?
✓ Answer: Australopithecus anamensis. 4 million years ago.
Based on Figure 3.14 (Cerling et al.), Australopithecus anamensis, living approximately 4 million years ago, shows the lowest δ¹³C ratio among the hominin specimens plotted from the Turkana Basin.
📷 Show original page
Q022
p47Interpret the DataShort Answer
What is the range of δ¹³C values for the genus Homo (our genus)?
✓ Answer: Approximately −9‰ to −5‰.
Plotted specimens of Homo from 2.3, 1.67–1.99, and 1.4–1.67 million years ago cluster at the higher (less negative) end of the δ¹³C axis, ranging from about −9‰ to −5‰.
📷 Show original page
Q023
p47Interpret the DataShort Answer
If the δ¹³C values generally range between −24‰ and −32‰ for plants using the C₃ pathway, and between −10‰ and −14‰ for plants using the C₄ pathway, what can you surmise about the diets of species with low and high ratios?
✓ Answer: Species with low δ¹³C values likely ate more plants that used the C₃ pathway, and species with high δ¹³C values likely ate more plants that used the C₄ pathway.
Carbon isotopes from ingested plants are incorporated into tooth enamel. Because C₃ plants have more negative δ¹³C signatures than C₄ plants, an animal's tooth δ¹³C reflects its dietary proportions of these plant types.
📷 Show original page
Q024
p47Interpret the DataShort Answer
What can you determine about the diet of our genus?
✓ Answer: Our genus, Homo, likely ate more plants that used the C₄ pathway.
Because Homo specimens show relatively high (less negative) δ¹³C values (−9 to −5‰), consistent with the C₄ plant range, the diet of Homo appears to have shifted toward C₄ grasses and sedges compared to earlier hominins.
📷 Show original page
Q025
p50Overcoming MisconceptionsMultiple Choice
Which of the following is a true statement?
  • A)Scientists debate about the Earth's age.
  • B)Scientific tools that only give results as a range of numbers are not valuable tools.
  • C)Scientists use independent lines of evidence to validate results.
  • D)When aging rocks, scientists use isotopes that will give them the results they think are best.
✓ Answer: C
Scientists use independent lines of evidence to validate results. Radiometric dates are not only supported by volumes of consistent results from multiple isotope systems, they have been validated with independent lines of evidence such as tree rings and varved sediments.
📷 Show original page
Q026
p50Delve DeeperShort Answer
Why do scientists consider radiometric dating a valid way to measure the age of rocks?
✓ Answer: Radiometric dating has been tested and retested over a long time, and ages from radiometric dating have been consistently cross-checked against other dating methods with agreeing results.
Scientists often spend a lot of time critically examining other scientists' evidence, and radiometric dating has been used as a tool to estimate age for a very long time. They've tested and retested radiometric dating and compared ages derived from radiometric dating with other methods for dating and found very consistent results.
📷 Show original page
Q027
p50Delve DeeperShort Answer
The beginning of the Cambrian period, 542 million years ago, is often referred to as the Cambrian Explosion. The name is appropriate in some ways, and inappropriate in others. Why is this a fitting name for this time? Why isn't the term explosion a good descriptor?
✓ Answer: Fitting: so many major living groups—including the lineage that gave rise to vertebrates and humans—first appear in the fossil record during the Cambrian, seemingly all at once. Not fitting: the 'explosion' actually lasted about 13 million years, and new pre-Cambrian evidence (such as the Ediacaran fauna) shows the rise of new groups was less abrupt than once thought.
The Cambrian Explosion was often called that because of the first appearance of so many living groups, seemingly from nowhere. The "explosion" actually lasted millions and millions of years—about 13 million. The lineage that gave rise to vertebrates, including humans, first appeared in the fossil record during the Cambrian period. Also, as more evidence was collected about life in the pre-Cambrian, such as the Ediacaran fauna, the rise of new groups did not seem quite as explosive as previously thought.
📷 Show original page
Q028
p51Delve DeeperFill-in-the-blank
In the space provided, indicate whether you think each statement is a Hypothesis (H), a Prediction (P), or an Observation (O). a) C₄ plants have higher levels of carbon-13 than both living and extinct C₃ plants. b) The ratio of carbon isotopes in extant animals reflects the kinds of animals and plants that they eat. c) Fossil animals will show the same relationship between the ratios of carbon isotopes and diet as living animals. d) Hominin fossils in East Africa dating from 4.2 million years ago have relatively low ratio of carbon-13 to carbon-12. e) The relatively low ratio of carbon-13 to carbon-12 in 4.2 million-year-old hominin fossils indicates a diet rich in C₃ plants. f) The relatively low ratio of carbon-13 to carbon-12 in hominin fossils is similar to the ratio found today in the teeth of chimpanzees. g) Chimpanzees feed on fruits and leaves. h) Plant fossils from the same sites where the teeth of 4.2 million-year-old hominin fossils were discovered will contain C₃ plants, consistent with early hominins living in grassy woodlands where they easily could have found C₃ plants. i) 4.2 million-year-old hominin fossils were actively selecting C₃ plants for their diet.
✓ Answer: a) O
Observations are statements about facts that have been directly recorded or measured. Hypotheses are tentative explanations grounded in evidence that can be tested. Predictions are testable expectations that follow from a hypothesis. a) Observation — a directly measurable property of plant carbon isotopes. b) Observation or Hypothesis — the relationship has been directly observed in extant animals, and also functions as a working hypothesis to be tested. c) Prediction — an expectation that extends the observed living-animal relationship to fossil animals. d) Observation — a recorded measurement of fossil isotope ratios. e) Hypothesis — a proposed dietary explanation for the observed isotope pattern. f) Observation — a comparison between two measured ratios. g) Observation — a documented behavior of extant chimpanzees. h) Prediction — a testable expectation about what would be found at the fossil sites. i) Hypothesis — a proposed explanation for the pattern that assumes active selection of C₃ plants.
📷 Show original page
Q029
p51Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Why was Lord Kelvin's estimate for the age of the Earth inaccurate?
  • A)Because he failed to account for plate tectonics and how that might affect heat flow
  • B)Because he could not directly measure the age of any rock
  • C)Because he used a model to generate a prediction for the age of the Earth
  • D)Because he was trying to prove that evolution by natural selection could not occur
  • E)Because he could not estimate cooling of rocks found deeper than in mines
✓ Answer: A
Kelvin modeled Earth's cooling as a simple conductive process from an initially molten state. He did not know about radioactive decay (a continuous internal heat source) or plate tectonics (which convectively transports heat from the mantle to the surface). These unaccounted mechanisms caused his estimate of ~20–100 million years to be far too young.
📷 Show original page
Q030
p51Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Why can't scientists hope to find conventional fossils from very early in Earth's history?
  • A)Because plate tectonics have destroyed almost all of the planet's original surface
  • B)Because most fossils from that period are tiny zircons that do not have body forms
  • C)Because the process of fossilization has changed over the course of Earth's history
  • D)All of the above
  • E)None of the above
✓ Answer: A
Most of Earth's original crust has been recycled by plate tectonics — subducted, remelted, or metamorphosed — destroying any fossils it may have contained. This is why the earliest evidence of life comes from biomarkers and isotopic signatures, not conventional body fossils.
📷 Show original page
Q031
p51Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which of the following molecules has NOT been used as a biomarker, an organic chemical signature of once living organisms?
  • A)Carbon isotopes
  • B)Oxygen isotopes
  • C)Sodium ions
  • D)Cellular pigments
  • E)All of the above have been used as organic chemical signatures.
✓ Answer: C
Biomarkers are organic molecules or stable-isotope signatures left by once-living organisms. Carbon and oxygen isotope ratios, lipids, and cellular pigments (such as degradation products of chlorophyll) are all used. Sodium ions are simple inorganic ions with no unique biological origin, so they are not used as biomarkers.
📷 Show original page
Q032
p52Test YourselfMultiple Choice
How confident are scientists about the discoveries related to the earliest signs of life on Earth?
  • A)Very confident. Two scientists found organic carbon produced by photosynthetic bacteria, so now all scientists accept the 3.7-billion-year-old carbon as the earliest sign of life.
  • B)Fairly confident. Stromatolite formation is a process observable today, and scientists have discovered stromatolite fossils that are 3.45 billion years old.
  • C)Not very confident. Scientists all have different opinions about the oldest signs of life on Earth.
  • D)Not confident. Scientists constantly have to test new techniques, so they can't be sure until they find an identifiable fossil.
  • E)It's all just a guess.
✓ Answer: B
Stromatolites — layered structures produced by microbial mats — form by an observable modern process, giving scientists a clear analog. Fossil stromatolites dated to ~3.45 billion years ago are generally accepted as evidence of early microbial life. Older claims (e.g., 3.7-billion-year-old biogenic carbon) remain under debate.
📷 Show original page
Q033
p52Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which of the following statements about the history of life is TRUE?
  • A)Because cyanobacteria, the lineage of bacteria that carries out photosynthesis, have not changed in 2.6 billion years, evolution must not be occurring.
  • B)Scientists have not found any fossils from before the Cambrian period, 542 million years ago.
  • C)Because scientists have not been able to resolve many of the relationships among the three domains of life, they cannot know anything about the early history of life.
  • D)Living bacteria can offer clues about how the first multicellular animals evolved.
  • E)None of the above is a true statement.
✓ Answer: D
Living bacteria — including colony-forming and biofilm-producing species — can illuminate evolutionary transitions to multicellularity, since many of the genetic and behavioral building blocks for multicellular cooperation are present in modern prokaryotes. The other options are factually wrong (pre-Cambrian fossils exist, morphological stasis in cyanobacteria does not disprove evolution, and domain-level uncertainty does not erase what we know of early life).
📷 Show original page
Q034
p52Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which fossils often took the form of disks, fronds, or blobs?
  • A)Prokaryotes
  • B)Cambrian
  • C)Stromatolites
  • D)Ediacaran fauna
  • E)None of the above
✓ Answer: D
The Ediacaran fauna (~575–535 million years ago) are known for soft-bodied organisms preserved as impressions of fronds, geometric disks, and blob-like forms — many with no clear affinity to modern animal phyla.
📷 Show original page
Q035
p52Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Did all the different organisms classified as Ediacaran fauna appear in the fossil record at the same time?
  • A)No. The fossil record indicates that some organisms appeared as early as 575 million years ago, but other organisms did not appear until 20 million years later.
  • B)Yes. The fossil record indicates that most organisms in the Ediacaran fauna coexisted, and radiometric dating is not accurate enough to distinguish when different organisms appeared.
  • C)Maybe. The fossil record is incomplete, and not enough is known about the Avalon Assemblage to state that the Ediacaran fauna didn't appear at the same time.
  • D)Yes. Even though the fossil record indicates that some organisms existed for long periods, and many organisms existed for relatively short periods, scientists cannot know whether these organisms appeared at the same time or not.
  • E)No. Scientists are unable to determine the relationships of Ediacaran fauna because they are so diverse.
✓ Answer: A
Ediacaran organisms did not all appear simultaneously. The earliest (Avalon Assemblage) forms appear by ~575 million years ago, followed by progressively different assemblages (White Sea, Nama) over roughly 40 million years, with some taxa appearing 20 million years after the earliest forms.
📷 Show original page
Q036
p53Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which group does the following idea NOT apply to: "many of the most diverse animal and plant species alive today belong to relatively recent radiations"?
  • A)Flowering plants
  • B)Insects
  • C)Birds
  • D)Bacteria
  • E)Mammals
✓ Answer: D
Flowering plants, insects, birds, and mammals all underwent major adaptive radiations relatively recently (mostly within the last ~150 million years). Bacteria, by contrast, have been diversifying for ~3.5 billion years — their current diversity is the product of deep, not recent, evolutionary history.
📷 Show original page
Q037
p53ContemplateShort Answer
Why is understanding how organisms fossilize important to paleontology?
✓ Answer: Understanding fossilization reveals which organisms and tissues are likely (or unlikely) to be preserved, letting paleontologists correctly interpret the fossil record and account for its biases.
Fossilization is a rare and selective process — it favors organisms with hard parts, rapid burial, and low-oxygen conditions. Paleontologists who understand these filters can tell when an absence in the record reflects true absence versus preservation bias. This is why exceptional Lagerstätten (which preserve soft tissues) are so valuable: they partially correct for the biases of ordinary fossilization. (Note: this is a Contemplate reflection question; no answer was provided in the answer key.)
📷 Show original page
Q038
p53ContemplateShort Answer
How certain are evolutionary biologists about the age of the earliest life on Earth?
✓ Answer: Biologists are confident that microbial life was present by ~3.45 billion years ago (stromatolites), fairly confident about biomarker evidence going back to ~3.5 billion years, and less certain about still-older claims (e.g., 3.7-billion-year-old biogenic carbon), which remain actively debated.
Confidence scales with the independence and repeatability of evidence. Fossil stromatolites, cross-validated by modern analog processes and multiple isotopic dating methods, give a well-supported lower bound of ~3.45 Ga. Biomarker and isotopic signatures push life even earlier, but because abiotic processes can mimic some of these signatures, scientists remain appropriately cautious until independent lines of evidence converge. (Note: this is a Contemplate reflection question; no answer was provided in the answer key.)
📷 Show original page

Ch 04 — The Tree of Life: How Biologists Use Phylogeny to Reconstruct the Deep Past

30 questions
Q001
p54Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
How does homology relate to the theory of evolution?
  • A)Homology refers to traits that only superficially look alike, like bat wings and human arms; the theory of evolution cannot explain these similarities.
  • B)Homology refers to traits that look alike but have entirely different origins, like the fins of dolphins and of fish; the theory of evolution cannot explain the origins.
  • C)Homology refers to traits that are structurally similar in different organisms, like bat wings and human arms, because they each were inherited from a shared common ancestor with those traits; the theory of evolution provides a mechanism for those observations.
  • D)Homology refers to traits that have converged on a shared form; the theory of evolution provides a mechanism for those observations.
✓ Answer: C
Darwin, and many scientists before him, recognized homology as a basis for organization for comparative biology—that related organisms often had similar morphologies. The fact that many homologies are found together in the same groups of species strengthens support for Darwin's idea of descent with modification (see Chapter 2).
📷 Show original page
Q002
p54Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
How accurate is radiometric dating?
  • A)Not very accurate because scientists cannot know how much of a particular isotope was originally present in a rock, and thus, they cannot know how much of it has decayed
  • B)Very accurate because scientists can determine the exact age of rocks and the fossils found within them
  • C)Accurate because radiometric dating can determine estimates for the ages of rocks and fossils, often with relatively small margins of error
  • D)Not very accurate because scientists use probabilities to determine decay rates for each isotope they use in radiometric dating
✓ Answer: C
Radiometric dating provides estimates of age based on the half-life of the isotopes within the rocks. Different isotopes can be used to measure different time scales, and the time scale being addressed can affect the precision of the estimate. Isotopes with very long half-lives can be used to determine the ages of very old rocks with relative accuracy—say a 3 million year range for stromatolite fossils dating back 3.4 billion years. Whereas isotopes with shorter half-lives can provide more precise estimates for younger rocks—for example, a margin of error of 7000 years. Scientists can also use different elements to check and hone their estimates (see Chapter 3).
📷 Show original page
Q003
p54Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
Besides radiometric dating, what other lines of evidence can be used to determine the ages of fossils?
  • A)Stratigraphy
  • B)Homology
  • C)Complexity
  • D)a and b only
  • E)All of the above
✓ Answer: A
Stratigraphy can be used to determine the relative ages of fossils from strata that are widespread and can be correlated in time. With the advent of radiometric dating, more precise estimates of age can be applied to fossils (see Chapter 3). Homology can be used to identify fossils that share a common ancestor, but it cannot be used to determine the ages of fossils. Complexity of fossils is not appropriate to determine the age of fossils—evolution has not been a "march of progress," and the development of complexity is not necessarily related to age.

Draw a Venn diagram that indicates the nested relationship among the stick insects. (Based on the stick insect cladogram and the data matrix of character states: Vomer Present, Thorn Pads on Male T10, Abdominal Sterna, Loss of Tergal Thorn Pads, Cerci Unsegmented, Fore Femora Curved, Cerci Dorsoventrally Flat, across Timematodea, Euphasmatodeans, Nanophyllium, Microphyllium, Chitoniscus, and Phyllium.)

Q011
p63Games and ExercisesShort Answer (Diagram)
Now consider some fossil evidence. Timematodea and Archipseudophasmatidae were both found in amber that dates from approximately 40 million years ago. Male Archipseudophasmatidae have thorn pads on their 10th terga, and the fore femora are curved—they do not have dorsoventrally flattened cerci or abdominal sterna. Eophyllium has abdominal sterna, but it lacks dorsoventrally flattened cerci (Wedman et al. 2007). Eophyllium was found in deposits that date to 47 million years ago. Where would you place these two new genera given your current understanding? Draw a new Venn diagram.
✓ Answer: Archipseudophasmatidae falls within the Euphasmatodea (thorn pads on male T10, curved fore femora, but lacking abdominal sterna and dorsoventrally flattened cerci). Eophyllium falls within the set that has abdominal sterna (Phylliinae) but outside the set with dorsoventrally flattened cerci.
Eophyllium has abdominal sterna, so it is considered a member of the Phylliinae, leaf insects that exhibit an extreme form of morphological and behavioral leaf mimicry. Since it doesn't have dorsoventrally flattened cerci though, Eophyllium must have arisen before the Nanophyllium, Microphyllium, Chitoniscus, and Phyllium clade split off. Archipseudophasmatidae, however, must be a member of the Euphasmatodea because it lacks abdominal sterna.

Then abdominal sterna defines the Phylliinae clade, with Eophyllium splitting off first (before the cerci dorsoventrally flattened clade: Nanophyllium, Microphyllium, Chitoniscus, Phyllium). Complete loss of tergal thorn pads characterizes the most derived group.
The answer key provides a cladogram with Timematodea as the outgroup, Archipseudophasmatidae as a fossil branch within the Euphasmatodea (having thorn pads on male tergum 10 and fore femora basally curved but lacking abdominal sterna), and Eophyllium as a fossil branch within the Phylliinae (having abdominal sterna but lacking dorsoventrally flattened cerci). Nanophyllium, Microphyllium, Chitoniscus, and Phyllium form the extant leaf-mimic clade with dorsoventrally flattened cerci and complete loss of tergal thorn pads.

Did the addition of Eophyllium clarify the resolution of the Euphasmatodea?
✓ Answer: No.
No. Eophyllium has abdominal sterna, so it is considered a member of the Phylliinae, leaf insects that exhibit an extreme form of morphological and behavioral leaf mimicry. Since it doesn't have dorsoventrally flattened cerci though, Eophyllium must have arisen before the Nanophyllium, Microphyllium, Chitoniscus, and Phyllium clade split off. Archipseudophasmatidae, however, must be a member of the Euphasmatodea because it lacks abdominal sterna. However, this extinct group provides little resolution for the extant Euphasmatodea.

Q015
p65Games and Exercises (Carnivorans Revisited)Short Answer
Additional Character #13: Presence (0) or Absence (1) of Lower Premolar 1. Walrus = 0, sea lion = 0, raccoon = 0. Map the character onto the phylogeny developed from the previous 12 characters. Does this additional character help resolve the phylogeny? Why or why not?
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key.
This character does not strongly help resolve the phylogeny because the absence of the lower premolar 1 is scored the same (0, present) for walrus, sea lion, and raccoon—species that are not sister taxa within the tree. If the character state is shared by non-sister taxa, it would represent either an ancestral retention or homoplasy (convergent loss in some lineages), so it does not cleanly identify a synapomorphy for a single clade.

Q016
p65Games and Exercises (Carnivorans Revisited)Short Answer
Is the presence of the lower premolar 1 a homologous trait in the carnivorans?
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key.
The presence of the lower premolar 1 is an ancestral trait inherited from a common ancestor of all carnivorans (i.e., it is homologous across the group). However, its absence in some lineages (walrus, sea lion, raccoon) arose independently through evolutionary loss—the losses themselves are not homologous if they occurred independently in separate lineages (this would be homoplasy rather than a shared derived state).
📷 Show original page
Q017
p65Games and Exercises (Tree Challenge: Shoes)Short Answer
Develop a phylogenetic tree that illustrates the relationships among your shoes. Grab one shoe from five different pairs, identify five different characters (e.g., presence/absence of shoe laces, sole material, type of shoe, toe shape), build a data matrix, and construct a tree. People are notorious for borrowing other people's ideas—so the observed "homology" may not really result from "descent"—it might result because one lineage (i.e., one fashion designer) took an idea from another lineage, something akin to horizontal gene transfer. Do you think "borrowing" might influence the relationships in the tree?
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key.
Yes, "borrowing" of features across fashion lineages (analogous to horizontal gene transfer) would introduce shared character states that are not due to common descent. These would appear as homoplasies on a cladistic analysis based purely on shared features, potentially creating false sister-group relationships. This illustrates why horizontal gene transfer complicates phylogenetic reconstruction in organisms like bacteria.

Q018
p70Overcoming MisconceptionsMultiple Choice
Which of the following is a true statement?
  • A)The fewer the nodes between species, the more related to each other they are.
  • B)Straight lines in phylogenies indicate species that have not evolved.
  • C)Lineages that branch off later are more advanced than lineages that branch off earlier.
  • D)Scientists are not looking for missing links.
✓ Answer: D
Scientists are not looking for missing links. Paleontologists do not search for missing links in the sense of a direct line of evolutionary history. Instead, they look for fossils that can help resolve uncertainties in phylogenies and that can help resolve the history by which characters evolved in major clades.
📷 Show original page
Q019
p70Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer
Bradley Livezey and Richard Zusi developed a phylogeny of modern birds to help resolve some of the conflicting evidence for how birds are related to each other—a heated topic among birders around the world. Why did Livezey and Zusi argue that avian systematics needed to be examined? Reference: Livezey, B. C., and R. L. Zusi. 2007. Higher-Order Phylogeny of Modern Birds (Theropoda, Aves: Neornithes) Based on Comparative Anatomy. II. Analysis and Discussion. Zoological Journal of the Linnean Society 149 (1): 1–95.
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key.
Livezey and Zusi argued that avian systematics needed re-examination because conflicting lines of evidence (from morphology versus molecular data) had produced inconsistent phylogenies of modern birds. Many higher-order relationships were unresolved, and prior classifications relied on limited character sets. Their goal was a comprehensive comparative-anatomy analysis that could serve as a rigorous morphological framework against which molecular hypotheses could be tested.
📷 Show original page
Q020
p70Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer
Scientists use "outgroups" to determine what characters are "primitive" in the analysis; the organisms being classified are considered the "ingroup." Why did Livezey and Zusi use the Theropoda as an outgroup?
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key.
Theropoda is the sister clade of modern birds (Neornithes) — modern birds are nested within Theropoda — so non-avian theropods are the closest available outgroup for distinguishing ancestral (primitive) character states from derived character states within the ingroup of modern birds. Using non-avian theropods as an outgroup allowed Livezey and Zusi to polarize character states and identify synapomorphies defining modern bird clades.
📷 Show original page
Q021
p71Delve DeeperShort Answer
Why are phylogenies based on shared derived characters? What does including an outgroup do for understanding those relationships?
✓ Answer: Shared derived characters (synapomorphies) evolved in an immediate common ancestor, so they should be present in all descendants and reveal clade membership. Outgroups help identify which character states are shared derived versus ancestral.
Shared derived characters, or synapomorphies, represent characters that evolved in the immediate common ancestor, so they should be present in all the descendants. Phylogenies are constructed from shared characters, and classification depends on the derived nature of those character states. Outgroups serve to help identify shared derived characters versus ancestral characters.
📷 Show original page
Q022
p71Delve DeeperShort Answer
Do you agree with how time is portrayed in this phylogeny? Why or why not? (Phylogeny of Birds, Crocodiles, Lizards, Turtles, and Mammals with arrows indicating time running left to right, from "oldest" to "youngest".)
✓ Answer: No.
No. The arrows incorrectly indicate that time is going from left to right, rather than from bottom to top. On this tree, the oldest nodes are at the bottom, so the branching event occurred earlier at those nodes than nodes further up the tree. So the lizard lineage branched off earlier than the bird lineage, but that doesn't mean that within these taxonomic groupings, all lizards are older than all birds.
📷 Show original page
Q023
p71Delve DeeperShort Answer
Scientists have been examining the correlation between brain size and basal metabolic rate. Some scientists have suggested that basal metabolic rate influences brain size because the brain tissue is metabolically active and costly to run. Maternal investment, either through gestation or lactation, can influence the transfer of metabolic energy during growth and development of offspring. Therians provide a unique opportunity to test hypotheses about basal metabolic rate because eutherians invest in gestation (by providing a placenta to feed developing offspring), and marsupials invest in lactation. How might tests of these hypotheses be influenced by phylogenetic relationships? What might be the effect on the relationship between basal metabolic rate and brain size if one group, the primates (eutherians), have inordinately large brain sizes? Read Weisbecker, V. and A. Goswami. 2012. Brain size, life history, and metabolism at the marsupial/placental dichotomy. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. What did the authors find?
✓ Answer: Phylogenetic relationships can make data points non-independent; primates (eutherians with unusually large brains relative to BMR) can disproportionately influence statistical comparisons and mislead the interpretation of marsupial vs. eutherian brain sizes.
Small brain size may be an ancestral trait common to both eutherians and marsupials. Therefore, data points within the phylogenetic contrasts might not be independent—the data might be influenced by these historical relationships. The effect of one group with exceptionally large brain sizes may influence the results even more. Because primates are a diverse group of eutherians and they have exceptionally large brains relative to their basal metabolic rates, they may influence the statistical outcome of the comparison of brain size among eutherians relative to marsupials. Indeed, the specific relationship between brain size and basal metabolic rate found in primates may have evolved only in that lineage, and without independent contrasts could lead unsuspecting researchers to believe that marsupials have smaller brains than eutherians for any given basal metabolic rate.
📷 Show original page
Q024
p72Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which of the following is NOT a monophyletic group?
  • A)A clade
  • B)Reptilia
  • C)Aves
  • D)Amphibia
  • E)All of the above are monophyletic groups.
✓ Answer: B
Reptilia, as traditionally defined (turtles, lizards, snakes, crocodiles), is a paraphyletic group because it excludes birds (Aves), which are descended from the common ancestor of all reptiles. A monophyletic group must include the common ancestor and ALL of its descendants. A clade, Aves, and Amphibia are all monophyletic.

Q025
p72Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Based on your understanding of Carl Linnaeus' classification system, how would you treat his conclusions?
  • A)His concept of nested hierarchies accurately characterized and organized species relationships, so his conclusions are valid.
  • B)His concept of nested hierarchies doesn't reflect modern understanding of species relationships, so Linnaean classification has no place in modern science.
  • C)His classification scheme is based on evidence that has been refuted with modern techniques and should be ignored.
  • D)His classification scheme provides a useful convention for naming species and thinking about species relationships, so his conclusions could be used as a starting point for examining taxonomic relationships.
  • E)His classification scheme provides a useful convention for naming species, so the taxonomic units he named should be maintained.
✓ Answer: D
Linnaeus' classification scheme provides a useful convention for naming species and thinking about species relationships, so his conclusions could be used as a starting point for examining taxonomic relationships. Compared with the tools of scientists today, Linnaeus built his taxonomic system on a relatively basic understanding of morphology, but evolutionary theory has added new explanations for these relationships and new insight to the meaning of taxonomic groupings.

Q026
p73Test YourselfMultiple Choice
In the past, morphological evidence alone was used to examine the relationships of the cetaceans to other groups. According to this phylogeny (showing Balaenoptera, Tursiops, Ambulocetus, Protocetus, Pakicetus, Elomeryx, Archaeotherium, Hippopotamus, and Equus):
  • A)The extinct genus Ambulocetus is more distantly related to the genus Balaenoptera than to the extinct genus Protocetus.
  • B)The genus Equus (horses) is more closely related to the genus Hippopotamus (hippos) than to the extinct genus Pakicetus.
  • C)More changes have occurred in the whale lineage than in the horse lineage.
  • D)Horses (genus Equus) were the ancestor of whales and hippos.
  • E)None of the above are correct statements.
✓ Answer: E
None of the statements are correct. Ambulocetus shares a more recent common ancestor with Balaenoptera (within the whale clade) than with non-whale genera, so option A is wrong. Equus is more distantly related to Hippopotamus than Hippopotamus is to Pakicetus (whales and hippos share a recent common ancestor), so B is wrong. Branch length on a cladogram does not directly represent the amount of morphological change, so C cannot be concluded, and horses are not ancestral to whales or hippos—they share a common ancestor but lie on a separate lineage (D wrong).

Q027
p73Test YourselfMultiple Choice
How does including fossils in phylogenies of extant taxa affect the conclusions scientists can draw?
  • A)Including fossils can change the hypothesis generated by the phylogeny.
  • B)Including fossils can define the timing of branching events.
  • C)Including fossils can affect understanding of common ancestors.
  • D)Including fossils can generate new questions about clades.
  • E)All of the above
✓ Answer: E
All of the above. Fossils can revise the hypothesis of a phylogeny, calibrate the timing of branching events (molecular clock calibration), clarify the character states of inferred common ancestors, and raise new questions about clade relationships and the tempo of character evolution.

p73ContemplateShort Answer
Can technology, such as the evolution of video games, be examined using [a phylogenetic analysis]?
✓ Answer: Answer not provided in answer key (open-ended reflection question).
Yes. Technology like video games can be analyzed with cladistic methods by treating design features (game mechanics, genres, engines, control schemes, graphical styles) as "characters." Shared features across games may reflect either inheritance from common predecessors (e.g., engines forked from earlier engines) or convergent design choices. As with fashion, horizontal "idea transfer" between developers complicates strictly tree-like relationships.
