Ch 05 — Raw Material: Heritable Variation Among Individuals

30 questions
Q001
p82Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
Who incorporated the idea that the theory of evolution required the capacity for one generation to pass on its traits to the next?
  • A)Charles Darwin
  • B)Jean-Baptiste Lamarck
  • C)Alfred Russel Wallace
  • D)All of the above
  • E)None of the above
✓ Answer: D) All of the above
Each of these naturalists incorporated some capacity for one generation to pass on traits to another (see Chapter 2), although none of them understood the mechanism by which that transference took place. Darwin conducted exhaustive studies indicating the capacity for traits to be passed on but had no explanation for how. Lamarck incorporated heritability into his ideas but believed traits could be acquired during an individual's lifetime (later rejected). Wallace developed a mechanism for evolution very similar to Darwin's natural selection, which required traits to be heritable.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p82Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
Why is heritable variation among individuals an important factor for natural selection?
  • A)Because variation has to be heritable for a species to survive
  • B)Because variation has to be heritable for individuals to pass down their beneficial mutations
  • C)Because when individuals respond to the environment, they can pass the traits they acquired on to offspring
  • D)Because natural selection cannot act when all individuals are absolutely identical
  • E)Both b and d
✓ Answer: E) Both b and d
Individuals of a species or group must vary in some characteristic (e.g., via beneficial mutations), and some of those variants must be advantageous. Natural selection acts to alter the abundances of those variants in the population based on the relative reproductive success of individuals possessing them. So, heritable variation is integral to natural selection for most organisms because it is an effective mechanism by which beneficial mutations may be transmitted. Option A is incorrect because variation does not have to be heritable for a species to continue existing (e.g., horizontal gene transfer). Option C reflects Lamarck's rejected idea of inheritance of acquired characteristics.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p82Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
How can the phylogeny on the left (showing family relationships among you, your sister, and your cousins) be used to understand the phylogeny on the right (showing relationships among humans, frogs, goldfish, and trout)?
  • A)The phylogeny on the left shows that you and your sister are more closely related to each other than you are to your cousins, just as humans and frogs are more closely related to each other than they are to goldfish or trout.
  • B)The phylogeny on the left shows that the relationship between humans and frogs cannot be compared with the relationship between siblings.
  • C)The phylogeny on the left shows that your cousins must be more closely related to each other than you and your sister because trout and goldfish are more closely related to each other than frogs and humans.
  • D)The phylogeny on the right shows that humans are more closely related to goldfish than to trout.
✓ Answer: A
Although the phylogeny on the left shows the relationships among individual family members and not groups of organisms, the idea of common ancestry is essentially the same. Humans are more closely related to frogs than they are to fish because they share a more recent common ancestor with frogs (P) — just like you and your sister share a more recent common ancestor (P) than you and your cousins (G). Humans share a more distant common ancestor with fish (G). The branches on a phylogeny can swing freely (like a mobile), so flipping positions does not change relatedness.
📷 Show original page
Q004
p82Games and Exercises (HBB / Sickle-cell Mutation)Short Answer
Given: Strand 1: CACGTGGACTGAGGACTC Strand 2: CACGTGGACTGAGGACAC What's the difference between the two strands?
✓ Answer: The last thymine is changed to an adenine (so the final codon changes from CTC to CAC).
When transcribed into mRNA: - Strand 1 mRNA: GUGCACCUGACUCCUGAG → Val His Leu Thr Pro Glu - Strand 2 mRNA: GUGCACCUGACUCCUGUG → Val His Leu Thr Pro Val The change at the DNA level is a single nucleotide substitution (T → A) in the last codon.
📷 Show original page
Q005
p82Games and Exercises (HBB / Sickle-cell Mutation)Short Answer
What's the difference between the amino acid sequences that result from Strand 1 (CACGTGGACTGAGGACTC) and Strand 2 (CACGTGGACTGAGGACAC)?
✓ Answer: The last amino acid is different — the sequence now transcribes Val (valine) instead of Glu (glutamic acid).
HBB is the gene associated with sickle-cell anemia. The hemoglobin variant Hb S (which causes sickle-cell disease) differs from normal hemoglobin (Hb A) in just one amino acid: in normal hemoglobin this residue is glutamic acid, but in sickle-cell hemoglobin it is valine. This single amino-acid change is caused by a single nucleotide substitution in the DNA.
📷 Show original page
Q006
p82Games and Exercises (HBB / Sickle-cell Mutation)Short Answer
If the mutation in Strand 2 was at the third base in that codon instead of the second, would the hemoglobin protein form normally?
✓ Answer: It depends on the specific mutation.
- If the third base in the DNA codon changed from cytosine to adenine (or to guanine), the DNA sequence would read CTA (or CTG), the mRNA codon would read GAU (or GAC), which codes for Asp (aspartic acid) — a different amino acid, so the protein would be altered. - If the third base changed from cytosine to thymine (making the DNA codon CTT), the mRNA codon would still code for Glu (glutamic acid) due to redundancy in the genetic code (a synonymous/silent mutation), and the protein would remain the same. This illustrates the degeneracy of the genetic code: changes at the third (wobble) position often have no effect on the resulting amino acid.
📷 Show original page
Q007
p82Interpret the Data (Twin Heights)Short Answer
Can you predict the height of Twin 2 when twins are identical (monozygotic)? For example, what would you expect the height of Twin 2 to be if the height of Twin 1 is 160 cm?
✓ Answer: Yes. You can predict the height of Twin 2 with some certainty when twins are identical. If Twin 1 is 160 cm, Twin 2 is likely to be about 160 cm.
In the graph (part A), the height of Twin 2 ranged from approximately 152 to 165 cm when Twin 1 was exactly 160 cm. Because monozygotic twins share identical genes, their heights show very strong correlation.
📷 Show original page
Q008
p82Interpret the Data (Twin Heights)Short Answer
Is there variation in the heights of identical twins?
✓ Answer: Yes. Heights of identical twins vary — their heights do not match up exactly.
Even though the twins share identical genes, other factors, such as subtle differences in the environment within which the individuals develop, affect the height of each individual.
📷 Show original page
Q009
p82Interpret the Data (Twin Heights)Short Answer
Why don't the heights of identical twins match exactly?
✓ Answer: Identical twins inherit identical sets of genes, but environmental factors can affect gene expression differently in different individuals.
While identical twins have a strong tendency to grow to the same height due to shared genetics, their heights still include some variation because the environment (nutrition, prenatal conditions, differential gene expression, etc.) influences phenotype.
📷 Show original page
Q010
p82Interpret the Data (Twin Heights)Short Answer
Do you see any correlation in height between fraternal (dizygotic) twins?
✓ Answer: Yes, there is some correlation, though not as strong as for identical twins.
Although predicting the heights of fraternal twins is not as straightforward as predicting the heights of identical twins, the heights of fraternal twins could still be correlated because the individuals are related (share about 50% of their segregating genetic variation on average) and they likely experienced generally similar environments as they developed.
📷 Show original page
Q011
p82Interpret the Data (Twin Heights)Short Answer
What might be contributing to the variation between fraternal twins?
✓ Answer: Fraternal twins develop from separate eggs; the alleles they carry will differ because of genetic recombination and independent assortment.
As a result of these two random processes during meiosis, fraternal twins inherit different combinations of maternal and paternal alleles. This genetic difference (combined with environmental variation) means fraternal twins are less likely to grow to the same height than identical twins.
📷 Show original page
Q012
p86Overcoming MisconceptionsMultiple Choice
Which of the following is a true statement?
  • A)Scientists expect to find a gene that codes for skin color.
  • B)Decoding the genome is a straightforward process.
  • C)Because offspring receive one chromosome from each parent, any given locus can only have two alleles in a population.
  • D)A low concentration of transcription factors can influence the expression of a gene.
✓ Answer: D) A low concentration of transcription factors can influence the expression of a gene.
Phenotypes are influenced by more than just genotypes. Internal environmental factors — including transcription factors, hormones, nutrients, toxins, and temperature — all affect whether or not a gene is expressed. Even low concentrations of transcription factors can switch genes on or off. The other options reflect common misconceptions: (A) most traits (including skin color) are polygenic, not single-gene; (B) decoding the genome is complex because of introns, exons, splicing, and regulation; (C) while an individual diploid carries only two alleles at a locus, a population can have many different alleles at the same locus.
📷 Show original page
Q013
p86Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer
In the study by Allen et al. (2010) examining genetic variation in 183,727 people and finding at least 180 loci influencing adult height (explaining only 10% of variation): Why do the authors argue their approach is so valuable?
✓ Answer: The approach is valuable because it demonstrates the genetic architecture of highly polygenic, heritable traits — showing that very large numbers of loci, each with small individual effects, jointly contribute to phenotypic variation.
Even though the 180 loci identified explained only about 10% of the variation in adult height, the methodology (genome-wide association analysis at large sample sizes) provides a framework for dissecting complex quantitative traits. It identifies specific genomic regions and biological pathways involved, gives insight into how many loci underlie a trait, and shows the limits of current approaches — highlighting missing heritability and pointing toward rare variants, gene–gene, and gene–environment interactions as remaining contributors. Reference: Allen, H. L., et al. 2010. Nature 467: 832–38.
📷 Show original page
Q014
p86Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer
How might the approach used by Allen et al. (2010) — a genome-wide association study of a highly polygenic, heritable trait — help our understanding of polygenic diseases?
✓ Answer: The same genome-wide association methodology can identify the many small-effect loci and biological pathways that contribute to polygenic diseases, allowing researchers to pinpoint molecular mechanisms, build polygenic risk scores, and prioritize therapeutic targets.
Most common diseases (e.g., diabetes, heart disease, schizophrenia, some cancers) are polygenic, meaning many loci each contribute small effects. Allen et al.'s approach shows that with very large samples, hundreds of loci clustered in biological pathways can be identified. Applied to disease traits, this helps reveal the underlying genetic architecture, improves risk prediction, and highlights shared pathways that may be targets for treatment. It also flags "missing heritability" as a key problem to solve with rare-variant and interaction-focused methods.
📷 Show original page
Q015
p87Delve DeeperShort Answer
Why aren't the mutations that occur in skin cells or in other organs, such as the heart or brain, heritable?
✓ Answer: Because the cells dividing in the skin, heart, and brain are not involved in sexual reproduction. All these cells die when the individual dies. A mutation that occurs in these cells can't be passed down to offspring.
Only eggs and sperm (germ-line cells) transmit DNA to the next generation, so only mutations that occur in these germ cells are heritable. Mutations in somatic cells (skin, heart, brain) can affect the individual (e.g., cause cancer) but do not enter the gamete lineage and therefore cannot be inherited by offspring.
📷 Show original page
Q016
p87Delve DeeperShort Answer
Why don't all phenotypic traits occur as discrete, alternative states like Mendel's peas?
✓ Answer: Phenotypic traits are rarely determined by single Mendelian loci. Variation in many traits results from the cumulative action of many genes (polygenic inheritance), and the environment can also influence phenotypes.
Besides polygenic effects, variation in the environment can lead to variation in the phenotypes that arise from a single genotype. Complex interactions between many different genes and the environment (gene × gene and gene × environment interactions) produce continuous (quantitative) variation rather than discrete alternative states.
📷 Show original page
Q017
p87Delve DeeperShort Answer
What sources of genotypic variation among individuals are random?
✓ Answer: Mutations, independent assortment, and genetic recombination are the three main random sources of genotypic variation.
- Mutations are random with respect to fitness — their effects depend on where and when they occur in the genome. They are the ultimate source of all genetic variation. - Independent assortment is random: pairs of chromosomes separate during meiosis and segregate randomly, so gametes contain any combination of maternal and paternal chromosomes. - Genetic recombination (crossing over) is a random event each time a germ cell divides, producing gametes with novel allele combinations. Note: Evolution itself is not entirely random — natural selection is decidedly NOT random; it acts on these random sources of variation, favoring variants that increase survival or reproduction.
📷 Show original page
Q018
p87Test YourselfMultiple Choice
What is alternative splicing?
  • A)The process of splicing different subsets of exons from the same gene to produce different combinations and therefore different proteins
  • B)The process of alternating introns and exons within the mRNA transcript
  • C)The process of creating messenger RNA from various genes on the DNA template
  • D)The process of switching a variety of genes on or off at the same time that results from transcription factors that can bind to identical regulatory regions near hundreds of different genes
  • E)None of the above
✓ Answer: A) The process of splicing different subsets of exons from the same gene to produce different combinations and therefore different proteins
Alternative splicing allows a single gene to produce multiple distinct mRNA transcripts and therefore multiple protein isoforms. After transcription, different combinations of exons can be retained or excluded, dramatically expanding the functional diversity encoded by a genome. This is one reason that gene number alone is a poor predictor of organismal complexity.
📷 Show original page
Q019
p87Test YourselfMultiple Choice
When an organism incorporates genetic material from another organism that is not its parent, this is known as:
  • A)Vertical gene transfer
  • B)Diagonal gene transfer
  • C)Horizontal gene transfer
  • D)None of the above
✓ Answer: C) Horizontal gene transfer
Horizontal gene transfer (HGT) occurs when genetic material moves between organisms that are not in a parent-offspring relationship. Examples include bacteria exchanging plasmids via conjugation, transformation, or transduction. Vertical gene transfer, by contrast, is transmission from parent to offspring. HGT is an important evolutionary mechanism, especially in prokaryotes.
📷 Show original page
Q020
p88Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Can a mutation that occurs within a cell during mitosis in a diploid cell be passed on to offspring?
  • A)No. Only somatic cells undergo mitosis, so the mutation cannot be passed on to offspring.
  • B)No. Mutations cannot occur during mitosis.
  • C)Maybe. It depends on whether the cell is part of the germ line or not.
  • D)Yes. Mitosis is the process of cell division, so mutations will be passed on to offspring because as cells divide they will reproduce the mutation and pass it on.
  • E)Yes. Because all mutations, beneficial and deleterious, are passed on to offspring.
✓ Answer: C) Maybe. It depends on whether the cell is part of the germ line or not.
Germ-line cells (which give rise to eggs and sperm) divide by mitosis before entering meiosis. A mutation that occurs during mitosis in a germ-line cell can be passed on to offspring via gametes. However, if the mitotic mutation occurs in a somatic cell (e.g., skin, liver), it cannot be transmitted to offspring because somatic cells do not contribute DNA to the next generation. Option A is wrong because germ-line stem cells also undergo mitosis.
📷 Show original page
Q021
p88Test YourselfMultiple Choice
What is an allele?
  • A)One of several alternative forms of the DNA sequence of the same locus
  • B)One of several alternative forms of a gene that occur at the same place on paired chromosomes
  • C)One of several alternative forms of a gene that occur at the same locus in different individuals
  • D)All of the above
  • E)None of the above
✓ Answer: D) All of the above
An allele is one of several alternative forms of the DNA sequence at a given locus. All three descriptions (A, B, and C) capture complementary aspects of the same concept: alleles are alternative DNA-sequence variants at a locus; in diploid individuals they occupy the same position on paired homologous chromosomes; and across individuals in a population, many different alleles of the same locus may exist.
📷 Show original page
Q022
p88Test YourselfMultiple Choice
What is/are the most important factor/s generating genetic diversity among individuals in a population of eukaryotes?
  • A)Mutation
  • B)Independent assortment
  • C)Genetic recombination
  • D)b and c only
  • E)All of the above are important for generating genetic diversity in eukaryotes.
✓ Answer: E) All of the above are important for generating genetic diversity in eukaryotes.
Three processes drive genetic diversity in sexually reproducing eukaryotes: 1. Mutation - the ultimate source of new alleles. 2. Independent assortment - random segregation of homologous chromosome pairs during meiosis I. 3. Genetic recombination (crossing over) - exchange of segments between homologous chromosomes, producing novel allele combinations. Together, these processes ensure that offspring inherit genetically unique combinations of alleles.
📷 Show original page
Q023
p88Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Complete dominance occurs when:
  • A)The phenotype of a heterozygote is identical to the homozygote.
  • B)The phenotype of a heterozygote is not identical to the homozygote.
  • C)The genotype of a heterozygote is identical to the homozygote.
  • D)The genotype of a heterozygote is not identical to the homozygote.
  • E)None of the above
✓ Answer: A) The phenotype of a heterozygote is identical to the homozygote.
Complete dominance describes the situation in which one allele (the dominant allele) fully masks the phenotypic effect of the other (recessive) allele in a heterozygote. Therefore, a heterozygote (Aa) expresses the same phenotype as the dominant homozygote (AA). The genotypes obviously still differ (Aa vs. AA), which is why options C and D are wrong - dominance is defined in terms of phenotype, not genotype.
📷 Show original page
Q024
p88Test YourselfMultiple Choice
How would you describe the development of horns in dung beetles?
  • A)Horn development is a phenotypically plastic trait.
  • B)Horn development is a result of dominant alleles.
  • C)Horn development is polyphenic.
  • D)All of the above
  • E)a and c only
✓ Answer: E) a and c only
Horn development in dung beetles is both phenotypically plastic (the same genotype can produce different phenotypes depending on environmental conditions such as larval nutrition) and polyphenic (individuals of the same population fall into discrete alternative morphs - horned vs. hornless - based on a threshold mechanism). It is not the straightforward result of a single dominant allele; rather, a body-size threshold triggers one of two discrete developmental programs.
📷 Show original page
Q025
p88Test YourselfMultiple Choice
According to Figure 5.19, do male dung beetles produce horns if their body size is 5mm?
  • A)Yes. All male dung beetles produce horns because horn length is genetically controlled.
  • B)Yes. A few male dung beetles with 5mm body sizes may produce horns, but their horns are relatively small.
  • C)Yes. Male dung beetles that are 5mm and produce horns are the most fit for their body size.
  • D)No. Because a 5mm body size is the critical threshold, only males larger than 5mm produce horns.
  • E)No. Males with body size of 5mm never produce horns.
✓ Answer: D) No. Because a 5mm body size is the critical threshold, only males larger than 5mm produce horns.
Dung beetle horn development is polyphenic and threshold-based. Figure 5.19 indicates that body size near 5 mm functions as the critical threshold: males smaller than this threshold develop as hornless, while males exceeding it grow horns whose length scales steeply with body size. The threshold dynamic produces two discrete male morphs rather than a smooth continuum.
📷 Show original page
Q026
p89Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Why don't all phenotypic traits occur as discrete, alternative states like Mendel's peas?
  • A)Because variation in the environment can lead to variation in the phenotypes that arise from a single genotype
  • B)Because the variation in some traits can be attributed to the cumulative action of many genes
  • C)Because phenotypes result from complex interactions between many different genes and the environment
  • D)a and c only
  • E)All of the above
✓ Answer: E) All of the above
Continuous phenotypic variation arises for several complementary reasons: (A) environments differ, so the same genotype can produce different phenotypes (phenotypic plasticity); (B) many traits are polygenic, with cumulative small effects from many loci; and (C) complex gene x gene and gene x environment interactions further blur discrete categories. Together these factors generate the continuous, quantitative variation typical of traits like height, skin color, and weight.
📷 Show original page
Q027
p89Test YourselfMultiple Choice
How does sexual reproduction contribute to the quantity of genetic diversity among individuals of a population?
  • A)By increasing the likelihood that an individual will have multiple offspring
  • B)By increasing the ploidy of individuals within the population
  • C)By separating, combining, and mixing alleles as a result of independent assortment and genetic recombination
  • D)By incorporating somatic mutations into the gametes or germ-line cells
  • E)By reducing the ability of an autosome to mask mutations affecting gene expression by the presence of a functional copy of the gene in the other autosome
✓ Answer: C) By separating, combining, and mixing alleles as a result of independent assortment and genetic recombination
Sexual reproduction generates genetic diversity primarily through two meiotic processes: independent assortment of homologous chromosomes (random segregation of maternal and paternal chromosomes into gametes) and genetic recombination (crossing over between homologous chromosomes). These processes produce gametes carrying novel combinations of alleles, so offspring inherit allele combinations different from either parent. Sexual reproduction does not change ploidy, does not cause mutations, and does not incorporate somatic mutations into gametes.
📷 Show original page
Q028
p89ContemplateShort Answer
Why are mutations important to evolution by natural selection?
✓ Answer: Mutations are the ultimate source of new genetic variation, which is the raw material upon which natural selection acts.
Natural selection cannot operate without heritable variation. Mutations introduce novel alleles into a population; some are neutral, some deleterious, and a rare few are beneficial. If a beneficial mutation occurs in a germ-line cell, it can be passed to offspring and, over generations, increase in frequency through selection. Without mutation there would be no new variants for selection to act upon, and adaptation could not occur. Mutations are random with respect to fitness, but selection is decidedly nonrandom - it filters these random changes based on their phenotypic consequences.
📷 Show original page
Q029
p89ContemplateShort Answer
If alcoholism is heritable, what factors can influence whether the children of an alcoholic will be alcoholics?
✓ Answer: Both genetic and environmental factors - and their interactions - influence whether children of an alcoholic will themselves become alcoholic.
Heritability does not mean destiny. Alcoholism is a complex, polygenic trait influenced by many loci of small effect, and its expression depends heavily on the environment. Factors include: - Genetic: specific alleles affecting alcohol metabolism (e.g., ADH and ALDH variants), reward/dopamine signaling, and impulsivity-related genes inherited from parents. - Environmental: exposure to alcohol, family dynamics, peer pressure, socioeconomic stressors, cultural norms, availability of alcohol, and co-occurring mental health conditions. - Gene x environment interactions: the same genotype may lead to alcoholism in one environment and not in another. - Epigenetic influences from parental experience can also modulate gene expression in offspring. Because phenotypes arise from genotype x environment interactions, a heritable predisposition is a risk factor, not a deterministic outcome.
📷 Show original page
Q030
p83Identify Key Terms (Concept Map)Matching / Fill-in
Using the concept map template provided in Chapter 5, identify how the following key terms relate to gene expression, mutation, and phenotype. Fill in the labels for each node in the concept map: - nucleotide, mutation, sequence, transfer RNA, ribosomal RNA, microRNA - trans-acting element, cis-acting element, transcription factor - external environment, gene expression, phenotype - germ-line, offspring Place each term in its proper location in the concept map so that the relationships (alters, codes for, affects, shapes) connecting nodes are correct.
✓ Answer: See the filled-in concept map in the answer key (page 318).
Key relationships in the completed map: - A mutation alters the nucleotide sequence. - The sequence codes for transfer RNA, ribosomal RNA, microRNA, cis-acting elements, and trans-acting elements (including transcription factors). - Transcription factors (trans-acting) affect gene expression by binding cis-acting elements near genes. - microRNA and other trans-acting elements also affect gene expression. - The external environment affects gene expression and affects phenotype directly. - Gene expression shapes the phenotype. - The phenotype (together with germ-line cells) influences offspring, because only mutations in germ-line cells are heritable. Together the map shows how a single nucleotide change can cascade through transcription, translation, and regulation to produce phenotypic differences that can be transmitted to the next generation when they occur in the germ line.

Ch 06 — The Ways of Change: Drift and Selection

28 questions
Q001
p90Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
According to the text, what is an organism's phenotype?
  • A)The interaction of an organism's genes with the environment to produce characteristics such as how the amount of light a plant is exposed to influences its height
  • B)Characteristics of an organism that can be classified into discrete categories, such as gender or eye color
  • C)Any aspect of an organism that can be measured, such as how it looks, how it behaves, how it's structured
  • D)The genetic makeup of an individual
  • E)Both b and d
✓ Answer: C
The phenotype is linked to the genotype—genes interact with other genes and with the environment during the development of the phenotype. Some aspects of the phenotype can be classified into discrete categories, such as gender (female or male) or eye color (blue, green, brown), but many other characteristics cannot, such as height, length of the femur, or antler size (these traits vary in a range around a mean). The phenotype is any aspect of an organism that can be measured: morphology, physiology, and behavior. Why the others are wrong: - A: Genes do interact with the environment during development, but the phenotype is the characteristic itself, not the interaction. - B: Some phenotypic characteristics are discrete, but many are continuous; limiting the definition to discrete categories is too narrow. - D: The genetic makeup of an individual is the genotype, not the phenotype. - E: Combines two incorrect definitions.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p90Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
How many alleles can a genetic locus in a diploid individual have?
  • A)One
  • B)Two
  • C)More than two
  • D)It depends on the locus.
✓ Answer: D
There are no hard and fast rules about the number of alleles at any particular genetic locus. Mutations can create new alleles at any genetic locus, and the number of alleles and their frequencies within a population can change as a result of natural selection and genetic drift. A more appropriate way of representing alleles uses subscripts (A1, A2, A3, A4) or superscripts (Ester1, Ester4), reflecting the diverse number of alleles that may exist at any particular locus. Why the others are wrong: - A: Only fixed alleles (where all members of a population are homozygous) show a single allele; fixed loci are not typical. - B: Simple models using AA/Aa/aa notation imply only two alleles exist, but this is merely a convention. - C: A locus can have more than two alleles, but it can also have only one (fixed) or two; the actual count depends on the locus.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p90Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
Why is the variation of phenotypic traits often continuous, distributed around a mean in a bell-shaped curve?
  • A)Because phenotypic traits are a result of dominance
  • B)Because phenotypic traits are not related to genotypes
  • C)Because phenotypic traits are only influenced by the environment
  • D)Because phenotypic traits are often polygenic
✓ Answer: D
Most phenotypic traits involve more than one gene (i.e., they are polygenic), and the different alleles from these different genes contribute to the trait differently. The result is a continuous distribution (like height, with all possible measurements in between). Most individuals will have measurements near the mean, with fewer and fewer in the "tails," so the curve appears bell-shaped. Why the others are wrong: - A: Dominance can affect phenotypic traits, but it refers to alleles at a single genetic locus—most traits are not reducible to simple Mendelian patterns. - B: Although the relationship is complex, genotype and phenotype are intimately related. - C: The environment influences phenotypic expression, but a distinct genetic architecture underlies it.

An allele that yields twice the phenotypic effect when two copies are present at a given locus than occurs when only one copy is present; not influenced by the presence of other alleles (no dominance). e. The type of selection that increases allele frequency in a population; occurs whenever the average excess for fitness of an allele is greater than zero. f. The type of selection that favors more than one allele; acts to maintain genetic diversity in a population. g. These definitions are drawn directly from the glossary/key-terms section on pages 92–93. Note the important distinctions: pleiotropy (one gene affects multiple traits) vs. antagonistic pleiotropy (where those multi-trait effects oppose each other in fitness); positive vs. negative selection distinguished by whether the average excess of fitness of an allele is greater or less than zero; and bottleneck (drastic size reduction of existing population) vs. founder effect (type of drift when a new population is founded by a small sample). F (inbreeding coefficient) applies to individuals while F_ST is a measure between populations.

Q005
p94Link ConceptsFill-in (concept map)
Fill in the bubbles of the concept map with the appropriate terms. The concept map organizes mechanisms of evolution and their effects on genotype/allele frequency and genetic variation within a population. Terms to place include: balancing selection, population subdivision, bottlenecks, founder effects, gene flow, genetic drift, heterozygote advantage, negative selection, negative frequency-dependent selection, positive selection. Key relationships to complete: - Random events include bottlenecks and founder effects (via genetic drift). - Gene flow, genetic drift, and selection each affect allele/genotype frequency. - Genetic drift → acts on allele frequency; increases or decreases depending on random event; affects variation within a population. - Balancing selection favors more than one allele → includes heterozygote advantage (favors heterozygotes) and negative frequency-dependent selection (favors rare genotypes). - Positive selection increases an allele's frequency; negative selection decreases it.
✓ Answer: - Random events (bottlenecks, founder effects) → feed into genetic drift
The concept map summarizes Chapter 6's framework for mechanisms of evolution. Genetic drift, gene flow, and selection are the three processes that change allele frequencies. Drift is the random sampling process (amplified by bottlenecks and founder effects). Selection comes in three broad flavors based on how fitness effects act: positive (directional increase), negative (directional decrease), and balancing (maintains multiple alleles). Balancing selection's two main mechanisms are negative frequency-dependent selection (rare wins) and heterozygote advantage (Aa > AA or aa).

✓ Answer: 6.1 The Genetics of Populations
This fill-in synthesizes the conceptual backbone of Chapter 6. Drift is a random sampling effect most potent in small populations; selection is directional (positive/negative) or balancing (maintains diversity via heterozygote advantage or negative frequency-dependent selection). Inbreeding redistributes existing alleles into more homozygous combinations without changing allele frequencies directly.

p98Interpret the DataShort Answer
Figure 6.18 shows data from Gigord et al. (2001) on elderflower orchids in Europe, which have a polymorphism for yellow and purple flowers. Unlike most flowers, these orchids produce no nectar, so naïve bumblebees pollinate them without reward. Panel B shows male fitness (pollinia removal) and panel C shows female fitness (fruit set / fertilized seeds) as a function of the frequency of the yellow morph. a) At the rarest frequency of the yellow morph, what was the relative reproductive success of males? Of females? b) At the most common frequency of the yellow morph, what was the relative reproductive success of males? Of females? c) Why might the foraging experiences of bumblebees drive this relationship?
✓ Answer: a) At the rarest frequency of the yellow morph: relative reproductive success was roughly 1.4 for males (range 1.35–1.5) and roughly 1.45 for females (range 1.3–1.6).
The negative correlations reported (r = -0.99 for pollinia removal and r = -0.85 for fruit set) demonstrate that reproductive success declines as the yellow morph becomes more common. This is the signature of negative frequency-dependent selection, a type of balancing selection that maintains polymorphism in a population. Bumblebee learning behavior is the ecological mechanism driving the rare-advantage dynamic.

Q008
p99Games and Exercises — When Can You Say That What You Observed Doesn't Match What You Expected to Find?Short Answer (chi-square application)
Cavalli-Sforza and colleagues examined 12,387 individuals for the A/S locus. They measured allele frequency p(A) = 0.877 and q(S) = 0.123. Hardy-Weinberg-expected counts were AA = 9,527.2 (76.9%), AS = 2,672.4 (21.6%), SS = 187.4 (1.5%). Observed counts were AA = 9,365 (75.6%), AS = 2,993 (24.2%), SS = 29 (0.2%). Chi-square calculation: (9,365 − 9,527.2)^2 / 9,527.2 = 2.76 (2,993 − 2,672.4)^2 / 2,672.4 = 38.46 (29 − 187.4)^2 / 187.4 = 133.89 X^2 = 2.76 + 38.46 + 133.89 = 175.11 df = (number of classes − 1) = 2 Compare to the chi-square critical-value table. What is the probability of obtaining a value this large by chance, and what likely caused the deviation from Hardy-Weinberg expectations?
✓ Answer: With df = 2, the largest critical value in the reference table (at p = 0.0005) is 15.20. The calculated X^2 = 175.11 far exceeds this value, so the probability of obtaining this chi-square value (or larger) by chance is much less than 0.0005. Therefore, the deviation from Hardy-Weinberg expectations is statistically highly significant.
The Pearson chi-square test quantifies how far observed counts deviate from expected (Hardy-Weinberg) counts. Large chi-square values, relative to the appropriate df, indicate that chance alone is an unlikely explanation. When a population violates Hardy-Weinberg expectations, at least one of the four assumptions (no mutation, no migration, no drift, no selection, plus random mating) has been broken. In this case, selection—specifically heterozygote advantage due to malaria resistance—is the widely accepted explanation.

Q009
p101Games and Exercises — White Bears and Hardy-WeinbergShort Answer (Hardy-Weinberg calculation)
Kermode ("Spirit") bears are white bears found in coastal British Columbia. The white fur is a recessive allele (W) unique to this subspecies. Ritland et al. found approximately 20% of the bears on Princess Royal and Gribbell Islands are white; the rest are black. Assuming complete dominance and Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium: a) What does q^2 equal? b) What does p^2 equal? c) What does 2pq equal? d) At what frequency should BB, BW, and WW genotypes occur? e) In a population of 37 bears on the two islands, how many would you expect to be black? How many white? f) On the mainland, 100 km from the coast, the frequency of the W allele drops to 0.08. For a population of 112 bears, how many would you expect to have black fur? How many Spirit Bears?
✓ Answer: a) q^2 = 0.20 (the frequency of white = WW homozygotes, which is 20% of the island population). Thus q = sqrt(0.20) ≈ 0.45.
With complete recessive inheritance, the frequency of the visible recessive phenotype directly equals q^2, from which q, p, and the remaining genotype frequencies are derived. The stark drop from ~20% white bears on the islands to roughly 1 white bear per 112 on the mainland reflects the dramatic reduction in W-allele frequency with distance from the coastal islands, suggesting geographic population structure and possibly selection (white fur may aid fishing success in coastal rainforests).

Q010
p102Games and Exercises — White Bears and SelectionShort Answer (selection coefficient and average excess of fitness)
Ritland and colleagues found q(W) = 0.369 for Kermode bears across Gribbell, Princess Royal, and Roderick islands, consisting of 87 bears: 42 BB, 24 BW, and 21 WW. a) What is p? Using Hardy-Weinberg, compute expected BB, BW, WW counts. Calculate X^2 with df = 2. Which genotypes are over- or underrepresented? b) Assume black bears have a selection coefficient s = 0.2 (relative fitness 0.8) and white bears have relative fitness 1.0. After one generation of selection: - Compute the post-selection genotype contributions (frequency × fitness). - Compute the average fitness w̄ of the population. - Compute the new genotype frequencies. - Compute p_(t+1) and q_(t+1). - What proportion of each genotype occurs in the next generation? - What does the selection coefficient mean for persistence of the B allele? c) Now compute the same change using the average excess of fitness (a_B and a_W) method: a_B = [p × (w_BB − w̄)] + [q × (w_BW − w̄)] Δp = (p/w̄) × a_B Do the same for the W allele. d) Compare observed/expected counts for the original 87 bears. What does this tell us about the population?
✓ Answer: a) p = 1 − 0.369 = 0.631.
This exercise demonstrates two mathematically equivalent frameworks for tracking selection: (1) selection coefficient applied to genotype frequencies, and (2) average excess of fitness applied to allele frequencies. With complete dominance (as in Kermode bears), both yield the same new allele frequencies. The deficit of heterozygotes in the observed data cannot be explained by simple directional selection against B alone (which would preserve Hardy-Weinberg proportions among survivors), so inbreeding/assortative mating or migration is likely also operating.

Q011
p107Overcoming MisconceptionsMultiple Choice
Which of the following is a true statement?
  • A)A dominant allele is an allele that produces the same phenotype whether it is homozygous or heterozygous.
  • B)The dominant allele of a trait will always have the highest frequency in a population.
  • C)Genetic drift does not occur in large populations.
  • D)Alleles are different forms of genes.
✓ Answer: A
A dominant allele is defined by its phenotypic effect: a single copy is sufficient to produce the trait, so the homozygous (AA) and heterozygous (Aa) individuals show the same phenotype. The answer key for this chapter identifies option A as the correct true statement. Why the others are wrong: - B: Dominance refers to phenotypic expression, not frequency. Huntington's disease is rare but dominant; blood type O is common but recessive. Allele frequencies are determined by selection and drift, not by dominance. - C: Genetic drift occurs in all populations—it is the random sampling of alleles each generation. Larger populations simply experience smaller proportional changes from drift because more copies of each allele are present. - D: Although intuitive-sounding, this answer is imprecise. An allele is a specific alternative form of a gene or genetic locus, identified by a particular DNA sequence; a gene is a segment of DNA whose sequence codes for protein/RNA or regulates expression. Calling alleles "different forms of genes" treats "gene" and "allele" as interchangeable, which the textbook flags as a misconception.
📷 Show original page
Q012
p108Go the Distance — Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer
Warren Johnson and colleagues reviewed a historic conservation decision: moving eight female pumas (Puma concolor stanleyana) from Texas to Florida to increase genetic diversity in a remnant population of Florida panthers (Puma concolor coryi). Fifteen years later, population size, heterozygosity, survival, and fitness had increased, and inbreeding measures had declined. a) What led to the levels of heterozygosity found in the Florida panther population prior to the management decision? b) Why did moving eight females affect the current levels of heterozygosity in the Florida panther population? c) Texas pumas are a different subspecies than Florida panthers. What impact, if any, would you argue this move had on those taxonomic designations?
✓ Answer: a) The Florida panther population was reduced to a very small remnant (a severe genetic bottleneck) through historical habitat loss and hunting. The small, isolated population experienced intense genetic drift, which eroded allelic diversity, and obligate inbreeding among the few remaining individuals drove heterozygosity still lower (inbreeding depression was evident in traits such as cryptorchidism, cardiac defects, and low sperm quality).
This case illustrates genetic rescue—using migrants from a related population to counteract inbreeding depression in a small, isolated population. It exemplifies how gene flow can counteract drift and increase fitness, but it also raises philosophical questions about taxonomy: does conservation-driven admixture undermine the subspecies concept, or is preservation of a viable population more important than preserving genetic "purity"?
📷 Show original page
Q013
p108Delve DeeperShort Answer
If a mutation that produces a new allele that is deleterious arises in a population, what will most likely happen to the frequency of that allele?
✓ Answer: Not all deleterious mutations disappear from populations; the outcome depends on the allele's effect on the phenotype. If the mutant allele is recessive, it may be rare enough that it is almost never expressed in a homozygous state, and it can even remain at a low frequency within the population for a very long time (hidden as heterozygotes). Of course, genetic drift may also determine whether the allele persists in the population.
Negative selection acts on the phenotype. For a dominant deleterious allele, every carrier expresses the detrimental phenotype, so negative selection removes the allele efficiently and it is usually driven to low frequency quickly (unless offset by mutation-selection balance). For a recessive deleterious allele, only homozygotes express the harmful phenotype; heterozygotes (who carry most copies of a rare allele) are phenotypically normal and invisible to selection. This is why rare deleterious recessive alleles can persist for many generations—a central point made in Chapter 6 about why selection cannot drive dominant (functional) alleles to complete fixation. In small populations, drift can also push such alleles up or down randomly.
📷 Show original page
Q014
p108Delve DeeperShort Answer
Why has the evolution of resistance to insecticides in mosquitoes been so rapid?
✓ Answer: Insecticides, and other pesticides, can impose extremely strong selection. Pest populations often are highly genetically variable. They don't necessarily have mutation rates any higher than other organisms; the variation comes from their large effective population size and rapid reproduction. Large amounts of standing genetic variation mean that populations are poised for rapid evolutionary responses to selection (h^2 is very large, and R = h^2 × s), and vast population sizes mean that the effects of drift are likely to be weak compared with the effects of selection.
Three ingredients underlie the rapid evolution of insecticide resistance in mosquitoes: 1. Strong selection (large s): Insecticides kill non-resistant individuals at extremely high rates, producing a very large selection coefficient against susceptible genotypes. 2. Abundant heritable variation (large h^2): With enormous census and effective population sizes and short generation times, mosquito populations carry substantial standing genetic variation, including rare resistance alleles that pre-exist the first insecticide application. 3. Minimal drift relative to selection: Large populations mean that beneficial alleles are unlikely to be lost by chance, so selection can act efficiently on them. The breeder's equation R = h^2 × s formalizes why strong selection on a heritable trait produces rapid response, and mosquitoes satisfy both terms simultaneously.
📷 Show original page
Q015
p109Test YourselfMultiple Choice
What are genetic loci?
  • A)Variants of a particular gene or DNA region
  • B)The plural form of genetic locus
  • C)The specific locations of genes or base pairs
  • D)Both a and b
  • E)Both b and c
✓ Answer: E
A genetic locus is the specific location of a gene or piece of DNA sequence on a chromosome; "loci" is the plural of "locus." Both B (plural form) and C (specific locations) are correct, so the combined answer E is correct. Why the others are wrong: - A: Variants of a gene are alleles, not loci. An allele is the DNA sequence variant; the locus is the location where that variant sits. Confusing these is a common misconception. - D and F: Incorporate the incorrect answer A.
📷 Show original page
Q016
p109Test YourselfMultiple Choice
In a tetraploid organism, how many copies of the same allele will it carry at a locus if it is heterozygous at that locus?
  • A)one
  • B)two
  • C)three
  • D)four
  • E)a, b, or c
✓ Answer: E
A tetraploid organism carries four copies of each chromosome, so four alleles at each locus. "Heterozygous" simply means NOT all identical. The four alleles could be distributed as 3-1, 2-2, 2-1-1, or 1-1-1-1 combinations. Therefore the number of copies of "the same allele" the organism carries at a heterozygous locus could be 1 (all four different, or three different plus one copy of the focal allele), 2 (two pairs), or 3 (three copies of one allele and one of another). Four copies of the same allele would be homozygous, not heterozygous. So the answer is a, b, or c (one, two, or three). Why the others are wrong: - A, B, C each alone: Any of these is possible but not the only possibility. - D (four): Four copies of the same allele means homozygous, the opposite of the premise.
📷 Show original page
Q017
p109Test YourselfMultiple Choice
How can population genetics help us understand the evolution of mosquito resistance?
  • A)By examining how the applications of pesticides cause mortality of mosquitoes
  • B)By examining the distribution of alleles within populations and the mechanisms that cause allele frequencies to change over time
  • C)By examining the mating and reproductive tactics of mosquitoes across populations that are resistant and non-resistant
  • D)By examining the phylogenetic relationships among species of mosquitoes and their common ancestors
  • E)Populations genetics examines all of the above.
✓ Answer: B
Population genetics is defined in Chapter 6 as the study of the distribution of alleles within populations and the mechanisms that can cause allele frequencies to change over time. Applying this framework to mosquitoes allows researchers to quantify resistance-allele frequencies across populations, identify selective sweeps, and estimate the strength and speed of selection driven by insecticide use. Why the others are wrong: - A: Describes toxicology/pharmacology, not allele-level dynamics. - C: Describes behavioral ecology, not the genetic architecture of resistance. - D: Describes phylogenetics at the species level—a different scale. - E: Incorrect because options A, C, and D are not part of population genetics.

Q018
p109Test YourselfMultiple Choice
What are population geneticists referring to when they say that sexually reproducing organisms are mating at random?
  • A)That, for a focal genetic locus, the probability of fertilization of one gamete by another will not vary depending on which allele is carried by the gametes
  • B)That mate choice is not an important factor for sexually reproducing organisms
  • C)That some gametes are not any more likely to encounter other gametes
  • D)That models of population genetics have to make unrealistic assumptions about sexually reproducing organisms to be useful
  • E)That sexually reproducing organisms choose mates randomly within the population
✓ Answer: A
"Random mating" in population genetics is a technical statement about gamete-level probabilities at a focal locus: the chance that one gamete fertilizes another does not depend on the alleles they carry at that locus. This assumption is what allows Hardy-Weinberg expectations (p^2 + 2pq + q^2) to hold. It does NOT mean organisms lack mate preferences in general—only that, with respect to the locus under study, those preferences do not create a correlation between allele identity and fertilization probability. Why the others are wrong: - B: Mate choice is often very important; the assumption is about allele-level outcomes at a specific locus, not the absence of mate choice. - C: Incorrect—random mating assumes gametes DO have equal opportunity to encounter others. - D: A philosophical mischaracterization of the purpose of models. - E: Organisms do not literally pick mates at random; this is a common misreading of the assumption.

Q019
p109Test YourselfMultiple Choice
In a population of 100 offspring, how many individuals would you predict will be heterozygous at a particular locus with two alleles if the frequency of one of the alleles in the parent generation is 0.4?
  • A)16
  • B)36
  • C)48
  • D)52
  • E)100
✓ Answer: C
Using Hardy-Weinberg, with p = 0.4 and q = 0.6, the expected frequency of heterozygotes is: 2pq = 2 × 0.4 × 0.6 = 0.48. For 100 offspring: 100 × 0.48 = 48 heterozygous individuals. Why the others are wrong: - A (16): That's p^2 × 100 = 0.16 × 100 — the frequency of the p-homozygote, not heterozygotes. - B (36): That's q^2 × 100 = 0.36 × 100 — the frequency of the q-homozygote. - D (52): No simple calculation produces this value. - E (100): Would require that every offspring be heterozygous, which is impossible when p and q are both nonzero and mating is random.
📷 Show original page
Q020
p110Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which of these statements about balancing selection is TRUE?
  • A)It is possible only in sexually reproducing species.
  • B)It is responsible for maintaining the S allele for sickle-cell anemia within humans.
  • C)It is not possible when heterozygotes have a higher fitness.
  • D)It is why sickle-cell anemia is selected against in areas with high levels of malaria.
  • E)None. All are false statements.
✓ Answer: B
Heterozygote advantage is a form of balancing selection. In regions with endemic malaria, heterozygotes (AS) at the β-hemoglobin locus have higher fitness than either AA (more vulnerable to malaria) or SS (severe sickle-cell anemia). This maintains the S allele at appreciable frequency in the population even though SS homozygotes have drastically reduced fitness—a textbook illustration of balancing selection preserving allelic diversity. Why the others are wrong: - A: Balancing selection can operate in any species with heritable variation; it is not restricted to sexual reproducers. - C: Heterozygote advantage is itself a form of balancing selection, so balancing selection is certainly possible when heterozygotes have higher fitness. - D: Sickle-cell homozygotes (SS) are selected against, but balancing selection explains why S is MAINTAINED, not why it is selected against. The statement misrepresents the mechanism.
📷 Show original page
Q021
p110Test YourselfMultiple Choice
How does drift affect the frequencies of alleles within a population?
  • A)Some individuals are more likely to breed with other individuals, and so only their alleles will appear in the next generation.
  • B)Random mating does not equal uniform mating, and as a result of this imperfect sampling, some alleles do not get represented in the next generation.
  • C)In large populations, the likelihood that all individuals will be able to mate is low, so the likelihood that all alleles being represented in the next generation is also low.
  • D)Drift results in a variety of genotypes over many generations because the heterozygotes mate randomly leading to some homozygotes of each allele and some heterozygotes, changing the frequency of the alleles.
  • E)Random mating within a population mixes alleles at a particular locus into many different combinations, and when this happens, frequencies of alleles change across generations.
✓ Answer: B
Genetic drift is the random, nonrepresentative sampling of alleles from the parental gene pool during breeding. Even if mating is random in the technical sense, the finite number of gametes that actually contribute to the next generation is a sample of the parental allele frequencies, and sampling produces stochastic deviations. Some alleles (especially rare ones) may fail to be sampled and drop to lower frequency or disappear entirely by chance alone. This is exactly what "imperfect sampling" captures in option B. Why the others are wrong: - A: Describes nonrandom mating (e.g., sexual selection or assortative mating), not drift. - C: Inverts the relationship—drift is MORE potent in small populations, not large ones. - D: Describes Mendelian segregation into genotype classes, not drift's effect on allele frequencies. - E: Random mating shuffles genotype combinations but does not itself change allele frequencies; that's a key Hardy-Weinberg insight.
📷 Show original page
Q022
p110Test YourselfMultiple Choice
According to Figure 6.8, what is the difference between the probabilities that a rare allele and a common allele will be lost in a population bottleneck of 20 individuals?
  • A)About 65 percent
  • B)100 percent
  • C)5 percent
  • D)0.9 percent
  • E)It depends on the allele.
✓ Answer: A
Figure 6.8 plots the probability of losing an allele through a bottleneck as a function of both starting allele frequency and bottleneck size. At a bottleneck size of 20 individuals, the probability of losing a rare allele is high (near or above ~65%) while the probability of losing a common allele is very low (near 0%). The difference is approximately 65 percentage points. Why the others are wrong: - B (100%): Implies certainty of loss of the rare allele AND certainty of retention of the common allele, an overestimate. - C (5%): Too small—matches the probability of losing a common allele alone, not the difference. - D (0.9%): Far too small. - E: Not appropriate because the figure specifically quantifies this comparison.
📷 Show original page
Q023
p110Test YourselfMultiple Choice
What is the difference between bottlenecks and founder effects?
  • A)Bottlenecks reduce genetic variation because of drift; founder effects reduce genetic variation by shrinking the population size.
  • B)Bottlenecks reduce genetic variation by shrinking the population size; founder effects reduce genetic variation because of drift.
  • C)Bottlenecks can happen to any population; founder effects only happen when small numbers of individuals start a new population.
  • D)Bottlenecks are events that reduce the number of individuals; founder effects describe the loss of genetic variation that accompanies events like bottlenecks.
  • E)Bottlenecks only affect the diversity of alleles when they severely cut down the population; founder effects always severely cut down the population.
✓ Answer: C
Bottlenecks are drastic reductions in the size of an existing population (e.g., from disease, climate, or hunting), and they can happen to any population. Founder effects are a specific type of drift that occurs when a small number of individuals colonize a new area and establish a new population, so that the new population begins with an unrepresentative sample of the source population's alleles. Both involve drift acting on a small sample, but they differ in scenario: bottleneck = existing population shrinks; founder effect = new population starts small. Why the others are wrong: - A and B: Incorrectly separate the mechanisms. Both bottlenecks and founder effects reduce genetic variation through drift acting on small sample sizes. - D: Conflates the two—both events reduce individuals AND involve loss of variation; they differ in context, not in whether they "are events" vs. "describe loss." - E: Mischaracterizes both—bottlenecks can affect diversity at any severity, and founder effects do not require severe cuts in an existing population (they start fresh).
📷 Show original page
Q024
p110Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which type of natural selection favors rare genotypes?
  • A)Balancing selection
  • B)Negative-frequency dependent selection
  • C)Negative selection
  • D)Positive selection
  • E)None of the above favor rare genotypes.
✓ Answer: B
Negative frequency-dependent selection occurs when the fitness of an allele or genotype is higher when it is rare than when it is common. This specifically favors rare genotypes. The elderflower orchid example (Figure 6.18) and the bumblebee learning dynamic illustrate this mechanism, as do many host-parasite systems where parasites track the most common host genotype. Why the others are wrong: - A (Balancing selection): This is a broader umbrella term that includes negative frequency-dependent selection AND heterozygote advantage. B is more specific and precise for "favors rare genotypes." - C (Negative selection): Removes deleterious alleles, reducing their frequency; it doesn't favor anything rare per se. - D (Positive selection): Increases allele frequency regardless of starting frequency; once an allele becomes common, positive selection still favors it, not "rarity." - E: Incorrect because B exactly describes the phenomenon. Note: One could argue A is also technically correct since negative frequency-dependent selection is a type of balancing selection, but B is the more specific and direct answer the question is looking for.
📷 Show original page
Q025
p111Test YourselfMultiple Choice
What does an inbreeding coefficient of 0.25 signify?
  • A)0.25 is the inbreeding coefficient assigned to Charles II.
  • B)0.25 is the average probability that alleles at two loci in an individual are identical by descent.
  • C)0.25 signifies the level of relatedness of most populations.
  • D)0.25 is the inbreeding coefficient of most royal families.
  • E)0.25 is the inbreeding coefficient of a child produced by a brother and sister mating.
✓ Answer: E
The inbreeding coefficient F is the probability that two alleles at a locus in an individual are identical by descent (IBD)—i.e., inherited from a common ancestor. For a child of full siblings (brother × sister), the probability that both alleles at any given locus trace back to the same grandparental allele is 0.25. This is the classic textbook value for a full-sib mating. Why the others are wrong: - A: Charles II of Spain had F ≈ 0.254 due to generations of cousin/uncle-niece marriages—coincidentally near 0.25, but this was the result of many generations of compound inbreeding, not a defining meaning of 0.25. The textbook uses him as a historical example but the definition of 0.25 is the full-sib value. - B: Misstates the definition—F is about alleles at a single locus (not "two loci"), identical by descent. - C: Most populations are not at F = 0.25. - D: Not a defining meaning; royal-family F values vary widely.
📷 Show original page
Q026
p111Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Why would conservation biologists be concerned about inbreeding depression?
  • A)Because endangered species often have small populations prone to inbreeding
  • B)Because rare, recessive alleles can become expressed in a homozygous state where they can reduce the fitness of individuals
  • C)Because inbreeding depression can affect reproductive rates of endangered species
  • D)Because inbreeding depression can reduce the genetic variation within a population
  • E)All of the above
✓ Answer: E
Every option identifies a legitimate conservation concern: - A: Small endangered populations are statistically more likely to experience mating between relatives simply because there are fewer unrelated individuals available. - B: The mechanistic basis of inbreeding depression—rare deleterious recessive alleles usually hidden in heterozygotes become exposed in homozygous descendants of related parents. - C: Reduced reproductive rates (lower fertility, offspring viability, juvenile survival) directly threaten population persistence. - D: Inbreeding homogenizes genotypes, reducing effective heterozygosity and thus the raw material for future adaptation. Together these illustrate why genetic rescue (e.g., the Florida panther Texas-puma translocation in the Go the Distance reading) is sometimes needed to restore heterozygosity and fitness.
📷 Show original page
Q027
p111ContemplateShort Answer (open-ended)
Why can't scientists conduct experiments like Richard Lenski's with organisms such as birds or mammals?
✓ Answer:
The Lenski experiment's power comes from coupling short generation times, huge population sizes, cheap housing, and the ability to freeze and revive ancestors. Birds and mammals break every one of these requirements, which is why most vertebrate evolutionary studies rely on natural populations (e.g., Darwin's finches, Trinidadian guppies) or on comparisons across preserved museum specimens rather than on controlled laboratory evolution.
📷 Show original page
Q028
p111ContemplateShort Answer (open-ended)
What might happen to a flower adapted to mountain habitats over time? Would climate change affect your predictions?
✓ Answer:
This question integrates several Chapter 6 concepts: population structure, drift, inbreeding, gene flow, and selection. It highlights how climate change can disrupt the usual balance between drift, gene flow, and selection, often pushing small mountain populations toward extinction via the combined effects of shrinking populations (intensified drift), habitat isolation (reduced gene flow), and directional selection that the population may be too small or too slow to meet.

Ch 07 — Beyond Alleles: Quantitative Genetics and the Evolution of Phenotypes

26 questions
Q001
p112Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: c
Section 5.4 introduces polyphenic and polygenic traits. Polyphenic traits often arise because of a threshold of sensitivity to the environment (e.g., an individual male beetle that would produce horns if food was plentiful but not produce horns if food was scarce). Polygenic traits are also known as quantitative traits - the topic of this chapter.

Q002
p112Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d
Darwin used two facts to infer that natural selection would lead to evolution and the production of new species: that no two individuals are exactly the same and much of the variation among individuals within a population was heritable (see Section 2.3 and Figure 2.19). Option c reflects Lamarck's theory of inheritance of acquired characteristics, which Darwin did not endorse.

Q003
p112Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: b
Some traits that are sensitive to environmental stimuli are heritable. Phenotypic plasticity is the changes in the phenotype produced by a single genotype in different environments (see Section 5.5). This chapter will explore the mechanism of phenotypic plasticity and its measurement (see Box 7.4 and Section 7.4).

Q004
p113Identify Key TermsMatching
✓ Answer: 1=a; 2=e; 3=b; 4=g; 5=f; 6=c; 7=h; 8=d
From answer key (p. 315): 1. a 2. e 3. b 4. g 5. f 6. c 7. h 8. d. Broad sense heritability (H^2) = a (total genetic variance / phenotypic variance). GWA = e (scanning genomes for markers). Narrow sense heritability (h^2) = b (additive genetic variance). Quantitative genetics = g (study of continuous traits). QTLs = f (DNA stretches correlated with traits). Reaction norm = c (phenotype across environments). Selection differential (S) = h (strength of selection). Variance = d (dispersion measure).

Q006
p115Key Concepts - Section 7.1 Genetics of Quantitative TraitsFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: (1) additive; phenotypic; variation. (2) broad sense heritability (H^2); phenotypic; genetic. (3) additive, dominance, epistatic; offspring (or relatives). (4) additive; variance.
No explicit answer key provided in the study guide for Key Concepts fill-ins. Answers derived from Section 7.1 of the Zimmer/Emlen textbook: variance components are additive when independent; heritability (H^2) is the proportion of phenotypic variance due to genetic variance; broad sense heritability includes additive, dominance, and epistatic gene effects as well as maternal/parental environmental effects that cause relatives to resemble one another; narrow sense heritability (h^2) reflects only the additive component of genetic variance, which is what is reliably transmitted to offspring.
📷 Show original page
Q007
p115Key Concepts - Section 7.2 The Evolutionary Response to SelectionFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: (1) directional selection; stabilizing selection; disruptive selection. (2) Selection; evolution (or response to selection). (3) strength of selection (selection differential, S); narrow sense heritability (h^2).
No explicit answer key provided. Answers derived from Section 7.2 of the Zimmer/Emlen textbook. The three modes of selection are directional (favoring one tail), stabilizing (favoring the mean), and disruptive (favoring both tails). Selection is the fitness difference among phenotypes; evolution is the genetic change from one generation to the next. The breeder's equation R = h^2 x S shows that the response to selection equals narrow sense heritability times the selection differential.
📷 Show original page
Q008
p115Key Concepts - Section 7.3 Dissecting Complex Traits: Quantitative Trait Locus AnalysisFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: genome (or chromosome); quantitative (or polygenic, complex); variation (or diversity, differences).
No explicit answer key provided. Answers derived from Section 7.3. QTL (quantitative trait locus) mapping identifies chromosomal regions of the genome that are statistically associated with variation in quantitative (polygenic) traits. These linked markers serve as a starting point for identifying the specific genes underlying phenotypic variation.

Q009
p116Key Concepts - Section 7.4 The Evolution of Phenotypic PlasticityFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: (1) heritable; reaction norms; phenotypic plasticity. (2) lifetime; frequencies. (3) heritable (or genetic, evolutionary); frequencies.
No explicit answer key provided. Answers derived from Section 7.4. Heritable differences in reaction norms can themselves evolve, producing adaptive phenotypic plasticity. Within-lifetime phenotypic change is not evolution; only intergenerational changes in allele frequencies constitute evolution. A heritable shift in the amount or form of plasticity therefore IS evolution because it reflects a change in allele frequencies across generations.

Q016
p123Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer
✓ Answer: Open-ended literature exercise. From Mills et al. 2013: the initiation dates of the seasonal molts (when hares start changing color) appear to have limited plasticity and are largely tied to photoperiod (day length). However, the rate of molt and the overall duration of white coat were more plastic, responding to snow duration and local conditions to a limited degree. The critical finding is that the initiation of the molt is fixed by photoperiod cues, so hares cannot readily adjust the timing when snow arrives late or melts early - producing camouflage mismatch as snowpack duration declines with climate change.
No formal answer key provided. Students are directed to the Mills et al. 2013 PNAS paper (110: 7360-7365). The take-home message is that some seasonal color-change components are strongly canalized (initiation timing via photoperiod) while others show modest plasticity - which matters because climate change is shortening snow duration faster than selection on photoperiod cues can track.

Q018
p123Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer
✓ Answer: Possibly, but slowly. Because molt initiation is largely cued by photoperiod (which does not change with climate), hares cannot adjust via plasticity alone. Evolutionary change in the timing of molt would require heritable variation in molt initiation date and strong selection over many generations. Mills et al. argue that some populations show such variation and selection could act - but whether the response can keep up with rapid climate change is uncertain. Short-generation time and existing geographic variation in molt timing offer some hope, but mismatch mortality is projected to increase substantially before adaptive evolution catches up.

Key concept: plasticity alone is insufficient because the proximal cue (photoperiod) decouples from the ecologically relevant cue (snow cover) under climate change; evolutionary rescue depends on standing heritable variation in molt timing and the strength of selection.

Q019
p123Delve DeeperShort Answer
✓ Answer: No. Evolution of a population depends on how much phenotypic variation is attributable to additive genetic differences among individuals.
From answer key (p. 318). Per the breeder's equation R = h^2 x S: if narrow sense heritability (h^2) is zero - meaning none of the phenotypic variance is additive genetic - then the response to selection R = 0, regardless of the selection differential S. Populations can thus experience selection without evolving when trait variation is entirely environmental or is due to non-additive genetic effects (dominance, epistasis) that do not reliably transmit.

✓ Answer: e

Broad-sense heritability H^2 = V_G / V_P (a, c). Rearranging gives V_G = H^2 x V_P (d). Therefore all statements describe correct aspects of the variance-components model.

The population with the steepest parent-offspring regression slope (the highest narrow-sense heritability, h^2) will evolve fastest for a given selection differential, because R = h^2 x S. Of the three, wild radish has the highest h^2 (shown in Figure 7.5 as having the steepest slope), so it is predicted to evolve most in response to selection.

Q025
p125ContemplateReflection
✓ Answer: Both - animal behavior can be heritable AND plastic simultaneously. Many behaviors (e.g., courtship displays, migratory orientation, foraging preferences) show heritable variation: offspring resemble their parents because the underlying neural and sensory architecture is genetically encoded. At the same time, most behaviors are plastic because they respond to learning, experience, social context, and current environmental conditions. The heritable component is the capacity (reaction norm) to respond to input; the plastic component is the specific expression of behavior in a given environment. This is analogous to morphological plasticity where a single genotype produces different phenotypes across environments.
No formal answer key provided. The question asks students to synthesize Chapter 7's concepts (heritability, phenotypic plasticity, reaction norms) and apply them to behavior. Key insight: the 'heritability vs. plasticity' dichotomy is false - most traits, including behavior, show both.
📷 Show original page
Q026
p125ContemplateReflection
✓ Answer: Plants, being sessile, cannot move to a better environment when conditions change - so phenotypic plasticity is the primary mechanism by which they cope with environmental heterogeneity. Examples include: (1) shade avoidance (stem elongation and reduced branching in low light); (2) heterophylly (different leaf shapes in sun vs. shade, or aquatic vs. aerial leaves); (3) allocation shifts between root vs. shoot biomass depending on water/nutrient availability; (4) plastic flowering time in response to photoperiod and temperature (vernalization); (5) induced chemical defenses following herbivory; (6) osmotic adjustments to drought or salinity. Because the environment experienced by any individual plant is unpredictable and cannot be escaped, selection strongly favors genotypes that produce context-appropriate phenotypes - i.e., adaptive plasticity.
No formal answer key provided. Key insight: sessility and environmental unpredictability together create strong selection for adaptive phenotypic plasticity in plants.
