Ch 08 — Natural Selection: Empirical Studies in the Wild

48 questions
Q001
p0Check Your UnderstandingCheck Your Understanding (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. All of the above
Chapter 5 discusses how variation in the genotype arises and how that genetic variation can be translated into phenotypic variation. Genetic recombination (Section 5.3), mutation (Section 5.2), and independent assortment (Section 5.3) all contribute to variation among individuals.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p0Check Your UnderstandingCheck Your Understanding (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. Natural selection because variation among individuals is the foundation for relative success of individuals, and that relative success affects the frequencies of alleles within the population
Section 6.6 discusses relative fitness and how natural selection affects allele frequencies. Natural selection depends directly on differences between individuals: without variation among individuals, some cannot have higher relative success than others. Genetic drift is a random process whose effects depend on population size, not on how different individuals are. Migration and mutation are not primarily driven by amount of variation.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p0Check Your UnderstandingCheck Your Understanding (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. The difference between the trait mean of reproducing individuals and the trait mean of the general population
The selection differential is a measure used by quantitative geneticists to examine the strength of selection on a phenotype (see Section 7.2). Selection can be positive or negative but selection itself is a mechanism, not evolution. Whether evolution occurs in response to selection also depends on the narrow-sense heritability of the trait (Section 7.1).
📷 Show original page
Q004
p0Identify Key TermsIdentify Key Terms (Matching)
✓ Answer: f. An antipredator strategy used by a potential prey item to signal danger or a lack of palatability. The most commonly known form is warning coloration, in which the bright coloration of prey that are potentially dangerous can act as a deterrent to potential predators.
Aposematism refers to warning signals (most commonly bright warning coloration) advertising that a prey item is dangerous, toxic, or unpalatable. This is distinct from crypsis (camouflage) and Mullerian or Batesian mimicry, though mimicry often co-opts aposematic signals.
📷 Show original page
Q005
p0Identify Key TermsIdentify Key Terms (Matching)
✓ Answer: d. Similar to natural selection, except that it results from human activity. When breeders nonrandomly choose individuals with economically favorable traits to use as breeding stock, they impose strong artificial selection on those traits.
Artificial selection differs from natural selection only in the selective agent: humans intentionally choose which individuals reproduce. This is the mechanism that produced dog breeds, crops, and the many domesticated varieties Darwin used as analogy for natural selection in "On the Origin of Species."
📷 Show original page
Q006
p0Identify Key TermsIdentify Key Terms (Matching)
✓ Answer: g. Evolution driven by competition between species for a shared resource (e.g., food). Traits evolve in opposing directions, minimizing overlap between the species.
Ecological character displacement is the evolutionary divergence of competing species in sympatry. The classic textbook example is Darwin's finches on the Galapagos, where beak shapes of co-occurring species diverge to reduce competition for seeds of particular sizes.
📷 Show original page
Q007
p0Identify Key TermsIdentify Key Terms (Matching)
✓ Answer: c. Structures constructed by organisms that can influence their performance or success. Although they are not part of the organism itself, their properties nevertheless reflect the genotype of each individual. Animal examples include the nests constructed by birds and the galls of flies.
Coined by Richard Dawkins, the extended phenotype refers to heritable effects of genes that extend beyond the organism's body. A gall fly's gall, a beaver's dam, and a spider's web are all extended phenotypes subject to natural selection even though they are not tissues of the organism itself.
📷 Show original page
Q008
p0Identify Key TermsIdentify Key Terms (Matching)
✓ Answer: a. The transfer of alleles from one population to another. It occurs when organisms or their gametes move from one location to another.
Gene flow is the evolutionary force that homogenizes allele frequencies across populations. Migration of individuals (animals, seeds, pollen, gametes) moves alleles between populations, often counteracting local adaptation by diluting locally favored alleles.
📷 Show original page
Q009
p0Identify Key TermsIdentify Key Terms (Matching)
✓ Answer: e. The physical proximity of alleles at different loci. Genetic loci that are physically close to one another on the same chromosome are less likely to be separated by recombination during meiosis. Thus they are said to be genetically linked.
Genetic linkage means loci close together on the same chromosome tend to be inherited together as a block. Strong linkage between a favored allele and neighboring alleles produces genetic hitchhiking during a selective sweep.
📷 Show original page
Q010
p0Identify Key TermsIdentify Key Terms (Matching)
✓ Answer: b. The situation in which strong selection can "sweep" a favorable allele to fixation within a population so fast that there is little opportunity for recombination. In the absence of recombination, alleles in large stretches of DNA flanking the favorable allele will also reach high frequency.
A selective sweep leaves a characteristic genomic signature: a region of reduced genetic variation (because hitchhiking alleles rise along with the favored allele). Scientists use this signature to detect recent positive selection in genomes.
📷 Show original page
Q011
p0Key Concepts 8.1 Evolution in a Bird's BeakKey Concepts (Fill in the Blank)
✓ Answer: (1) heritable
The Grants' long-term study of medium ground finches (Geospiza fortis) on Daphne Major demonstrated all three prerequisites for evolution by natural selection: variation in beak depth, heritability of that variation (high narrow-sense heritability estimates), and differential survival during the 1977 drought (big-beaked birds could crack tough Tribulus seeds). Because the trait was heritable, the post-drought population had larger beaks - evolution by natural selection. Follow-up years showed that the direction of selection can reverse when environmental conditions change (e.g., wet El Nino years favored smaller-beaked birds that could handle small soft seeds). NOTE: This is a fill-in-the-blank concept question. The answer key does not provide explicit answers; these are inferred from the textbook chapter content.

Q012
p0Key Concepts 8.2 Mice in Black and WhiteKey Concepts (Fill in the Blank)
✓ Answer: (1) (natural) selection / predation
Hopi Hoekstra's work on oldfield mice (Peromyscus polionotus) in beach habitats of Florida showed parallel evolution: different coastal populations independently evolved light fur for crypsis against pale sand. Importantly, Atlantic Coast light fur is caused by mutations in the Agouti gene while Gulf Coast light fur involves a mutation in the Mc1r gene. Same phenotype, different genetic solutions - a striking example of convergent phenotypic evolution with distinct genetic mechanisms. Q013
p0Key Concepts 8.3 The Geography of FitnessKey Concepts (Fill in the Blank)
✓ Answer: (1) geographic variation (variation / divergence)
When different populations inhabit different selective environments, they can diverge phenotypically and genetically (geographic variation). The scarlet kingsnake mimicry cline (strong mimicry where coral snakes occur, breakdown where coral snakes are absent) exemplifies fitness geography. This is a spatial analogue of the temporal shifts documented in the Grants' finch work. NOTE: Fill-in-the-blank answers inferred from chapter.

Q014
p0Key Concepts 8.4 Predators versus ParasitoidsKey Concepts (Fill in the Blank)
✓ Answer: (1) opposing directions (opposition / conflict)
In the Eurosta solidaginis gall fly system (Weis and Abrahamson), downy woodpeckers prey preferentially on large galls while parasitoid wasps attack flies in small galls. These opposing selection pressures produce stabilizing selection: an intermediate gall diameter (~20 mm) has the highest fitness. This is a classic empirical example of opposing selection agents generating a stable phenotypic optimum. NOTE: Fill-in-the-blank answers inferred from chapter.
📷 Show original page
Q015
p0Key Concepts 8.5 Replicated Natural ExperimentsKey Concepts (Fill in the Blank)
✓ Answer: (1) independently
Threespine sticklebacks (Gasterosteus aculeatus) provide a textbook example of replicated natural experiments: marine sticklebacks colonized many freshwater lakes independently after the last glaciation, and in each lake similar reductions in body armor evolved. Because these replicates share the initial conditions (marine ancestor) and the new selective environment (freshwater lake), scientists can distinguish the signal of natural selection from the noise of drift. NOTE: Fill-in-the-blank answers inferred from chapter.

Q016
p0Key Concepts 8.6 Drinking Milk: A Fingerprint of Natural SelectionKey Concepts (Fill in the Blank)
✓ Answer: (1) fitness
Lactase persistence alleles (upstream of the LCT gene) illustrate a rapid recent human sweep. In dairying populations, carriers retained high fitness after weaning by exploiting milk as a nutrient source. The sweep is detectable as extended haplotype homozygosity around the LCT gene - a genomic signature of strong recent positive selection. Lactase persistence alleles independently arose in European and African pastoralists (another example of parallel evolution). NOTE: Fill-in-the-blank answers inferred from chapter.
📷 Show original page
Q017
p0Key Concepts 8.7 Humans as Agents of SelectionKey Concepts (Fill in the Blank)
✓ Answer: (1) variation (heritable variation / genetic variation)
Humans impose some of the strongest selection pressures observed in nature through agriculture (pesticides, herbicides), medicine (antibiotics), fisheries, and trophy hunting. Two factors determine the rate of evolutionary response: (1) standing genetic variation plus mutational input, and (2) the strength/consistency of selection. Trophy hunting for the largest bighorn rams, for example, imposes directional selection against large horns and has been shown to reduce mean horn length within decades. NOTE: Fill-in-the-blank answers inferred from chapter.

Q018
p0Interpret the DataInterpret the Data
✓ Answer: Label East Africa (near the modern region of Sudan/Ethiopia/Kenya) and Northern Europe (Scandinavia/Britain/Baltic region) on the map. These are the two regions where lactase persistence reaches the highest allele frequencies and where archaeological/genetic evidence supports independent domestication of cattle and emergence of dairying cultures.
Cattle were domesticated independently at least twice (aurochs in Southwest Asia/Near East giving rise to taurine cattle, and in the Indian subcontinent giving rise to zebu). The lactase persistence phenotype, however, shows strong concordance with pastoralist cultures in Northern Europe (primary -13910*T allele) and East Africa (multiple independent alleles: -14010*C, -13915*G, -13907*G). These parallel sweeps are among the strongest signals of recent positive selection in the human genome. NOTE: This is an exercise task; specific map labeling answer not given in answer key.

Q020
p0Interpret the DataInterpret the Data
✓ Answer: Humans with the alleles of the lactase gene, LCT, that conferred lactase persistence migrated from Africa (or Europe) to Pakistan bringing cattle and a cultural shift to drinking their milk.
Gene flow plus a dairying culture is the likely explanation. Q021
p0Interpret the DataInterpret the Data
✓ Answer: A balance between strong selection in regions with a history of domestication, on the one hand, and gene flow, on the other.
Wherever cattle were domesticated and milk became a staple (Northern Europe, East Africa, parts of the Middle East/South Asia), strong positive selection drove lactase persistence alleles to high frequency. Gene flow (migration, admixture) spread those alleles into neighboring regions but diluted their frequency with non-dairying populations. The global pattern reflects a gradient shaped by both selection intensity (tied to dairying history) and gene flow across populations.

Q024
p0Games and Exercises - Natural Selection (PhET simulation)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: Open-ended / simulation-dependent. In the default green-grass environment, white bunnies are conspicuous and wolves quickly eliminate them. Extinction usually occurs within a few generations (often 2-5 generations) depending on starting population size and stochastic outcomes.
The green-grass environment makes white fur a conspicuous (non-cryptic) trait. Wolves preferentially find and eat white bunnies, so the white allele goes rapidly toward fixation at 0. The exercise demonstrates directional selection driven by a visually hunting predator.
📷 Show original page
Q025
p0Games and Exercises - Natural Selection (PhET simulation)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: White bunnies are conspicuous against the default green background. They lack crypsis (camouflage), so wolves can see and catch them more easily than a brown-furred bunny that would blend in with the grass/earth.
Selection by visually-hunting predators favors cryptic coloration. When the environment is green (grass), white fur is maladaptive. This parallels the empirical oldfield mouse example from section 8.2: in beach environments light fur is favored, while in inland environments darker fur is favored.
📷 Show original page
Q026
p0Games and Exercises - Natural Selection (PhET simulation)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: Expected result: Population count depends on run. The proportion of brown bunnies will be small initially (brown is recessive, so only homozygous recessive individuals actually display brown fur while many individuals will be carriers). With wolves active in the green environment, white bunnies are preferentially eaten. The brown allele will steadily increase in frequency because brown individuals are cryptic and survive. If the simulation continues, the brown phenotype should come to dominate the population (directional selection against white).
Recessive brown fur means only bb individuals express brown (Bb and BB display white). So brown is rare at first but confers high fitness against wolves. Over many generations the brown (b) allele rises in frequency; if selection is strong the population can approach fixation for brown. This simulation mirrors the classic peppered moth (Biston betularia) dynamic.
📷 Show original page
Q027
p0Games and Exercises - Natural Selection (PhET simulation)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: Expected result: When the environment flips to arctic (white/snow), the selective pressure reverses direction. Now brown bunnies are conspicuous and white ones are cryptic. Wolves preferentially eat brown bunnies. The brown proportion should decline over successive generations, and white allele frequency will rise again. If the simulation continues long enough, the population should approach fixation for white.
This exercise drives home the key message that "the fittest trait depends on the environment." The same brown allele was highly beneficial in green grass but becomes maladaptive in snow. Direction of selection can flip when environments change - exactly the pattern seen empirically in Galapagos finches between drought years and El Nino years.
📷 Show original page
Q028
p0Games and Exercises - Natural Selection (PhET simulation)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: Prediction: If brown is dominant instead of recessive, the brown phenotype will appear in heterozygotes (Bb) AND homozygotes (BB). Selection can then "see" and favor the brown allele immediately - there's no hidden recessive phase. Consequently, in a green environment the brown phenotype should spread through the population much faster than when it was recessive.
Dominance affects the speed of allele frequency change under selection. When a favored allele is recessive, it can be hidden in heterozygotes and thus shielded from positive selection when rare (Haldane's sieve). When the favored allele is dominant, even rare carriers express the favored phenotype, so selection is more efficient and fixation is faster.
📷 Show original page
Q029
p0Games and Exercises - Natural Selection (PhET simulation)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: Coat color in snowshoe hares (and in oldfield mice, rabbits, many rodents) is typically governed by a small number of major-effect loci - notably the Agouti signaling protein (ASIP) gene and the melanocortin-1 receptor (Mc1r) gene. These genes control the switch between eumelanin (brown/black pigment) and pheomelanin (yellow/red pigment) production in melanocytes, or modify the length of each pigment band along the hair shaft.
The Fontanesi et al. (2010) rabbit ASIP paper cited in the study guide identifies ASIP mutations as the genetic basis for the nonagouti black coat color in domestic rabbits. In wild populations, loss-of-function or regulatory mutations at ASIP or Mc1r can dramatically alter coat color. Hoekstra's oldfield mice work (Section 8.2) implicates both of these same genes, and parallel genetic pathways are known from horses, birds, and big cats.
📷 Show original page
Q030
p0Games and Exercises - Natural Selection (PhET simulation)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: A plausible mechanism: coat-color pigmentation genes could be regulated by photoperiod (day length), temperature, or seasonal hormonal cycles (e.g., melatonin). When daylight shortens in autumn, ASIP or Mc1r expression could be upregulated/downregulated to shift new hair growth from brown (summer pheomelanin-rich) to white (reduced pigment), and reversed in spring.
This is an open-ended experimental-design question; the answer key does not supply a specific answer. Research on seasonal molt in snowshoe hares (Mills et al. 2013, 2018) has shown that photoperiod is indeed the primary cue, with climate change now producing "mismatch" between brown/white coats and snow cover - a modern selection pressure.

Q031
p0Overcoming MisconceptionsOvercoming Misconceptions (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: c. Natural selection and genetic drift are both important mechanisms of evolution.
Evolutionary theory includes multiple mechanisms. Natural selection, genetic drift, mutation, gene flow, and sexual selection all shape genetic variation within and between populations. Options a and d incorrectly anthropomorphize selection as goal-driven ("need to"); evolution has no foresight or needs. Option b is false because selection can favor simpler traits (e.g., the parasite-driven loss of genes in many obligate intracellular bacteria; the loss of legs in whale ancestors; loss of armor in freshwater sticklebacks).

Q032
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer: Akey and colleagues asked whether they could use genome-wide single nucleotide polymorphism (SNP) data to identify the regions of the dog genome that have been targeted by artificial selection during dog breed formation, and thereby pinpoint the genetic basis of breed-specific phenotypes such as body size, coat color, and behavior.
Their approach used patterns of elevated genetic differentiation (e.g., FST) and reduced within-breed heterozygosity as signatures of selective sweeps. These footprints highlight the specific loci that breeders - consciously or not - targeted when producing modern dog breeds. NOTE: This is an open-ended primary-literature question; the answer key does not provide a specific solution.
📷 Show original page
Q033
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer: Regulatory changes are less likely to be pleiotropic (affecting many traits at once) than coding changes that alter protein function. Mutations that destroy or drastically alter a protein tend to break multiple downstream processes and produce unhealthy animals. Regulatory changes - tuning where, when, or how strongly a gene is expressed - can produce phenotypic change without destroying the protein's essential function, which makes them the preferred substrate for rapid adaptive evolution, especially under strong artificial selection where breeders select for one trait at a time.
This is a general evolutionary principle (King and Wilson 1975; Carroll 2008) and is consistent with Akey et al.'s genomic findings: many sweep regions fall in non-coding DNA near genes, suggesting cis-regulatory change is a major route to breed-specific phenotypes in dogs. NOTE: Answer key does not explicitly provide text for this question.
📷 Show original page
Q034
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer: Dogs and humans co-evolved alongside each other and share many genomic features. The techniques for detecting selective sweeps (reduced heterozygosity, elevated differentiation, haplotype length tests) used in dogs apply equally to humans. Dog breeds, because they underwent intense recent selection for specific phenotypes, serve as a natural amplifier: signals are stronger and easier to detect than in humans. Lessons learned in dogs (e.g., which tests work well, how to correct for demography) can be ported to searches for loci under selection in humans, including loci affecting disease resistance, diet (e.g., LCT, AMY1), or pigmentation.
Dogs also share many genetic diseases with humans (cancers, hip dysplasia, epilepsy), so mapping breed-specific variants informs medical genetics beyond evolutionary biology. NOTE: Answer key does not explicitly provide text for this question.

p0Delve DeeperDelve Deeper (Short Answer)
✓ Answer: Scientists used at least three distinct lines of evidence to reconstruct stickleback history. Some of the evidence points to the necessary conditions for evolution by natural selection, and other evidence supports the evolution of the lineage over time.
The convergence of ecological, genetic, and paleontological data makes sticklebacks one of the best-characterized cases of natural selection in the wild. The ectodysplasin (Eda) locus is a large-effect QTL that explains most of the variation in lateral-plate number between marine and freshwater populations.

Q037
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. Because light-colored mice on the Atlantic Coast lacked the alleles of Mc1r that produced light coat color on the Gulf Coast
Different populations of oldfield mice along the Atlantic and Gulf coasts each evolved pale fur, but the underlying genetic mechanisms differ. Gulf Coast mice have derived Mc1r alleles, while Atlantic Coast mice evolved light fur via Agouti gene changes. Had light fur arisen once and spread, both populations should share the same causative mutations. Their distinct genetic solutions prove independent (parallel) evolution.
📷 Show original page
Q038
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: e. All of the above
Gene flow moves alleles across population boundaries, which: (a) ties populations together genetically, preventing complete isolation; (b) changes local allele frequencies by adding/removing alleles; (c) interacts with genetic drift by counteracting random loss of variation in small populations (migration-drift balance); and (d) homogenizes allele frequencies across habitats, potentially reducing between-habitat divergence. All are real effects documented in the chapter and in population genetics theory.
📷 Show original page
Q039
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. Between 80-150 km outside of the border
Harper and Pfennig's clay-replica experiments found that mimicry fidelity still had positive fitness advantages beyond the edge of coral snake range - but only up to a point. Beyond roughly 80-150 km past the range edge, mimicry no longer conferred a survival benefit because local predators had no experience with coral snakes and therefore did not generalize their avoidance to the mimic. This shows a geographic gradient of selection on mimicry.
📷 Show original page
Q040
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: a. They compared the mean gall size of different females' offspring while controlling for genetic differences among the plants.
By rearing offspring of different fly mothers on host plants of known (controlled) genotype and comparing mean gall diameter among sibships, Weis and Abrahamson could separate the fly-genetic contribution from the plant-genetic contribution. Because daughters' galls resembled their mothers' galls more than random - after controlling for plant genotype - gall size in Eurosta has a real additive genetic (narrow-sense) heritability component. The chapter does not describe them making distinct fixed-size lineages or mapping QTL.
📷 Show original page
Q041
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. Only flies in galls that are approximately 20 mm survive to reproduce.
The gall fly system produces stabilizing selection: ~20 mm is the optimal gall diameter, but many galls of intermediate size (not just exactly 20 mm) allow survival and reproduction - fitness is a continuous curve peaking near the optimum, not a strict threshold. Stabilizing selection reduces variance but it does not make extreme sizes impossible. Statements a, b, c, and e are all supported by the chapter (optimal ~20 mm; woodpeckers hit large galls; parasitoid wasps hit small galls driving directional selection upward; wasp detection ability varies among species/individuals).
📷 Show original page
Q042
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. Because populations in the lakes are isolated from each other, and each population experienced natural selection independently of other populations
Lake sticklebacks are a natural replicated experiment. After glaciers receded, marine sticklebacks independently colonized many newly formed freshwater lakes. Because each lake population is isolated from the others but faces similar selective pressures (freshwater environment, different predator regime), convergent evolution of reduced armor across multiple independent lake populations provides strong evidence that natural selection - not drift, not shared ancestry alone - drove the change.
📷 Show original page
Q043
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: e. All of the above
(a) The low-Eda allele is ancient, dating back to at least 2 million years ago in marine sticklebacks. (b) Because low-Eda is recessive, heterozygotes in saltwater express the dominant full-armor phenotype, and the low allele can persist at low frequency without being eliminated by selection. (c) The allele is favored in freshwater (where predator regime and ion availability differ) but disfavored in saltwater (where armor is protective against marine predators). (d) Two copies (low/low homozygotes) in freshwater produce reduced armor with rapid growth - a fitness advantage. All four statements are true; the answer is "all of the above."
📷 Show original page
Q044
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: a. Associations among alleles that result because loci are physically close to each other on a chromosome
Genetic linkage is purely a chromosomal-proximity phenomenon. Because recombination (crossing over) happens only at a limited number of sites per meiosis, two loci that are physically close together are rarely separated between generations, so alleles at those loci are inherited as a block. Option b describes the genetic code (codon-amino acid mapping), option c describes synapsis of homologous chromosomes, and option d describes regulatory (trans/cis) relationships among genes - none of these are linkage.
📷 Show original page
Q045
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: c. Because cane toad predators were likely larger and more resistant in their native region
In native South American habitats, cane toads (Rhinella marina) face large, toxin-resistant predators; being large can deter or survive encounters with such predators. In invasive Australian habitats, most native predators are small and toxin-naive - a large toad full of bufotoxins kills its predator rather than merely surviving the encounter. Smaller toads carry less toxin, so naive predators that sample small toads may survive and learn avoidance (or die outright removing the predator). Selection regimes can thus invert with novel predator communities. (Option a is a post-hoc observation, not a mechanism; b only describes raw material; d and e are incorrect.)
📷 Show original page
Q046
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. Horn length in bighorn populations has been reduced by 20 cm.
Coltman et al.'s classic study of Ram Mountain bighorn sheep (referenced in Figure 8.28) documented a ~20 cm decline in mean horn length of harvested rams across roughly 30 years of intense trophy hunting. Because horn length is heritable and hunting regulations selectively removed the largest-horned rams before they could reproduce, the population evolved smaller horns - an unintended consequence of size-biased human harvest. This is a classic example of humans as agents of directional selection against a trait they value.
📷 Show original page
Q047
p0ContemplateContemplate (Open Reflection)
✓ Answer: Fashion satisfies analogues of the three conditions loosely but not literally. (1) Variation: fashion trends clearly vary among people, designers, regions. (2) "Heritability"/transmission: trends are culturally transmitted via social networks, media, celebrities - this is cultural inheritance (memetic), not genetic, but the transmission principle is present. (3) Differential success: some trends "survive" (get adopted widely) while others die out based on perceived desirability, social signaling, cost, etc.
Open-ended reflective question; no single correct answer. The prompt encourages students to apply the three-condition framework (variation, heritability, differential fitness) to a non-biological system and notice both the analogy and its limits.

Ch 09 — The History in Our Genes

32 questions
Q001
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: e. Morphogen
A morphogen is a signaling molecule that acts to alter expression of target genes - that is, it influences the expression of DNA, but it does not alter DNA. Incorrect options (all ARE mutations that can alter DNA of future generations if they occur in the germ line - see Section 5.2): - Chromosome fusion: two chromosomes joined together as one. - Deletion: segments of DNA deleted accidentally, leading to the disappearance of a small portion of a gene or an entire set of genes. - Insertion: a segment of DNA (as short as a single base or as long as thousands of bases, including entire genes) inserted into the middle of an existing sequence. - Point mutation (substitution): a single base changes from one nucleotide to another.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: b. It depends on the clade of interest within the phylogeny.
Within a phylogeny, homologous traits can be considered those inherited from a common ancestor, or those that are derived traits shared among members of a clade. Whether the trait in question is ancestral or derived depends on the area of interest. For example, the tetrapod limb can be considered an ancestral homology when looking at the evolution of mammals, but derived when looking at the classification of vertebrates (see Figure 4.22). Section 2.3 introduces homology, and Chapter 4 explains how homologies function in phylogenies. Note: options (a) and (c) are "correct, but so are other answers" - each captures part of the truth (homologous traits ARE inherited from a common ancestor; they CAN be used to define clades), but whether they are ancestral or derived depends on the clade of interest. Option (d) is incorrect because homologous traits can indeed be ancestral or derived depending on context.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. Synapomorphies are important when developing phylogenies because they are the shared derived traits that can distinguish groups of species, populations, or genes from other groups sharing different characters.
A synapomorphy is a trait that evolved in the immediate common ancestor of a clade and was inherited by all the descendants. Because the ancestor of such a group does not possess the trait, a synapomorphy can be very useful in identifying the historical relationships within the phylogeny (see Section 4.3 for how to use synapomorphies for developing a phylogeny of carnivores). Incorrect options: a. While all members of a clade share the synapomorphy, because the ancestor does NOT possess it, this trait is highly useful - not useless. b. Homoplasy (not synapomorphy) is similarity of traits not due to common ancestry; convergent evolution/reversal produce homoplasies, not synapomorphies. c. Confuses synapomorphy (shared derived trait) with synapsid (the lineage of tetrapods that gave rise to mammals).

Q007
p0Link Concepts (Concept Map)Concept Map
✓ Answer:
The concept map shows how different types of substitutions in DNA (synonymous vs. replacement) correspond to different evolutionary processes (neutral evolution, purifying selection, positive selection). Neutral substitutions accumulate at a relatively constant rate, forming a molecular clock that allows scientists to date branching events on a molecular phylogeny, which is itself built by tracing alleles back to their coalescent points.
📷 Show original page
Q008
p0Interpret the DataShort Answer
✓ Answer: Humans from East Asia.
On Tishkoff et al.'s (2009) mitochondrial DNA phylogeny, the Americas branch is sister to the East Asia branch, meaning the two groups share their most recent common ancestor. This is consistent with the prevailing model that the Americas were colonized by people who migrated from northeast Asia across Beringia.
📷 Show original page
Q009
p0Interpret the DataShort Answer
✓ Answer: Because those groups may have started with just a few individuals as small numbers migrated to new locations. The influence of genetic drift is greater in small populations than in large populations, so different initial population sizes should leave different genetic signatures.
Genetic drift (random change in allele frequencies from generation to generation) has a much stronger effect in small populations. When a small subset of a population migrates and founds a new population (the "founder effect"), allele frequencies can shift dramatically from the source population. Groups that migrated far from Africa (to the Americas and Oceania), as well as isolated hunter-gatherer populations, typically passed through such population bottlenecks, producing longer branch lengths in mitochondrial phylogenies relative to the larger, more continuously interbreeding African populations.
📷 Show original page
Q010
p0Games and Exercises - More Fun with PhylogeniesExercise / Data Analysis
✓ Answer:
The cytochrome c data illustrate the molecular-clock principle: more closely related species share more similar amino acid sequences because fewer mutations have had time to accumulate since their last common ancestor. Fungi (yeast) and plants (wheat) share the fewest amino acids with animals and are the most distant lineages on this tree.
📷 Show original page
Q011
p0Games and Exercises - More Fun with PhylogeniesShort Answer
✓ Answer:
This is the core logic of molecular phylogenetics and the molecular clock. Under neutral evolution, synonymous/neutral mutations accumulate at a roughly constant rate per unit time. Therefore, the degree of sequence divergence between two species is approximately proportional to the time since their last common ancestor. Cytochrome c is also under strong purifying selection because its function is essential, so most variation observed is neutral or near-neutral - reinforcing its usefulness as a molecular clock across deep evolutionary time.
📷 Show original page
Q012
p0Games and Exercises - More Fun with PhylogeniesExercise / Data Analysis
✓ Answer:
Humans, as mammals, are closest to other mammals (whale: 10, donkey: 11, horse: 12). Birds (chicken/penguin) are slightly more distant (13 each), snake (reptile) further (14), moth (insect) further still (29), and yeast and wheat, representing fungi and plants, are the most divergent (44 each). This matches the accepted tree of life.
📷 Show original page
Q013
p0Games and Exercises - More Fun with PhylogeniesShort Answer
✓ Answer:
Confidence in a phylogeny is typically quantified using bootstrap values, Bayesian posterior probabilities, or likelihood ratios. A tree built from a single gene (and only ~100 amino acids) carries much less statistical support than one built from many genes or whole genomes. Scientists cross-check molecular trees against morphology, the fossil record, and trees built from different genes to increase confidence.
📷 Show original page
Q014
p0Games and Exercises - More Fun with PhylogeniesShort Answer
✓ Answer:
A polytomy on a phylogenetic tree indicates either (a) a true near-simultaneous radiation (a "hard" polytomy), or more commonly (b) insufficient data to resolve the branching order (a "soft" polytomy). Adding more genes, more sites, or more taxa usually resolves soft polytomies.
📷 Show original page
Q015
p0Games and Exercises - More Fun with PhylogeniesShort Answer
✓ Answer:
A well-supported phylogeny is built by triangulating independent sources of evidence. Modern phylogenetic studies routinely combine hundreds or thousands of genes (phylogenomics), calibrated with fossils and tested statistically (bootstrap, posterior probabilities), to arrive at robust relationships.
📷 Show original page
Q016
p0Overcoming MisconceptionsMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: a. The coalescent represents the lineage of an allele that arose and ultimately persisted within a population.
The coalescent is the point in the past at which all copies of an allele in a population trace back to a single ancestral allele - i.e., the lineage of an allele that arose (often by mutation) and eventually became fixed in the population. It is NOT "the individual" who first carried the allele (a common misconception applied to "mitochondrial Eve"). Incorrect options: b. Many mutations are neutral (e.g., synonymous substitutions) and some are beneficial; not all are deleterious. c. A straight branch simply shows a lineage - mutations accumulate along every branch (neutral, beneficial, or purified away); length is determined by the tree style, not by lack of change. d. The placement of taxa depends on the style of the tree. Extant (living) species can be at the top, bottom, right, or on the outside of a radial tree; older nodes are found in the opposite direction (bottom/top/left/center).
📷 Show original page
Q017
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer (primary literature - Enard et al. 2002, FOXP2)
✓ Answer:
These two amino-acid replacement substitutions in the human FOXP2 protein (relative to the shared great-ape FOXP2) are a striking observation: two nonsynonymous changes with zero synonymous changes is extremely unusual under neutral evolution (where synonymous changes are expected to be much more common). This pattern - an elevated ratio of replacement to synonymous substitutions - is a classical signature of positive selection, and it is one reason FOXP2 has been implicated in the evolution of human speech and language.
📷 Show original page
Q018
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer (primary literature)
✓ Answer:
A KA/KS ratio > 1 (or an excess of replacement substitutions) on a specific lineage, combined with reduced within-species variation, is strong evidence that positive selection - not neutral drift or purifying selection - has acted on the gene in that lineage. The timing coincides with the emergence of modern human language abilities.
📷 Show original page
Q019
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer (primary literature)
✓ Answer:
Molecular-clock methods estimate divergence times by assuming mutations accumulate at an approximately steady rate along a lineage. The rate is calibrated by anchoring one or more nodes on the tree to a fossil date of known age; remaining nodes are then inferred by rescaling the number of substitutions per unit time. More sophisticated variants (relaxed molecular clocks, Bayesian divergence-time estimation) allow rates to vary among branches, which Enard et al.'s analysis accommodates.
📷 Show original page
Q020
p0Delve DeeperShort Answer
✓ Answer: Yes. Scientists often check their results against different lines of evidence, such as the fossil record and morphological evidence. Molecular data can be used to develop phylogenetic hypotheses and tested with other lines of evidence, or molecular data can be used to support and/or test phylogenetic hypotheses developed with evidence from the fossil record or morphological data.
Independent lines of evidence strengthen phylogenetic inference. Fossils provide direct (if imperfect) snapshots of past organisms and minimum dates for clade divergences; morphology provides characters (often with developmental or functional constraints) that can be compared across living and extinct taxa; molecular data provide large numbers of independently evolving characters. When all lines converge on the same tree, confidence is high; when they conflict, scientists investigate the source of conflict (e.g., convergent morphology, incomplete lineage sorting in genes, incomplete fossils).
📷 Show original page
Q021
p0Delve DeeperShort Answer
✓ Answer: Scientists can look for evidence of different kinds of selection in the past by comparing the ratios of synonymous to replacement substitutions within coding regions. The probability of a substitution at a synonymous site should be equal to the probability of a substitution at a replacement site. When they are not equal, scientists can reject the hypothesis of neutral evolution. Then they can look at the patterns of the ratios of synonymous to replacement substitutions. For example, strong positive selection on a gene can lead to unusually large numbers of replacement substitutions that change the structure of proteins, whereas purifying selection can eliminate replacement mutations to genes with essential functions that are easily disrupted by mutations, leading to a very low number of replacement substitutions compared to synonymous ones. For example, the FOXP2 gene has changed very little in our common ancestors, but in humans, two amino acids have changed in the protein in the past 6 million years and may contribute to our human-ness.
The KA/KS (dN/dS) ratio is the standard test: - KA/KS ~ 1: neutral evolution (no selection distinguishing the two site classes). - KA/KS < 1: purifying (stabilizing) selection removing deleterious replacement changes (most essential genes, e.g., cytochrome c, histones). - KA/KS > 1: positive (directional) selection favoring amino-acid changes (e.g., FOXP2 on the human lineage, immune system genes under pathogen pressure). This approach allows biologists to detect episodes of selection that occurred millions of years ago using only DNA sequences from living species.
📷 Show original page
Q022
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. All of the above (answer key: "1 d")
Coalescence refers to all three descriptions combined: it is the PROCESS of tracing homologous alleles back through generations to a common ancestor (a), which collectively constitutes the HISTORY of an allele within a population (b), and marks the POINT at which a locus originated and could begin to vary/become polymorphic (c). All describe different facets of the same concept of genealogical convergence of alleles back to a single ancestral copy.

Q023
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. Both b and c (answer key: "2 d")
A gene tree is a branched genealogical diagram showing the genealogy of homologous alleles (b) and it is literally a graphical depiction of how those alleles coalesce back to common ancestors (c). Option (a) is too narrow/misleading - a gene tree traces the history of an ALLELE, not necessarily of a species (gene trees and species trees can disagree due to incomplete lineage sorting, introgression, etc.), so "all of the above" (e) is not correct.
📷 Show original page
Q024
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: c. Because species 2 and species 3 share a more recent common ancestor than either shares with species 1 in terms of that single gene's alleles (answer key: "3 c")
Relatedness on a gene tree is determined entirely by the recency of the most recent common ancestor of the alleles sampled. For a single gene, if the alleles of species 2 and species 3 coalesce more recently with each other than either does with species 1's allele, then - FOR THAT GENE - species 2 and 3 appear more closely related. Option (e) is similar in spirit but lacks the crucial qualifier "in terms of that single gene's alleles" - the figure highlights that a single gene tree may not match the species tree (the phenomenon of gene-tree/species-tree discordance), so the answer must be explicitly about the gene's alleles, not about the species overall.
📷 Show original page
Q025
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. Both a and b (answer key: "4 d")
Both maximum-likelihood (ML) and Bayesian phylogenetic methods explicitly use statistical models of sequence evolution that can incorporate rate variation among sites and among loci (e.g., gamma-distributed rates, partitioned models, relaxed molecular clocks). Bootstrapping (c) is a resampling procedure used to assess statistical support for branches on a tree - it is NOT itself a method of tree building and does not model mutation-rate variation; rather, it evaluates how robust a tree is to subsampling of sites.
📷 Show original page
Q026
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: e. Because scientists use it to provide a test of the likelihood of the branching events they've proposed (answer key: "5 e")
Bootstrapping is a statistical resampling procedure: sites in the original alignment are resampled with replacement (many thousands of times), a tree is built for each replicate, and the proportion of replicate trees supporting each branch is reported as the bootstrap value. This quantifies the statistical support for each node - essentially a measure of how robust (likely) each proposed branching event is, given the data. It does NOT identify the "true" tree (a), does not improve the underlying data (b), and is not a substitute for being uncertain in general (c); nor is it a remedy for inaccuracy of other methods (d).
📷 Show original page

✓ Answer:
Language evolution shares many formal properties with genetic evolution: descent with modification, branching into isolated lineages, and ways of quantifying divergence. The analogy is not perfect - horizontal borrowing between languages is much more common and faster than horizontal gene transfer between animal species - but the phylogenetic methods developed for genes are routinely and productively applied to linguistic data.

Ch 10 — Adaptation: From Genes to Traits

20 questions
Q001
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. All of the above
Many traits are polygenic—they are influenced by many genetic loci. Individuals can vary because they have different alleles for different genes, and because these alleles occur in different combinations in different individuals, resulting in diverse epistatic effects from individual to individual (Section 7.1). Phenotypic plasticity occurs when alleles interact with the environment in ways that affect the expression of a phenotype, and these genotype x environment interactions can contribute to variation as well (Section 5.5). Finally, a single mutation can affect many phenotypic traits if, for example, the mutation occurs in a regulatory gene (Section 6.6). All three statements (a, b, and c) are correct.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: a. Additive effects
An additive allele will have twice the effect on a phenotype when two copies are present at a given locus than when only a single copy is present. Additive alleles are not influenced by the presence of other alleles, so their effects are not context (genotype) dependent the way dominant and epistatic effects are (see Box 7.1). Because of this, additive effects of alleles cause relatives to resemble each other. Scientists have characterized the influence of this effect on variation among individuals by narrow-sense heritability (see Section 7.1). Dominant and epistatic effects are context-dependent and change every generation after meiosis, so they do not typically contribute to resemblance among relatives.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: e. b and c only
Cis-acting elements, also known as cis-regulatory regions, cis-regions, and cis-regulatory elements, are non-coding regions that function to regulate nearby genes. They can be found up- or downstream from the focal gene, or even inside an intron. Cis elements often code for binding sites for one or more trans-acting factors. Trans-acting elements are located away from a focal gene, even on another chromosome, and they tend to code for proteins, microRNAs, or other diffusible molecules. A mutation to either a cis-acting or a trans-acting element can influence gene expression (see Section 5.2). Choice (a) describes trans-acting (not cis-acting) elements, so "all of the above" is incorrect.
📷 Show original page
Q004
p0Identify Key TermsMatching
✓ Answer: 1. Antagonistic pleiotropy - f
- Antagonistic pleiotropy (f): a mutation has beneficial effects for one trait but detrimental effects for others. - Complex adaptations (h): suites of coexpressed traits selected together for a common function. - Deep homology (j): shared traits arising from regulatory networks inherited from a common ancestor. - Gene recruitment (i): co-option of a gene/network for a new function via mutation. - Novel traits (g): traits with no obvious homologs in related lineages. - Orthologs (b): homologous genes separated by speciation. - Parallel evolution (c): independent evolution of similar traits from a similar ancestral condition. - Paralogs (a): homologous genes arising by gene duplication (forming a gene family). - Promiscuous proteins (d): proteins that can carry out more than one function. - Regulatory networks (e): interacting circuits of genes, transcription factors, promoters, RNAs controlling gene expression.
📷 Show original page
Q005
p0Link ConceptsConcept Map / Fill in the Bubbles
✓ Answer: Completed concept map (from answer key):
Concept map shows that evolutionary novelty emerges by co-opting existing building blocks: (1) promiscuous proteins (opsins, defensins) repurposed for new functions like vision and venom, and (2) changes to cis-regulatory elements in the genetic toolkit (e.g., Hedgehog/Shh signaling) that alter where and when developmental genes are expressed, generating morphological novelties such as snake leg loss, ant wing loss, and modified cricket hind legs. For the student-generated map (constraints on eye evolution): mutations are the raw material but pleiotropy means changes to deeply shared toolkit genes (e.g., Pax6, opsins) affect many traits at once, biasing which variants are viable. Homology across lineages means vertebrate eyes build on an inherited gene network; shared ancestry constrains the developmental path available, so the vertebrate retina is "inside-out" (with a blind spot) rather than redesigned from scratch. Lineage-specific history further channels future variation.

Placopsins branched off from the opsin lineage before opsins evolved the ability to sense light. Therefore, placopsins are opsin-family proteins that are not used for vision.

Q008

Cnidarians possess receptors for R-opsins, C-opsins, and RGR/Go-opsins. Because these opsin types (which evolved after opsins gained the ability to capture photons ~650 million years ago) are present in cnidarians, cnidarians have the molecular machinery required for light sensitivity.
📷 Show original page
Q009
p0Interpret the DataShort Answer
✓ Answer: Duplication of the opsin gene.
Gene duplication was the key mutation. It was important because the original copy of the gene could still function to produce receptors for its ancestral role, while the duplicated copy was freed from that selective constraint and could accumulate mutations leading to new functions—such as the ability to capture photons and, eventually, to produce the diverse opsin types (R-opsins, C-opsins, RGR/Go-opsins) used in animal vision.
📷 Show original page
Q010
p0Overcoming MisconceptionsMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: b. Through natural selection and time, evolution can produce seemingly complex adaptations through gene duplication and recruitment.
(a) is false: with a few exceptions, all diploid nucleated cells of an organism carry the same DNA and therefore the same genes—cell types differ because different genes are differentially regulated, not because different cells carry different genes. (b) is true: many lines of evidence (physiology, chemistry, genetics, phylogenetics) show that mutations, gene duplication, and gene recruitment combine to produce complex structures like eyes. Each incremental gain in functionality can be favored by natural selection. (c) is false: scientists do debate evidence and argue vigorously about topics like the construction of evolutionary trees, causes of mass extinctions, and the relative importance of selection vs. neutral evolution. Science is a process of creativity and argument—not faith. (d) is false: natural selection is not a random process. While mutations are random, selection non-randomly retains those with beneficial effects, allowing series of mutations to give rise to complex traits.
📷 Show original page
Q011
p0Delve DeeperShort Answer / Table
✓ Answer: Where are they located?
Hox genes are master regulatory genes in the developmental "genetic toolkit." They are located on chromosomes in every cell of the body, but they function contextually within regulatory networks. Mutations can reshape the developmental flow chart, producing either catastrophic failures or novel traits depending on where in the hierarchy they act.
📷 Show original page
Q012
p0Delve DeeperShort Answer
✓ Answer: Because every cell does not respond exactly the same to every regulatory network.
A cell's fate depends on its context. For example, a cell may respond differently because of its location in the body of the organism—a diffusible signal, such as a morphogen, may be more or less concentrated depending on how close the signal source is to the cell. Alternatively, the genes in a cell may respond differently depending on the amount of other gene products that are present as the result of other regulatory networks. So although every cell potentially has the capacity to develop into an eye cell, cells will only do so if ALL of the other elements in the regulatory network are functioning appropriately to produce an eye cell. This is why differential gene expression (not differential DNA content) is what distinguishes cell types.
📷 Show original page
Q013
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: e. All of the above
All four statements describe important aspects of why gene networks matter for trait evolution. Gene networks underlie quantitative traits (a); a single mutation within a network can cascade to affect many downstream genes and produce novel traits rapidly (b); networks behave as modules that can be co-opted into new developmental contexts to generate novelty (c); and mutations to upstream regulators can truncate an entire pathway with dramatic phenotypic consequences (d). Together, these properties make regulatory networks powerful engines for evolutionary innovation.

Octopus and human eyes are both camera-type eyes with a single lens and retina—they are structurally much more similar to each other than either is to the compound eye of an insect. The octopus eye is a famous example of convergent evolution with the vertebrate eye. Meanwhile, (a) is supported (jellyfish and octopus camera-type eyes are more similar to each other than to insect compound eyes), (c) is supported (crystallins and opsins trace back to shared ancestral proteins across animal lineages via deep homology), and (d) is supported (complex eyes arose independently in several animal lineages).

Q017
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. Yes. Scientists have evidence from phylogenies based on shared derived morphological structures as well as gene sequences and entire genomes.
Multiple independent lines of evidence converge on a robust picture of early eye evolution. Phylogenetic trees built from morphology, gene sequences (e.g., opsins, Pax6), and whole-genome data all corroborate each other, showing how gene duplication and co-option of ancestral G-protein-coupled receptors produced the opsin family and how patterning genes were recruited for eye development. Q018
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: c. They compared the sizes of insects in the fossil record when oxygen concentrations were higher to when oxygen concentrations were lower.
Scientists tested the oxygen-constraint hypothesis by looking at the historical correlation between atmospheric oxygen and insect body size in the fossil record. During periods of elevated atmospheric O2 (such as the Carboniferous), insects attained very large body sizes (e.g., giant dragonflies); when oxygen levels declined, maximum insect body size also declined. Because insects rely on passive diffusion through tracheal tubes, their maximum size is physiologically limited by O2 availability, and this pattern in the fossil record supports the hypothesis.
📷 Show original page
Q019
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: c. No. Because the human eye evolved through modifications of ancestral eyes, the optic nerve leaves the retina through the back of the eye creating a blind spot.
The vertebrate eye is not perfectly designed—it is a jury-rigged product of history. Because it evolved by modifying ancestral structures, the retina is "inside-out": photoreceptors face away from incoming light, and axons of retinal ganglion cells must cross the inner surface of the retina and exit through a hole in it (the optic disc), creating a blind spot. Natural selection cannot start from scratch; it can only tinker with what already exists. Option (a) misinterprets compensation (the brain fills in the blind spot) as perfection, (b) incorrectly claims selection achieves perfection, and (d) uses a trivial criterion. The blind spot is a genuine structural imperfection imposed by evolutionary history. (Note: The Test Yourself answer list in the key shows "6.c" for this question.)
📷 Show original page
Q020
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. Because their phenotypes converged on similar forms and ecological functions even though they are from two different lineages of mammals
Convergent evolution refers to the independent evolution of similar PHENOTYPES in lineages that do NOT share a recent common ancestor with those traits. The gray wolf (Canis lupus) is a placental mammal, while the Tasmanian wolf / thylacine (Thylacinus cynocephalus) is a marsupial. Despite their deep divergence, both evolved a similar body plan, skull shape, and role as apex canine-like predators—a classic case of convergence driven by similar ecological roles. Option (a) is wrong because genotypes did not converge (only the phenotypes did); (b) overstates the similarity as "identical"; (c) is wrong because they do not share a recent canine common ancestor; and (e) describes parallel evolution / deep homology rather than convergence.

Ch 11 — Sex: Causes and Consequences

31 questions
Q001
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice (Review)
✓ Answer: b. Variation within a population
Heritable variation among individuals serves as a vital raw material for natural selection. Natural selection is a population-based phenomenon that requires heritable variation among individuals that confers some advantage or disadvantage. Within a population, those that do better are more likely to reproduce and have offspring that share some of the variable traits that led to that success. Over time, individuals with those successful traits become more common than those without — the essence of natural selection (see Chapter 8). Why the others are wrong: a. Individuals changing to meet their needs have no genetic basis — evolution cannot identify an individual's needs. c. Although lineages can evolve in relatively short time periods, thousands of years is not the defining requirement; rapid evolution can occur when generation times are short or selection is strong. d/e. Incorrect because only (b) is the essential element.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice (Review)
✓ Answer: b. In artificial selection, breeders create very strong selection on traits, which can result in a rapid evolutionary response if the heritability of the selected traits is high.
R = h^2 x S is called the breeder's equation. It describes the evolutionary response (R) to selection that results from the interaction of the heritability of a trait (narrow sense) and the strength of selection (S) acting on the trait. In artificial selection, breeders serve as strong selective forces, picking and choosing individuals with the traits they want to breed. The greater the heritability of the trait, the greater the response to selection (see Section 7.2). Why the others are wrong: a. Heritability remains crucial in artificial selection; the response is not simply R = S. c. Breeders cannot manipulate heritability itself — they select for traits and the heritability determines response. d. The breeder's equation applies to ANY population under selection (including natural populations), not only to artificial selection.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice (Review)
✓ Answer: d. Yes. Individuals may vary in their plastic responses to the environment, and those responses can be heritable.
Phenotypic plasticity is the capacity for a genotype to express more than one phenotype depending on the environment. Genetically identical plants can grow large leaves in high light and small leaves in low light. This reaction norm may be heritable, and it may vary among different genotypes (see Section 7.4). Why the others are wrong: a. Plasticity is not a Lamarckian "acquired characteristic" — it has a genetic (and therefore heritable) basis. b. Plasticity is not a response to "need"; organisms that happened to respond appropriately had higher fitness (e.g., seasonal color change in hares). c. Although the phenotype-genotype link is complex, it can be observed and measured — reaction norms can be heritable.
📷 Show original page
Q004
p0Identify Key TermsMatching (Terms to Definitions)
✓ Answer: 1. Anisogamy = S
Each term is paired with the definition whose content matches its technical meaning in the chapter. Key distinctions: anisogamy (S) is defined by the gamete size dimorphism that defines male/female; intrasexual selection (H) is competition WITHIN a sex (usually male-male), while intersexual selection (U) is CHOICE between sexes (female choice); sperm competition (L) is post-mating competition among sperm from different males, while cryptic female choice (C) is post-mating female bias of which sperm fertilize her eggs; Muller's ratchet (M) describes the accumulation of deleterious mutations, and genetic load (A) is the fitness burden caused by that accumulation; Red Queen effect (G) explains ongoing evolutionary arms races (e.g., host-parasite); the twofold cost of sex (F) captures why asexual populations could replicate faster than sexual ones.
📷 Show original page
Q005
p0Link ConceptsConcept Map Fill-In (Diagram)
✓ Answer:
Anisogamy (the asymmetry between tiny, abundant sperm and large, costly eggs) sets up the fundamental asymmetry of investment between the sexes. The sex that invests less per offspring (typically males) becomes available to mate again more quickly (high turnover), which biases the operational sex ratio toward that sex. Members of this overabundant sex must compete for mates. Conversely, the sex investing more per offspring (typically females) takes longer to return to the mating pool (low turnover), making them the limiting resource — and therefore they can be choosy. This is the logic that links anisogamy to intrasexual (male-male) competition and intersexual (female choice) selection.
📷 Show original page
Q006
p0Key Concepts - 11.1 Evolution of SexFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: (a) Sex is the combining and mixing of GENETIC MATERIAL (alleles/chromosomes from two parents) during the formation of offspring. It involves (1) MEIOSIS halves the number of chromosomes during gamete formation, and (2) FERTILIZATION restores the original chromosome number as two gametes fuse to form a zygote.
Sexual reproduction's two defining cellular processes are meiosis (halves ploidy) and fertilization (restores ploidy). Sex generates novel genotypes and breaks up linkage, allowing beneficial alleles to be combined and deleterious ones to be purged. The Red Queen effect is the leading hypothesis for why sex persists despite the twofold cost: parasites and pathogens rapidly adapt to common host genotypes, so shuffling alleles each generation helps hosts stay one step ahead.
📷 Show original page
Q007
p0Key Concepts - 11.2 Sexual SelectionFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: (a) ... difference between the SEXES in their relative investment in OFFSPRING (gametes/reproduction).
Anisogamy produces the asymmetry underlying sexual selection. Females, limited by egg production and offspring provisioning, maximize fitness by acquiring resources and mating with high-quality males. Males, limited by access to eggs (mates), maximize fitness by mating with many females. When the OSR is male-biased, intrasexual (male-male) competition is strong. Females can gain direct benefits (resources) or indirect benefits (good genes); they evaluate males using ornaments and courtship behavior. Variance in reproductive success sets the upper limit on how intense sexual selection can be (the opportunity for selection).
📷 Show original page
Q008
p0Key Concepts - 11.3 The Rules of AttractionFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: (a) ... pass to their offspring ALLELES (genes) influencing the expression of both the PREFERENCE (female preference) and the TRAIT (male display/ornament). The resulting genetic correlation between preference and trait can lead to an escalating POSITIVE feedback cycle of COEVOLUTION (runaway Fisherian selection) of stronger preferences and larger display traits.
This section describes Fisher's runaway model (genetic correlation between preference genes and trait genes produces a positive-feedback coevolutionary cycle) and the Zahavi handicap / honest signaling theory (costly, condition-dependent ornaments cannot be faked by low-quality males, so they reliably advertise male genetic quality or condition). Together these explain how both arbitrary and honest ornaments can evolve under female choice.
📷 Show original page
Q009
p0Key Concepts - 11.4 The Evolution of Mating SystemsFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: Mating systems evolve because of the COSTS and BENEFITS they confer on males and females. Who mates with whom can depend on the TRADE-OFFS (or PRESSURES / SELECTION PRESSURES) that arise through both inter- and intrasexual SELECTION.
Mating systems (monogamy, polygyny, polyandry, promiscuity) are themselves evolutionary outcomes shaped by the balance of costs (e.g., energy of defending a mate, risk of losing paternity) and benefits (e.g., paternity certainty, access to multiple mates, good genes). Both intrasexual (e.g., male-male competition) and intersexual (e.g., female choice) selection contribute. Some species mix strategies (e.g., social monogamy with extra-pair copulations).
📷 Show original page
Q010
p0Key Concepts - 11.5 The Hidden Dimension of Sexual SelectionFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: (a) When females mate with multiple males, SPERM from the different males must COMPETE for opportunities to fertilize the females' eggs. (This is sperm competition.)
The "hidden dimension" of sexual selection occurs AFTER copulation. When females are polyandrous, two post-copulatory mechanisms operate: (1) sperm competition (male-vs-male competition at the gamete level — e.g., large sperm, sperm plugs, larger testes) and (2) cryptic female choice (females preferentially store, transport, or use sperm from particular males). Because fertilization is not guaranteed by mating, males of many species continue investing in courtship, mate guarding, or repeated mating after copulation.
📷 Show original page
Q011
p0Key Concepts - 11.6 Sexual Conflict and Antagonistic CoevolutionFill in the Blank
✓ Answer: Sexual conflict can lead to ANTAGONISTIC COEVOLUTION and arms races between males and females. Mutations BENEFITING (favoring / advancing) the reproductive interests of males select for COUNTER-ADAPTATIONS (resistance / defenses) in the females, and vice versa.
Because males and females often have divergent optima for mating traits (e.g., number of matings, mating duration, paternal investment), mutations that benefit one sex frequently impose costs on the other. This sets up an evolutionary tug-of-war: male traits that increase male fertilization success but harm females (e.g., spiny genitalia in seed beetles, harmful seminal proteins in Drosophila) select for female counter-adaptations (thicker reproductive tract tissues, detoxification), which in turn select for further male escalation — producing ongoing antagonistic coevolution.
📷 Show original page

Reading the x-axis of either graph, the rightmost data point lies at roughly 19 cm of phallus length.
