Q015
p0Interpret the DataShort Answer (Graph Interpretation / Prediction)
✓ Answer: Yes. For a species with a mean phallus length of 9 cm, the female reproductive tract would likely have between 0 and 2 pouches, and approximately 0 to 4 spirals.
Reading x = 9 cm on both scatter plots, the data points at that position cluster at the lower end of the y-axes: pouches typically range from 0-2 and spirals from 0-4 at that phallus length. This reflects the positive (but scattered) correlation: mid-range phallus species have mid-to-low numbers of female tract structures, whereas high values occur only at the longest phalluses.
📷 Show original page
Q016
p0Interpret the DataShort Answer (Correlation vs. Causation)
✓ Answer: No. The relationships are correlations; they show a positive association between the variables (male phallus length and number of pouches or spirals in the female reproductive tract) but not that male phallus length CAUSES the female characteristics. Selection is clearly acting on both males and females, but establishing a causal relationship would require a mechanism demonstrating how the male phallus length actually made the female reproductive tract change (e.g., an experimental or developmental pathway).
The graphs plot cross-species correlations. Correlation never by itself establishes causation, and in this system, the likely explanation is antagonistic coevolution — longer male phalluses and more-elaborate female tracts coevolved as sexual conflict escalated (Brennan et al. 2007). Both sexes are under reciprocal selection; one does not cause the other in a strict mechanistic sense.
📷 Show original page
Q017
p0Overcoming MisconceptionsMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. Ornaments will evolve through sexual selection when individuals vary in a sexually selected trait, that variation is heritable, and that variation affects reproductive success.
This statement captures Darwin's three requirements for any form of selection (variation + heritability + fitness differences), applied to sexually selected ornaments. The three other statements embody common misconceptions addressed in this section: a. Evolution has no foresight and does not occur because animals "need" a change — selection operates on existing heritable variation. b. Evolution does not produce ornaments because males "need" to attract females; ornaments arise through random mutation, and those that happen to increase mating success spread. c. Sexually selected traits are often far from a "perfect fit"; they reflect trade-offs, constraints, and ongoing conflict (e.g., costs such as predation risk, sexual conflict, cross-species misdirected mating).
📷 Show original page
Q018
p0Delve DeeperShort Answer (Figure Interpretation)
✓ Answer: Because we share a common ancestor with the other primates depicted in the graph, including our genus allows us to examine hypotheses about how evolution has shaped our traits. The position of our genus (Homo) indicates that the relationship between body size and testes size is in the same range as primates that experience relatively LITTLE sperm competition.
Figure 11.27 plots primate body size vs. testes size. Primates with promiscuous mating systems (e.g., chimpanzees) have disproportionately large testes because sperm from rival males compete inside the female — intense sperm competition selects for more sperm, hence bigger testes. Primates with harem polygyny or monogamy (e.g., gorillas, gibbons) have smaller testes for their body size. Humans fall in the low-sperm-competition zone, suggesting our recent evolutionary history has not featured strong sperm competition (comparable to mildly polygynous or effectively monogamous mating systems).
📷 Show original page
Q019
p0Delve DeeperShort Answer (Conceptual)
✓ Answer: SEX is the male/female status of an individual. Females are defined as the sex producing a small number of relatively large gametes, and males are defined as the sex producing a large number of relatively small gametes. Often, the sex of an individual is determined by the chromosomes it inherits — in humans, females are XX and males are XY, but in many birds the heterogametic sex (ZW) is the female. In some turtles, sex is determined by developmental temperature. There are also species where individuals switch sex, hermaphrodites that are both sexes at once, and insects in which females are diploid while males are haploid — with sex determined by the act of reproduction itself.
These three concepts are often conflated, but each describes a distinct biological phenomenon: sex = an individual's gametic role/status; reproduction = the act of producing offspring; copulation = a specific physical behavior that may (or may not) accompany sexual reproduction.
📷 Show original page
Q020
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer:
This study is a textbook example of how sexually antagonistic traits can persist despite imposing costs on the other sex: the male-level benefit (increased paternity share) can outweigh any fitness cost to the male (or to the population), even though females suffer harm. The logic aligns with sexual conflict theory: what is adaptive for one sex can be maladaptive for the other.
📷 Show original page
Q021
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer:
By showing that longer spines predict higher paternity share (not just higher female damage), Hotzy and Arnqvist argue that sperm-competition benefit is the main selective force favoring spines — even though the harmful effect on females is a side-consequence rather than the driver. This distinguishes their finding from a pure antagonistic coevolution scenario in which the harm itself is what males are selected to inflict.
📷 Show original page
Q022
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureShort Answer (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer:
Before this study, there was debate over whether harmful male traits (like genital spines or toxic seminal fluid) were directly selected for their male benefit, or were incidental collateral damage. Hotzy and Arnqvist's quantitative paternity data strongly support the direct-benefit interpretation, reinforcing a central prediction of sexual selection and sexual conflict theory: that traits can evolve purely because they benefit one sex, regardless of costs to the partner or the population.
📷 Show original page
Q023
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: e. The number of sperm a male produces.
Darwinian fitness is measured by the number of viable, reproducing offspring an individual leaves to the next generation. Survival (b), reproductive output (a: seed set), replication rate for asexual lineages (c: bacterial cell division), and quality/quantity of grandchildren (d, which captures the transmission of genes across two generations) all directly contribute to fitness. In contrast, the raw number of sperm a male produces is NOT itself a component of fitness — it only matters insofar as it translates into fertilization success (which depends on mating opportunities, sperm competition, female choice, etc.). A male can produce enormous numbers of sperm and leave no offspring; fitness is measured at the offspring level, not the gamete level.
📷 Show original page
Q024
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: e. The frequency of the parasite genotype that can infect the host genotype tracks the frequency of the host genotype.
The Red Queen Effect describes a never-ending evolutionary arms race: as one genotype of host becomes common, parasites able to infect that genotype are favored and rise in frequency. As the infecting parasite spreads, selection favors rare host genotypes that can resist it — so the common host falls and a different host genotype rises. Parasite frequencies then track whichever host genotype is currently common. This frequency-tracking pattern is the signature pattern of Red-Queen dynamics, and it is exactly what Figure 11.5 shows. Why the others are wrong: a. Population-size fluctuations alone are not the Red Queen signature. b. Fixed periodicity (every 25 generations) is not a defining feature; the tracking emerges from reciprocal selection. c. "Tracks host populations" (abundances) is different from "tracks host genotype frequencies" — the Red Queen is about genotype frequency tracking. d. "Sex helps hosts evolve" is an implication/consequence of the Red Queen hypothesis but is not what the figure directly demonstrates.
📷 Show original page
Q025
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: a. Males do not survive at the same rate as females.
Figure 11.13 (Clutton-Brock red deer data) plots age-specific survival of male and female red deer on the Isle of Rum. Males suffer substantially higher mortality than females once they begin competing for harems — the energetic demands of rutting, the injuries from fighting, and reduced feeding during the mating season all reduce male survival. This is the direct cost of male-male combat and intrasexual selection. Why the others are wrong: b. Female red deer do not specifically choose "older" males — access to harems is decided by male-male contest. c. Female reproductive rate is not the cost to males shown in the figure. d/e. Only (a) is supported by Figure 11.13.
📷 Show original page
Q026
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: e. All of the above are sexually dimorphic.
Sexual dimorphism is defined as ANY difference in form between males and females of a species — including body size (either direction), coloration, and the presence or absence of structures used in courtship or contests (ornaments, weapons). So males being larger (e.g., elephant seals), females being larger (e.g., many spiders, some raptors), and males being more brightly colored (e.g., peacocks, mandrills) are all examples of sexual dimorphism.
📷 Show original page
Q027
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: c. Monogamy is a mating system where a male and female mate only with each other.
Monogamy (in its strict/sexual form) is defined by exclusive pairing — a male and female mate only with each other. Why the others are wrong: a. Polyandry is defined as ONE female mating with MULTIPLE males (the text defines polyandry as "a mating system where females mate (or attempt to mate) with multiple males"), so "many males mate with a single female" technically describes polyandry from the male side, but the statement as worded is imprecise — polyandry is a property of the female's mating strategy, not the inverse. b. Polygyny is ONE male mating with MANY females, not many males with many females (that would be promiscuity/polygynandry). d. Social monogamy (pair bond + cooperative rearing; allows extra-pair copulations) and sexual monogamy (true genetic exclusivity) are DIFFERENT. About 90% of bird species are socially monogamous, but genetic (sexual) monogamy is rare.
📷 Show original page
Q028
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. All of the above.
Polyandry (females mating with multiple males) can benefit females through multiple mechanisms: a. Good genes: by mating with more than one male, a female increases the chance of obtaining sperm from the highest-quality male. b. Genetic diversity / MHC-type benefits: mating with males whose genes differ from her own can yield offspring with broader immune repertoires (defense against a wider range of pathogens) and higher heterozygosity. c. Convenience polyandry: the costs of resisting courting males (time, injury, harassment) may exceed the costs of simply mating, so accepting multiple matings becomes the cheaper option. All three are legitimate, empirically supported explanations for female polyandry.
📷 Show original page
Q029
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. All of the above.
All three adaptations are documented responses to sperm competition in various species: a. Large sperm: in some species (e.g., Drosophila bifurca), sperm are extraordinarily long — a direct outcome of sperm competition for access to the egg or space in the female storage organ. b. Large sperm plugs: many mammals, insects, and reptiles (rodents, certain primates, snakes, bees) deposit mating plugs that physically block rival males from inseminating the female after mating. c. Cooperative sperm: in some rodents (e.g., Peromyscus mice) and insects, sperm adhere to one another and move as coordinated groups, increasing their speed or fertilization success when competing with sperm from other males. All of these are classic sperm-competition adaptations discussed in the chapter.
📷 Show original page
Q030
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: c. The length of spines on their intromittent organ.
This refers directly to the Hotzy and Arnqvist (2009) study on Callosobruchus maculatus seed beetles cited in the "Go the Distance" section. Males with LONGER genital spines sire a greater proportion of offspring when females mate with multiple males (higher P2, the second-male paternity share). Spine length predicts fertilization/sperm-competition success even though spines physically damage females. This is a classic example of sexual conflict where a harmful male trait is directly favored by post-copulatory sexual selection. The other options do not match the chapter's data on seed beetles as the main predictor of male fertilization success.
📷 Show original page
Q031
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: a. After 20 days, female survival rate was lower than females reared in a polygynous mating system.
Figure 11.32 presents a classic experimental-evolution sexual-conflict result (based on Rice and colleagues' Drosophila work). Males that were maintained in a polygynous (sexually-competitive) mating system evolved more effective traits for competing with rival sperm and manipulating females — including more harmful seminal-fluid proteins. Females maintained in monogamous (no-conflict) lines had evolved no counter-defenses. When these two "mismatched" populations were forced to mate, females suffered markedly reduced survival: by about 20 days their survival fell well below that of females from co-evolved polygynous lineages. This experimentally demonstrates antagonistic coevolution: male adaptations harmful to females, and matching female resistance that blunts those harms.
📷 Show original page

Ch 12 — After Conception: The Evolution of Life History and Parental Care

45 questions
Q001
p186Check Your UnderstandingMCQ
How does sexual selection differ from natural selection?
  • A)Sexual selection only acts on males, whereas natural selection acts on populations.
  • B)Variation among the choosy sex is not necessary for sexual selection to operate; variation among all individuals is necessary for natural selection to operate.
  • C)Sexual selection does not really differ from natural selection; both require heritable variation in traits that confer some fitness differences to individuals possessing those traits.
  • D)No. Sexual selection does not really differ from natural selection, but the optimum trait value under sexual selection can often be at odds with the optimum trait value under natural selection.
  • E)Both c and d
✓ Answer: E (Both c and d)
Natural selection and sexual selection both act on the heritable variation among individuals within a population that leads to differential fitness (Section 2.3). Sexual selection refers specifically to the differential reproductive success that results because individuals vary in traits that affect their ability to compete for fertilizations. In that sense, sexual selection can be considered a specific type of natural selection. Sexual selection also can produce phenotypic traits that actually reduce the fitness of many of the individuals that possess them, however (e.g., long, gangly tails that impair flight; bright colors or mating behaviors that attract the attention of predators), especially in populations where the operational sex ratio is highly skewed (Section 11.2).
📷 Show original page
Q002
p186Check Your UnderstandingMCQ
What is an/are important factor(s) influencing gene expression?
  • A)The gene control region
  • B)Cis- and trans-acting factors
  • C)Internal environmental variables, such as hormone levels and microRNAs
  • D)The external environment in which the organism develops, such as sunlight or food availability
  • E)All of the above
✓ Answer: E (All of the above)
The gene control region includes binding sites where different molecules can bind, affecting gene expression. Cis-regulatory factors bind to sites adjacent to the genes they affect. Trans-factors bind to sites far away from the genes they affect, coding for proteins, microRNAs, or other diffusible molecules that then influence expression of the focal genes. These gene products and microRNAs are components of the internal environmental variables that influence gene expression, but external environmental variables can also influence gene expression (see Section 5.1, Section 5.5, and Section 7.4).
📷 Show original page
Q003
p186Check Your UnderstandingMCQ
How may polyandry benefit females?
  • A)Females may be able to get the highest-quality genes possible for their offspring by mating with a number of males.
  • B)Females may benefit because they accumulate nutrients or other resources offered by each of the males.
  • C)Mating with multiple males may allow females to boost the health of their offspring by giving them a defense against a wider range of pathogens.
  • D)Females may mate with multiple males simply because the costs of resisting courtship overtures are higher than the costs of mating multiple times.
  • E)All of the above
✓ Answer: E (All of the above)
Polyandry is a mating system where females mate (or attempt to mate) with multiple males. Although polyandry is rare relative to polygyny, females benefit because their offspring may not only get good genes, but genes that are different from their mothers', or genes that provide a diversity of defenses against pathogens. Also, the cost of courtship may simply be so energetically expensive that females simply mate rather than resist (Section 11.4).

Imprinting can result in offspring who express either the maternal or paternal copy of the gene, but not both. c) An effect on the phenotype of an offspring caused by an allele inherited from a particular parent. d) A conflict between the fitness effects of alleles of a given locus on males and females. e) The deterioration in the biological functions of an organism as it ages. f) Refers to the pattern of investment an organism makes in growth and reproduction. Life-history traits include an organism's age at first reproduction, the duration and schedule of reproduction, the number and size of offspring produced, and life span. g) The process by which methyl groups are added to certain nucleotides. Methylation alters gene expression, thereby reducing or eliminating the production of proteins or RNA molecules. h) When parents have an evolutionary conflict of interest over the optimal strategy for parental care.
✓ Answer: c
A parent-of-origin effect is an effect on the phenotype of an offspring caused by an allele inherited from a particular parent.

Q033
p196Overcoming MisconceptionsMCQ
Which of the following is a true statement?
  • A)Males that try to maximize their reproductive success over their lifetimes will be more successful than males that don't.
  • B)Females try to assess environmental conditions so they can adjust the numbers of male and female offspring they have.
  • C)Theories in science are generally incomplete, so they can't explain complex issues like the diversity of life.
  • D)Evolution is more than chance.
✓ Answer: D (Evolution is more than chance.)
Chance is a factor in evolution, but natural selection and other evolutionary mechanisms are not random. Random mutations are the ultimate source of genetic variation, but natural selection, sexual selection, and genetic drift are non-random processes that influence the relative frequencies of alleles. So while part of the evolutionary process relies on randomness, shaping the diversity of life is decidedly not random. Options A and B are incorrect because organisms are not "trying" — adaptation results from natural selection, not intention (anthropomorphism). Option C is incorrect because evolutionary theory, while a work in progress, is supported by extensive evidence across diverse disciplines.

Q038
p197Delve DeeperShort Answer
How might parent-offspring conflict factor into sex-ratio adjustment in Seychelles warblers?
✓ Answer: Parent-offspring conflict occurs when parents benefit from withholding care or resources from some offspring in order to invest in other offspring. Natural selection has favored helping behavior in Seychelles warblers — helping provided by female offspring. Adult females and their offspring would conflict, however, whenever the conditions changed so that one sex (e.g., males) may be preferable to another in terms of reproductive fitness. For example, females may withhold care to female offspring in deteriorating environmental conditions, when producing more male offspring would be more adaptive. Conflict would arise because the deprived female offspring would benefit more if they received the withheld care or resources.
The mother's fitness interests may favor skewing the sex ratio toward males under deteriorating conditions, while the current female offspring's fitness would be maximized by receiving care — the source of parent-offspring conflict in this sex-ratio adjustment context.
📷 Show original page
Q039
p197Delve DeeperShort Answer
What predictions might you make about the timing of senescence in the different populations of guppies that Dr. Reznick studies?
✓ Answer: Because the different populations are evolving with different life history strategies, they are likely to experience different tradeoffs as well. If senescence results because of antagonistic pleiotropic effects, populations with shorter lifespans are less likely to experience those effects than populations with longer lifespans. Guppy populations from low predation environments may start breeding later and experience longer lifespans than populations in high predation environments. They may also experience the harmful effects of antagonistic pleiotropic effects. In the lab, one might predict that populations from low predation environments would show signs of aging, such as immune system decline, more than populations from high predation environments.
Antagonistic pleiotropy causes senescence because alleles favored early in life have deleterious effects later. Low-predation guppy populations live longer and thus express these late-life deleterious effects, whereas high-predation populations die young before the pleiotropic costs manifest.
📷 Show original page

  • C)Sex ratio adjustment only occurs in role-reversed species, so males choose which gender survives.
  • D)Parents selectively feed some offspring and not others, so only the most competitive gender survives.
  • E)The mechanisms are largely unknown, but substantial evidence indicates that parents can and do adjust the sex ratios of their broods.
✓ Answer: E
The mechanisms by which parents adjust offspring sex ratios are largely unknown, but substantial evidence indicates that parents can and do adjust the sex ratios of their broods across many taxa (answer key indicates Q1 = e/E).

Q042
p198Test YourselfMCQ
Which is the definition of senescence?
  • A)The deterioration of the human mind as a person ages
  • B)Alzheimer's disease
  • C)The deterioration in the biological functions of an organism as it ages
  • D)The tradeoff between reproducing and living longer
  • E)None of the above
✓ Answer: C
Senescence is defined as the deterioration in the biological functions of an organism as it ages. It is a whole-organism biological phenomenon, not limited to cognition (options A, B) nor the same as the life-history trade-off that may drive it (option D).
📷 Show original page
Q043
p198Test YourselfMCQ
Why would some scientists argue that menopause simply results from life-history trade-offs?
  • A)Because everything is a trade-off, and it makes sense that a female's inability to breed late in life must be a trade-off
  • B)Because natural selection cannot act on individuals that no longer reproduce.
  • C)Because genetic imprinting by males can cause females to become infertile
  • D)Because the mechanism that leads to infertility in menopausal women (i.e., damage to a female's eggs over time) is the same in other species
  • E)All of the above
✓ Answer: B
The life-history trade-off argument for menopause centers on the idea that natural selection cannot directly act on traits expressed by individuals that no longer reproduce. Once a female has stopped reproducing, selection no longer filters alleles affecting her post-reproductive biology, so late-life infertility may simply emerge as a byproduct of earlier life-history trade-offs rather than a directly selected adaptation.

Ch 13 — The Origin of Species

Q001
p0Interpret the DataInterpret the Data
✓ Answer: Drosophila species pairs were more likely to hybridize when they had a genetic distance < 0.5.
For closely related species (genetic distances < 0.5), the amount of hybridization ranged from about 0.15 to 1 - some pairs were hardly isolated at all. For more distantly related species pairs (D > 0.5), hybridization values ranged from about 0.45 to 1, so reproductive isolation was generally stronger. In general, more closely related species were more likely to hybridize, even though all species pairs hybridized at some level.

✓ Answer: Yes, even species that were very distantly related (D values greater than 1) were not completely isolated reproductively.
The figure shows hybridization still occurred among distantly related species pairs - reproductive isolation values did not all reach 1 (complete isolation). Some genetic exchange remained possible even at large genetic distances.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p0Interpret the DataInterpret the Data
✓ Answer: Drosophila species could be considered reproductively isolated at roughly half a million years (D between 0.5 and 1), because by then a typical pair of Drosophila species no longer interbred.
Since D increases roughly linearly with time and D = 1 corresponds to about 1 million years, D values in the 0.5-1 range correspond to about 0.5-1 million years. Within that window, reproductive isolation becomes substantial enough that typical species pairs no longer hybridize effectively.

Q007
p0Overcoming MisconceptionsOvercoming Misconceptions
✓ Answer: a. Random mutations are important to evolution because they provide the raw material for natural selection.
Mutations arise randomly and supply heritable variation, but natural selection acting on that variation is not random. (b) is false - indirect evidence (genetics, biogeography, fossils) is also valid. (c) is false - parent lineages need not go extinct when a new lineage splits off (e.g., allopatric speciation). (d) is false - thousands of transitional fossils have been discovered, including feathered dinosaur intermediates and many hominin ancestors.

Cryptic species arise when reproductive isolation evolves without accompanying adult morphological divergence. In Astraptes, stabilizing or convergent selection on adult appearance apparently constrains change, while ecological divergence plays out among caterpillars and their host plants.

Q012
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself
✓ Answer: c. The distribution of spawning times is completely non-overlapping.
Figure 13.4B shows that Montastrea coral species spawn at different times and their spawning-time distributions do not overlap. This temporal (allochronic) prezygotic isolating barrier prevents gametes of the two species from meeting even though they share the same habitat.
📷 Show original page
Q013
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself
✓ Answer: c. Reproductive isolating barriers are important in both allopatric and sympatric speciation.
Reproductive isolating barriers (prezygotic and postzygotic) are essential whether populations diverge in geographic isolation (allopatry) or while overlapping in range (sympatry). In allopatry, isolating barriers typically evolve as a byproduct of divergence. In sympatry, they must arise despite ongoing gene flow. In both cases, barriers are what allow lineages to remain distinct.

Q016

✓ Answer: b. Groups of organisms that are genetically distinct and do not interbreed, but are almost indistinguishable morphologically.
Cryptic species are genetically distinct, reproductively isolated lineages that look so similar that humans (and often their potential mates' visual cues) cannot easily tell them apart. The Astraptes fulgerator butterflies are a classic example. (a) describes a single species. (c) describes the phylogenetic species concept. (d) describes convergent evolution between distinct lineages.

Q017
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself
✓ Answer: d. All of the above.
All three statements are accurate and reinforce each other. HGT plus rapid diversification undermines universal species concepts built for sexually reproducing eukaryotes (a). Homologous recombination between microbial cells is often treated analogously to sex because it exchanges genetic material (b). The resulting mosaic genomes make discrete classification difficult (c). Together these features explain why microbial species concepts require different models.
📷 Show original page
Q018
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself
✓ Answer: d. Within a single 'species,' microbial strains may not be that closely related genetically.
Genomic studies show that strains classified as the same microbial 'species' can have genomes differing by thousands of genes because of horizontal gene transfer and rapid diversification. This challenges the notion of a coherent species-level genome in microbes and underscores why classical species concepts apply poorly to prokaryotes.
📷 Show original page
Q019
p0ContemplateContemplate
✓ Answer: Open-ended reflection (no answer key provided in the study guide).
Benefits of speciation can include adaptive diversification into new niches, increased overall biodiversity and ecosystem complexity, and the origin of novel traits through reproductive isolation and divergent selection. Costs can include reduced gene flow (and thus reduced heritable variation) within each new lineage, vulnerability of small newly formed species to extinction, and potential disruption of mutualisms tied to the ancestral lineage. The question encourages students to weigh evolutionary trade-offs rather than arrive at a single answer.
📷 Show original page
Q020
p0ContemplateContemplate
✓ Answer: Open-ended reflection (no answer key provided in the study guide).
Language differences can act as a prezygotic behavioral isolating barrier by reducing courtship, mate choice, and stable pair-bonding between speakers of different languages. Shared language facilitates communication, cultural similarity, and assortative mating, while language barriers can reduce gene flow between populations. This is analogous to behavioral isolation in other species (e.g., bird song differences between meadowlark species). The effect is partial, however - bilingualism, migration, and intermarriage frequently cross language boundaries.
📷 Show original page

Ch 14 — Macroevolution: The Long Run

27 questions
Q001
p014.8 The "Big Five" Mass ExtinctionsKey Concepts (Fill in the Blank)
✓ Answer: single cause / unifying cause
Each of the Big Five mass extinctions had distinct causes — massive volcanism, asteroid impact, sea-level change, or climate shifts — and affected different groups of organisms. No single mechanism unifies all five events. (Inferred from textbook Section 14.8.)

Q002
p014.9 Macroevolution and Our "Sixth Mass Extinction"Key Concepts (Fill in the Blank)
✓ Answer: cascading / ecosystem-wide effects (loss of ecosystem services)
While losing a single species may not dramatically alter ecosystem function, a mass extinction disrupts food webs, mutualisms, and ecosystem services across the planet. These cascading effects propagate through ecosystems, potentially driving additional extinctions and undermining services humans depend on. (Inferred from textbook Section 14.9.)

Q005
p0Interpret the Data (Figure 14.27)Interpret the Data
✓ Answer: The baseline is the average temperature measured over 20 years, from 1960 to 1980. Temperature anomalies are shown as the difference between that baseline and the average temperature for a given year.
Establishing a baseline allows scientists to understand how observational data may change over time. The baseline establishes a point of reference, and calculating differences from the baseline can help determine relative changes — to understand trends. Average annual temperatures are one line of evidence for global climate change, and calculating that statistic requires actual measurement of temperatures (though indirect proxies also exist). Data on global temperatures can be limiting because of history and ability to measure global temperature from a diversity of locations. The period from 1960 to 1980 generally has better coverage and data availability than earlier years (even though data from earlier years might have been less affected by anthropogenic changes). Calculating differences from this baseline shows that the years before 1960 were relatively cooler and the years after 1980 were relatively warmer. More importantly, the extent of the differences is readily apparent.

Q006
p0Interpret the Data (Figure 14.27)Interpret the Data
✓ Answer: No.
Models take into account a diversity of variables, conditions, and scenarios. Different scientists weigh the evidence supporting those variables differently, so variation is expected. Having different models with different variables and different conditions is an important part of the scientific process, allowing for rigorous debate and consensus building. The computer models used to make predictions about temperature in the year 2100 consistently show that the planet will warm — even if carbon levels remain at levels measured in the year 2000.

Q007
p0Games and Exercises — How Big Is a Billion?Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: Open-ended hands-on exercise. The point is to visualize deep time by scaling stacks of quarters.
A U.S. quarter is about 0.069 inches (1.75 mm) thick. Scaling up: 10 quarters ≈ 0.69 in (~1.75 cm) 100 ≈ 6.9 in (~17.5 cm) 1,000 ≈ 69 in / 5.75 ft (~1.75 m — roughly your height) 10,000 ≈ 57.5 ft (~17.5 m) 100,000 ≈ 575 ft / ~0.11 mi (~175 m — roughly 1.5 football fields / 1/10 mile) 1,000,000 ≈ 1,094 yd / ~1.1 mi (~1.75 km — a typical distance to school) 10,000,000 ≈ ~11 mi (~17.5 km) 100,000,000 ≈ ~110 mi (~175 km) 1,000,000,000 ≈ ~1,100 mi (~1,750 km — roughly the flight distance from mid-U.S. to Anchorage, Alaska) The exercise illustrates how a billion years is vastly larger than the time frames of human experience — macroevolutionary patterns unfold over spans the human brain has difficulty intuitively appreciating. (Open-response; answer key does not list specific numerical answers.)

Q008
p0Games and Exercises — Adaptive Radiation in the Hawaiian HoneycreepersGames and Exercises
✓ Answer: No single beak type works best for all foods. Each tool excels at one food type:
This demonstrates a core principle of adaptive radiation: different morphologies create trade-offs, so no single "generalist" design is optimal across all resource types. Specialization yields greater foraging efficiency on particular food types, which is the mechanism by which natural selection can favor divergent bill morphologies in different populations. (Open-response exercise; answer key does not list specific results.)
📷 Show original page
Q009
p0Games and Exercises — Adaptive Radiation in the Hawaiian HoneycreepersGames and Exercises
✓ Answer: Individuals in the ancestral population varied in bill shape. In a given habitat, individuals whose bills happened to be better suited to locally abundant food types were more efficient foragers, survived and reproduced at higher rates, and passed those heritable bill traits to offspring. Over generations, different populations experiencing different selective pressures (different food types on different islands or in different habitats) accumulated divergent morphologies, ultimately producing the diverse bill forms seen in modern honeycreepers.
This is an application of the four Darwinian requirements: heritable variation in bill shape, differential reproductive success tied to that variation, and descent with modification over deep time. Combined with geographic isolation among the Hawaiian Islands, the process produced an adaptive radiation — many descendant species filling different ecological niches from a single common ancestor. (Open-response exercise.)
📷 Show original page
Q010
p0Games and Exercises — Adaptive Radiation in the Hawaiian HoneycreepersGames and Exercises
✓ Answer: Yes. Different habitats offer different resources (different plants, insects, nectar sources, etc.), so natural selection would favor different bill morphologies and foraging strategies on different islands. Combined with reduced gene flow between islands (geographic isolation), reciprocal natural selection on divergent resource bases would drive the populations on different islands to evolve into different species — an adaptive radiation.
This is the logic behind allopatric speciation and adaptive radiation on archipelagos: (1) colonization of multiple isolated habitats by a common ancestor, (2) differential selection pressures in each habitat, and (3) reproductive isolation among islands. Together these produce a set of closely related but ecologically distinct species — as observed in both Hawaiian honeycreepers and Darwin's Galápagos finches. (Open-response exercise.)

Q011
p0Games and Exercises — Adaptive Radiation in the Hawaiian HoneycreepersGames and Exercises (Matching)

Per the textbook description on page 224: - Kona grosbeaks used their thick, heavy bills to crack open hard seeds such as the naio. - The Kaua'i 'akialoa foraged for insects in deep mats of moss; its unusually long, down-curved bill also reached nectar from long tubular lobelia flowers. - The po'ouli used its small bill to feed on snails, grubs, and insects in moss. - The 'akiapola'au, like a woodpecker, uses its sharp beak to forage for insects (larvae) under bark and lichens. These forms illustrate the ecomorphological diversity generated by the honeycreeper adaptive radiation — a cautionary tale given that introduced predators and diseases have driven many of these species extinct.

Q012
p0Overcoming Misconceptions — Microevolution and Macroevolution Are Two Sides of the Same Coin / Gaps in the Fossil Record and EvolutionOvercoming Misconceptions (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. Scientists have discovered many fossils that show the transition between dinosaurs and birds and between our primate ancestors and modern humans.
Since Darwin's time, paleontologists have discovered numerous transitional fossils, including those within the human lineage, between land mammals and modern whales, and between dinosaurs and modern birds. The other options are misconceptions: - (a) is false — microevolution and macroevolution are driven by the same mechanisms; macroevolutionary patterns emerge when microevolutionary changes accumulate over long time spans. - (b) is false — given enough time, microevolutionary processes can produce enough differences between populations that they become separate species. - (c) is false — fossilization is rare, and the record is inherently incomplete; many transitional forms will never be found.
📷 Show original page
Q013
p0Go the Distance — Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer: Paleodiversity estimates are biased by uneven sampling of the fossil record: fossil-bearing rocks differ in area and volume exposed across geological periods, sampling effort (number of collections, fossiliferous localities) varies with time period, and preservation quality varies among environments. More-sampled intervals yield higher apparent diversity even when actual diversity was similar, so raw fossil counts do not directly reflect true biodiversity.
Mayhew et al. (2012) explicitly controlled for these sampling biases (e.g., by subsampling to equal effort) to test whether marine invertebrate biodiversity tracks temperature across the Phanerozoic. Without that control, patterns attributed to biology could actually reflect the geological record's unevenness. (Open-response; answer key does not list a worked answer for this item.)
📷 Show original page
Q014
p0Go the Distance — Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer: Because any real biological pattern — such as a relationship between biodiversity and past temperature — could be obscured or even generated spuriously by the uneven sampling of the fossil record. If fossil-bearing rock availability is correlated with climate, sampling bias could mimic or mask a genuine biodiversity–temperature relationship. Controlling for sampling lets them test whether the biological signal is real.
Earlier studies had produced conflicting conclusions about the diversity–temperature relationship precisely because they had not adequately corrected for geological bias. Addressing the bias is a prerequisite for drawing valid macroevolutionary inferences from the Phanerozoic fossil record. (Open-response.)
📷 Show original page
Q015
p0Go the Distance — Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance (Primary Literature)
✓ Answer: After correcting for sampling bias, Mayhew et al. found that marine invertebrate biodiversity is negatively correlated with greenhouse (warm) climate states across the Phanerozoic — higher global temperatures are associated with lower marine biodiversity (higher extinction rates and/or lower origination), and cooler intervals are associated with higher diversity. Biodiversity tracks temperature over time.
This finding has implications for the current warming trend: if the correlation between warmth and lower biodiversity holds, modern anthropogenic warming may be contributing to elevated extinction risk. The result complements evidence from Figure 14.27 and Section 14.9 about the potential sixth mass extinction. (Open-response.)
📷 Show original page
Q016
p0Delve DeeperDelve Deeper
✓ Answer: No. Punctuated equilibria is a model of the pattern of change over time. It proposes that although most lineages do not change much over the course of their geologic history, every once in a while there are periods of rapid change, often leading to speciation events. Darwin proposed that gradual changes accumulate over extended periods of time, ultimately leading to speciation events.
Critics of evolution sometimes set up a false dichotomy that punctuated equilibria and Darwin's gradualism are alternative, incompatible theories. But just like macroevolution and microevolution, this argument focuses on the patterns — not the underlying mechanisms. Darwin proposed that organisms struggle for existence — those with traits that help them do better (survive and/or reproduce) have more offspring, and those traits become more common in the population in future generations. Although he did not have an understanding of mutations, genes, or genetic toolkits, he understood that traits were valuable commodities in the struggle. If a mutation — say, to an important gene in the genetic toolkit — led to an altered development of fins, that trait may rapidly spread through a portion of the population if individuals possessing the new limb structure do well (e.g., accessing a new food source at the water's edge). Changes may continue to accumulate in the genetic structure of the limb relatively rapidly (remember, "rapid" in the geologic record can still be hundreds of thousands of years) because individuals with incrementally better limbs do relatively better. That set of traits may persist for a while — maybe with small gradual changes accumulating that are not detectable in the fossil record — a long period of stasis. Then another structure-changing mutation may occur, and rapid change resumes. Also, the tree of life is not linear. A trait that allows exploitation of a new habitat may lead to rapid adaptive radiations on small or large scales. Because of the fossil record, scientists may never find more than one or two representatives of any radiation — they must piece together the tree of life from an incomplete record. Both models (gradualism and punctuated equilibria) offer insight into the patterns of the history of life.
📷 Show original page
Q017
p0Delve DeeperDelve Deeper
✓ Answer: The term "Cambrian Explosion" is misleading because it implies that a diversity of animals sprang into existence all at once. In reality:
Sometime around 542 million years ago, evolutionary processes led to a huge diversification of groups found in the fossil record. Molecular clock estimates predict that scientists should be able to find fossil animals in rocks up to ~800 million years old; macroevolution predicts that the Cambrian Explosion may be related to changing physical conditions and a new kind of ecosystem; and microevolution predicts that it may be related to the evolution of a versatile genetic toolkit.

Q018
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: a. The total number of originations and extinctions in a given interval of time
Turnover rate quantifies how much taxonomic composition changes over a given time interval by summing the events that add species (originations / speciation) and those that remove species (extinctions). It measures ecosystem dynamism, not net change. Option (b) defines standing diversity. Options (d) and (e) describe net diversification or net loss, not turnover.
📷 Show original page
Q019
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: c. Darwin did not believe in vicariance.
This statement is FALSE — it misrepresents what Darwin thought. Actually, Darwin tended to favor dispersal over vicariance as an explanation for biogeographic patterns (he did not strongly endorse vicariance as a primary mechanism), but the statement as phrased is used to flag the historically inaccurate option. The other three statements are true: biogeography studies species distributions over space and time (a); Wallace pioneered the field (b); and plate tectonics supplied a powerful geological framework (continental drift) that explained many biogeographic patterns Darwin could only speculate about (d).
📷 Show original page
Q020
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. Patterns observed in the fossil record cannot be tested using statistical methods.
This is NOT a problem — patterns in the fossil record can be, and routinely are, tested statistically (e.g., Mayhew et al. 2012 used rarefaction/subsampling and correlation tests). The other listed issues (a, c, d) are real methodological challenges: uneven rock exposure biases sampling; paraphyletic or polyphyletic higher taxa confound analyses; and working above the species level can obscure patterns because higher-rank taxa are not equivalent across clades.
📷 Show original page
Q021
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. Because significant changes in phenotypes may occur within small isolated populations on the fringes of a species' range, leading to rapid speciation and little fossil evidence.
Punctuated equilibria (Eldredge & Gould, 1972) proposes that most morphological change concentrates in brief bursts during speciation events, typically in small peripheral (allopatric) populations. These populations are both geographically restricted and evolutionarily short-lived, so they leave little fossil evidence. Once a new species becomes widespread, it enters a long period of stasis that does fossilize well — producing the appearance of abrupt replacement in the stratigraphic record. Options (a) and (c) describe incidental or definitional issues, not the mechanism. Options (d) and (e) describe gradualism/anagenesis, which punctuated equilibria contrasts with.
📷 Show original page
Q022
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: c. Wings
The evolution of flight (wings) is the canonical key innovation for insects. Wings opened up aerial niches, allowed rapid dispersal, escape from predators, and access to new resources — triggering one of the most successful adaptive radiations in the history of life. Insects are by far the most species-rich animal clade. Flowering plants (a) also contributed to later insect diversification (especially Hymenoptera, Lepidoptera) but the wings innovation long predates that and seeded the radiation.

Q023
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. The sudden appearance of sponges marks the beginning of the Cambrian Explosion.
This statement is FALSE. Sponges appear in the fossil record well before the Cambrian — some fossils date to more than 600 million years ago, long before the Cambrian (~541 million years ago). The Cambrian Explosion is defined by the rapid diversification of bilaterian animals with complex body plans, not by the first appearance of sponges. The other statements are correct: rising oxygen levels (a), the evolution of the developmental genetic toolkit (b), the gradual precursors to "explosive" body plans (c), and molecular clock estimates placing animal origins near 800 Mya (e) are all supported by current evidence.
📷 Show original page
Q024
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: a. As a statistical departure from background extinction rates
Paleontologists define mass extinctions rigorously as statistically significant elevations above the long-term background extinction rate — not simply as "many species disappearing." This statistical definition avoids artifacts from uneven sampling and accounts for the fact that some fossil "extinctions" are actually Lazarus taxa (reappear later), Elvis taxa (convergent look-alikes), or Zombie taxa (reworked fossils). Option (c) is imprecise; (d) describes corrections to raw counts but not the formal definition.

✓ Answer: e. All of the above
All four factors together make up the scientific case for concern: - (a) Climate models project warming 2–3× the magnitude observed by 2000, within a lifetime. - (b) Four of the Big Five mass extinctions have been linked to climate disruption (whether warming or cooling), so climate change is a known extinction driver. - (c) Observed warming of ~0.8 °C over the last 80 years is documented in Graph B. - (d) Modern CO₂ concentrations (now >400 ppm) are ~100 ppm higher than any point in the last 600,000 years (Graph A), indicating an unprecedented forcing. Together these lines of evidence are why scientists argue anthropogenic climate change could drive a sixth mass extinction.

Q026
p0ContemplateContemplate (Open-ended)
✓ Answer: (Open-ended — answer key does not provide a worked response.)
Vicariance predicts that lineages on South America and Africa that share a common ancestor from before the breakup of Gondwana (~100–150 million years ago) should form sister clades on those two continents, with their split dates roughly matching the timing of continental separation. For such lineages, the phylogeny should show South American and African species as each other's closest relatives, with African-South American sister relationships across a range of independent groups. Dispersal, by contrast, predicts more recent, taxonomically scattered links — cases where a lineage crossed the Atlantic (e.g., via wind, ocean currents, or rafting) long after the continents separated. Dispersed clades would show younger split dates than the geological separation and often nest within a clade from one continent rather than forming deep sister relationships. In practice, South American and African plant floras show a mix: some ancient Gondwanan groups (e.g., Proteaceae, Restionaceae, certain legumes) show vicariant patterns, while others show more recent dispersal (e.g., some Rapateaceae, certain Malpighiales). Using Figure 11.7 as a guide, a student would identify clades with deep SA-African splits (vicariance candidates) versus young SA-African connections (dispersal candidates).
📷 Show original page
Q027
p0ContemplateContemplate (Open-ended)
✓ Answer: (Open-ended — answer key does not provide a worked response.)
Yes. Punctuated equilibria's central insight — long periods of relative stasis interrupted by short bursts of rapid change, often concentrated in small "isolated" subpopulations — maps well onto linguistic evolution. Potential applications: - Long stasis: Written/standardized English changes slowly across centuries, with pronunciation, grammar, and core vocabulary remaining fairly stable over many generations. - Rapid change: Major historical events (the Norman Conquest in 1066, the Great Vowel Shift ~1400-1700, the printing press, colonization, the internet) produce rapid shifts in vocabulary, pronunciation, or grammar within decades rather than centuries. - "Peripheral" populations driving change: Just as small, isolated biological populations can quickly diverge, small speech communities (geographic dialects like AAVE, Singlish, Caribbean Englishes, or Internet/subculture slang communities) often generate innovations that later spread into the mainstream. - Speciation analog: When isolated speech communities diverge sufficiently, they can become mutually unintelligible — analogous to reproductive isolation — producing distinct languages (e.g., Old English → modern English, or the divergence of Romance languages from Vulgar Latin). - Fossil-record analog: Written records are patchy and biased toward elite, formal usage, so everyday speech changes may be underrepresented, mirroring the incompleteness of the biological fossil record. The analogy is not perfect — language transmission is horizontal (learned) rather than strictly vertical (genetic), and individuals can be "bilingual" in a way organisms cannot — but the core pattern of stasis punctuated by rapid, localized change is a useful framework for both evolutionary biology and historical linguistics.
📷 Show original page

Ch 15 — Intimate Partnerships: How Species Adapt to Each Other

27 questions
Q001
p0Check Your UnderstandingCheck Your Understanding (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. Otters (Lutrinae) share a common ancestor with Odobenidae (walruses).
Phylogenies represent the relationships among populations, genes, or species. Each branch represents a lineage, and lineages are organized in clades so that the taxa within them share derived characteristics. The visualization of the phylogeny is fluid, with the parts at every intersection swinging independently — any of the branches from Ursidae (bears) to Lutrinae can easily be flipped within the phylogeny. The correct way to interpret the relationships is to follow the lineage back to the node; groups joined at a node share a common ancestor. Why the other choices are incorrect: - a. Phylogenies are often misinterpreted as ladders of evolution (one species evolving into the next). The Lutrinae/Procyonidae branch could easily flip, so Lutrinae did not evolve "from" martins. - b. Taxa within a clade share derived characteristics; this does not mean ancestors were becoming progressively more "otter-like." Any branch from Ursidae to Lutrinae can be flipped. - c. Otters share more derived characteristics with Odobenidae (walruses) than with wolves (Canidae), but otters do not represent the end of the Caniformia lineage, and the common ancestor was not necessarily wolf-like. - e. Only d is correct; a, b, and c all reflect ladder-of-evolution or end-of-lineage misinterpretations. (Section 4.1)
📷 Show original page
Q002
p0Check Your UnderstandingCheck Your Understanding (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. All are valid definitions of a species.
Each of the first three choices defines a species according to a different species concept, and all three are valid. A single definition may not be applicable to all organisms. - a. Biological species concept — defines a species based on the ability of individuals to interbreed. Commonly used for sexually reproducing organisms, though it has limitations (e.g., giraffes may not comprise a single species according to other species concepts). - b. Phylogenetic species concept — defines species as recognizable geographic forms with a unique evolutionary history; species are the "tips" of a phylogenetic tree. Especially useful when reconstructing relationships using DNA. - c. General lineage species concept — defines species based on the boundaries of the gene pool; this is more a conceptual definition than a methodological one. - e. Incorrect — scientists do know what species are; they simply recognize that different concepts apply in different contexts. (Section 13.1)
📷 Show original page
Q003
p0Check Your UnderstandingCheck Your Understanding (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: c. Types of DNA that can move around in the genome.
Mobile genetic elements include transposons ("jumping genes") and plasmids. They are considered genomic parasites — they make new copies of themselves that can then be reinserted into the genome, and they can literally dominate a genome. Mobile genetic elements can have devastating effects on the organism. For example, if a mobile genetic element disrupts a cell's normal rhythms of growth and division, the cell may begin to multiply out of control, giving rise to cancer. Why the other choices are incorrect: - a. Bacteria and viruses are parasites that can infect other organisms, but only some viruses became mobile genetic elements by integrating their genes into host genomes. The origins of many mobile genetic elements are unknown. - b. Mobile genetic elements are not genes. They can disrupt gene expression (e.g., by inserting into a gene's regulatory region), but they are DNA segments that move, not genes of unknown function. - d. Noncoding sections of DNA that function in alternative splicing are introns, not mobile genetic elements. (Section 5.1)
📷 Show original page
Q004
p0Identify Key TermsIdentify Key Terms (Matching)
✓ Answer:
- Batesian mimicry (c): Harmless species resemble harmful/distasteful species to gain protection from predators. - Coevolution (d): The reciprocal evolutionary change between interacting species, driven by natural selection. - Coevolutionary escalation / arms race (h): Antagonistic interaction with reciprocal directional selection; one species evolves to overcome the other's weapons, selecting for new weaponry in turn. - Diversifying coevolution (f): Increase in genetic diversity caused by heterogeneity of coevolutionary processes across the range of ecological partners. - Endosymbionts (a): Mutualistic organisms that live within the body or cells of another organism (e.g., mitochondria ancestors). - Geographic mosaic theory (g): Population geographic structure is central to coevolutionary dynamics; direction and intensity of coevolution varies from population to population, with coevolved genes mixing through gene flow. - Müllerian mimicry (i): Several harmful or distasteful species resemble each other, facilitating learned avoidance by predators. - Reciprocal selection (b): Selection in two species due to their interactions with one another — the critical prerequisite of coevolution. - Retrovirus (e): An RNA virus that uses reverse transcriptase to integrate its DNA into host cells' DNA (e.g., HIV). (Chapter 15 Key Terms)
📷 Show original page
Q005
p0Link ConceptsLink Concepts (Concept Map / Fill-in Bubbles)
✓ Answer:
Reciprocal selection is the process by which interacting species impose selection on each other — this is the prerequisite for coevolution. The geographic mosaic theory explains how coevolutionary hot spots and cold spots vary across a species' range. Diversifying coevolution increases biodiversity because heterogeneous coevolutionary pressures produce distinct locally-adapted forms. Batesian and Müllerian mimicry both rely on predators' learned avoidance of distasteful/harmful signals. Endosymbiosis — ancestral free-living bacteria becoming internal organelles — produced mitochondria and plastids, enabling the evolution of eukaryotic cells. Retroviruses are genomic parasites that insert themselves into host genomes; "cheating" occurs when members of a mutualism exploit partners without reciprocating benefits.
📷 Show original page
Q006
p0Interpret the DataInterpret the Data (Short Answer)
✓ Answer: Grade I in 1951 and Grade III in 1952.
In 1951, when the myxoma virus was first introduced to control the Australian rabbit population, Grade I (the deadliest grade) was the most common. By 1952, only one year later, the most common grade had shifted to Grade III (an intermediate level of virulence). This rapid shift reflects strong natural selection acting on the virus population as host availability and transmission dynamics changed.
📷 Show original page
Q007
p0Interpret the DataInterpret the Data (Short Answer)
✓ Answer: Myxoma virus is not a single individual, nor a single strain. Viruses can evolve quickly because of their high reproductive rates — mutations spread through a virus population producing new strains rather quickly. Any particular rabbit is likely to be infected by more than one strain of myxoma virus, and competition among strains within that rabbit would lead to increased virulence. However, individual rabbits infected with strains that become too virulent may die before the virus can be transmitted — any virus population within a dead individual is a dead strain. So strains that aren't too virulent will be more successful because their populations can grow and still be transmitted to new hosts.
This is a classic case of coevolution between a pathogen and host. Short generation times and large population sizes make viruses evolve extremely quickly. The initial high virulence reflected within-host competition among strains; subsequent selection favored intermediate virulence because overly deadly strains killed hosts before being transmitted.
📷 Show original page
Q008
p0Interpret the DataInterpret the Data (Short Answer)
✓ Answer: Fairly stable, but not absolute. Selection clearly favors the intermediate grades, but that doesn't mean something couldn't alter the balance. Virus populations are still experiencing mutations, and one of those could lead to entirely unknown trajectories. In fact, the data show that the balance does indeed shift — a more virulent grade appeared for a short time from 1970 to 1974, although it was only a small proportion of the total virus population.
Intermediate virulence represents a fitness optimum balancing within-host replication with successful transmission to new hosts. This balance is a stable equilibrium under current ecological conditions, but mutations, changes in host density, or changes in host resistance can shift selection pressures and momentarily push the virus population toward either greater or lesser virulence.

Q010
p0Overcoming MisconceptionsOvercoming Misconceptions (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. Coevolution can lead to the loss of biodiversity.
Coevolution can intensify extinctions and the loss of biological diversity. Highly specialized coevolutionary interactions make species highly dependent on each other; if one species becomes extinct, its obligate partners may also go extinct as a result. Coevolutionary arms races can also push one partner to such high fitness that the other cannot survive. Why the other choices are incorrect: - a. Convergent evolution describes similarities in traits produced by independent evolution in unrelated lineages (pattern). Coevolution describes reciprocal evolutionary change driven by interactions between species (process). They are different concepts and do not explain the same thing. - b. Mutualisms are not guaranteed to persist. If circumstances change (e.g., arrival of a new species, environmental shift), mutualisms can break down or become antagonistic. There is no evolutionary guarantee of continued coevolution. - c. Coevolutionary relationships often impose fitness costs (e.g., snakes that are highly resistant to newt toxin move more slowly and are more vulnerable to their own predators; producing stronger toxins, weapons, or defenses is metabolically expensive).

This study is a classic demonstration of rapid contemporary evolution in response to an invasive species.

This illustrates a general principle of evolutionary field biology: researchers almost never measure every individual. They rely on random or stratified sampling of the population and use statistical inference to draw conclusions about population-level trait distributions. This is a key reason variance and sample size are so important in evolutionary studies.

The temporal resolution of historical studies of evolutionary change is limited by the availability of dated specimens. Soapberry bug researchers were fortunate that museum collections existed pre- and post-invasion by the balloon vine, providing a rare "before/after" natural experiment. When archival material isn't available, researchers rely on common-garden experiments, experimental evolution, or quantitative genetic predictions to evaluate evolutionary change over time.
