Q014
p0Delve DeeperDelve Deeper (Short Answer / Essay)
✓ Answer: Variation among individuals, differential survival or reproduction, and heredity are all necessary for the coevolution of newts and snakes.
This answer ties Darwin's three prerequisites for natural selection (variation, differential fitness, heredity) to the geographic mosaic theory of coevolution. Reciprocal selection operates only where populations of the two species overlap and vary in their traits; elsewhere, selection is weaker or absent, producing a geographic mosaic of coevolutionary hot spots and cold spots.
📷 Show original page
Q015
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. When two or more species interact, and each acts as an agent of selection causing evolution of the other.
Coevolution is the reciprocal evolutionary change between interacting species, driven by natural selection. The critical feature is that each species acts as an agent of selection on the other — changes in one produce selective pressures on the other, and vice versa. Why the other choices are incorrect: - a. Describes a one-way response; coevolution requires reciprocal selection, not just one species changing in response to another. - c. Shared traits such as wings reflect convergent evolution (or homology), not coevolution. Convergent evolution describes pattern; coevolution describes process. - d. Coevolution need not be complementary or beneficial to all species. Antagonistic coevolution (predator–prey, parasite–host, arms races) is common and does not benefit all partners. - e. Only b is correct. (Chapter 15)
📷 Show original page
Q016
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: a. Positive mutualism occurs when the fitness outcomes for all interacting species are high; positive commensalism occurs when the fitness outcomes some species are high, but others realize no fitness consequences.
Mutualism is a relationship in which all interacting species benefit — all have higher fitness as a result of the interaction. Commensalism is a relationship in which one (or some) species benefit while the other(s) are neither helped nor harmed (i.e., experience no net fitness consequence). Why the other choices are incorrect: - b. Describes the definitions backwards (mutualism would require all partners to benefit, not just some). - c. Both mutualism and commensalism can involve coevolution; whether coevolution occurs depends on whether each species imposes selection on the other, not on whether fitness is shared. - d. Natural selection does not always favor mutualism over commensalism; it favors whatever maximizes the fitness of individuals, and mutualistic relationships are vulnerable to "cheating." - e. Mutualism can and often does lead to coevolution (mutualistic species often exert strong positive frequency-dependent selection on each other). The directionality asserted here is incorrect. (Chapter 15)
📷 Show original page
Q017
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: e. a and c only.
Figure 15.11 plots log newt toxicity against log snake resistance and shows that within and across populations, both traits vary widely. In populations where snakes have low resistance, newts still display a range of toxicity levels (a is true). Within any given location, individual snakes vary in their resistance to the toxin (c is true). Why the other choices are incorrect: - b. Extremely resistant snakes still exert strong selection on newts — if they can eat newts even at high toxicity levels, they favor further escalation of newt toxicity (classic arms race). They do affect the evolution of newt toxicity. - d. Within locations, newts do vary in their ability to produce toxin — variation exists within populations, which is the raw material for coevolutionary selection. - f. "All of the above" includes false statements b and d, so it is incorrect. (Chapter 15, Figure 15.11; Brodie et al. study system)
📷 Show original page
Q018
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: e. All of the above.
Each of the first four statements captures a real piece of evidence from Figure 15.18 that supports diversification of red crossbills driven by coevolution with pine trees: - a. The time-series data show divergence of bill-depth trajectories, and the right panel shows survival consequences — together these indicate that disruptive or divergent selection acts on bill depth. - b. Fitness peaks when bill depth is matched to scale thickness (slightly larger than scale thickness). Birds with mismatched bills specialize on other cone types, driving divergence. - c. Within-population variation in bill depth is narrower than species-wide variation, consistent with local specialization on local cone types and the buildup of structure that can lead to cladogenesis. - d. Scale thickness varies among pine species within the range of red crossbills, so different bill depths are favored in different areas. Spatial variation in selection maintains morphological variation among populations. Together, these lines of evidence show that red crossbills are experiencing divergent selection linked to their coevolutionary relationship with different pine species — a plausible engine of speciation. (Chapter 15, Figure 15.18; Benkman et al. studies on red crossbills)
📷 Show original page
Q019
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. That mainland flowers were not naturally producing much fruit, even though they were capable of producing much more.
Anderson and colleagues hand-pollinated Rhabdothamnus solandri flowers on mainland New Zealand and found that manually pollinated flowers produced far more fruit than flowers left to natural pollination. This demonstrated that mainland plants were pollinator-limited: they had the reproductive capacity to produce much more fruit but were not receiving enough pollination visits. On offshore islands (free of invasive predators), native bird pollinators were still abundant, and natural fruit set was much closer to the maximum demonstrated by hand-pollination. This is a classic case showing the reproductive consequences of pollinator loss, linking mutualism breakdown to reduced plant reproductive success. Why the other choices are incorrect: - a. The comparison showed that mainland flowers, under natural pollination, produced fewer fruits than island flowers — not more. Manually pollinated mainland flowers could match island levels, which is the point. - c. This is actually (approximately) true as background context — island populations were producing near-maximum fruit — but it is not what the hand-pollination experiment on the mainland specifically demonstrated. - d. The mainland had fewer effective pollinators (native birds had been lost from the mainland), not more. - e. Since a and d are wrong, "all of the above" cannot be correct. (Chapter 15)
📷 Show original page
Q020
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: f. Both c and d.
A coevolutionary arms race between bacteria and their hosts produces two diagnostic evolutionary signatures: - c. Negative frequency-dependent selection: As bacterial variants (e.g., new surface antigens) become common, hosts evolve immunity specifically against them, so common variants lose fitness while rare variants are favored. This generates the classic "Red Queen" dynamic typical of host–parasite arms races. - d. Matching patterns of speciation (cophylogeny / codivergence): In a long-term reciprocal coevolutionary relationship, when the host lineage splits into two new species, the associated bacterial lineage is often partitioned along with it. Over many splits, this produces parallel (or closely matched) phylogenies between hosts and their bacteria. Why the other choices are incorrect: - a, b. Coevolution does not require that one lineage be older than the other; both lineages can originate and diversify together. Relative age of the clades is not a direct prediction of arms-race coevolution. - e. Includes a, which is not a required prediction. (Chapter 15)
📷 Show original page
Q021
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: e. Both c and d.
Scientists are fairly certain about the broad story of mitochondrial origin. Multiple independent lines of evidence — mitochondrial DNA being closely related to α-proteobacteria (SAR11 and relatives), the presence of their own ribosomes and circular genome, their double membranes, and independent binary fission — all support the endosymbiotic origin hypothesis (Margulis). This means: - c. The weight of evidence supports an origin early in the evolution of eukaryotes. - d. Mitochondria were once free-living, oxygen-consuming bacteria engulfed by an ancestral host cell. At the same time, scientists are still refining details about the exact lineage, the precise timing, and the mechanistic steps by which the coevolutionary relationship began (hence "still gathering evidence"). Why the other choices are incorrect in isolation: - a, b. These overstate uncertainty — the overall hypothesis is well-supported, even though fine-scale details remain under investigation. - c alone or d alone. Both statements are true, so the best answer is the combined choice e. (Chapter 15, Section 15.4)
📷 Show original page
Q022
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. An RNA virus that uses an enzyme to become part of the host cells' DNA.
A retrovirus is an RNA virus that encodes the enzyme reverse transcriptase, which copies its RNA genome into DNA. This DNA copy is then integrated into the host cell's genome by a second viral enzyme (integrase), allowing the virus's genetic material to be replicated along with the host's chromosomes. HIV (human immunodeficiency virus) is the best-known example. Because retroviruses can integrate into germline cells, endogenous retroviruses (ERVs) become heritable parts of host genomes and can leave a molecular fossil record. Why the other choices are incorrect: - a. "Fairly old" has nothing to do with the definition of a retrovirus — the "retro" in retrovirus refers to the reverse (backwards) flow of genetic information from RNA to DNA, not to age. - c. A retrovirus is not a resurrected extinct virus; that would be a separate experimental or evolutionary concept. - d. A virus that replicates using only RNA (e.g., influenza) is a standard RNA virus, not a retrovirus. Retroviruses are specifically distinguished by their reverse-transcription step and integration into host DNA. (Chapter 15, Section 15.5)
📷 Show original page
Q023
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. Natural selection does not vary in strength or direction.
Natural selection is not a constant force; it varies in both strength and direction: - a. It varies across space — this is the foundation of the geographic mosaic theory of coevolution; a trait may be strongly favored in one population and selectively neutral or deleterious elsewhere. - b. It varies over time — environmental change, ecological shifts, and arrival or loss of interacting species all alter selection pressures. - c. Within a coevolutionary relationship, different species can experience different strengths and directions of selection at the same time (e.g., hotspots where reciprocal selection is strong, coldspots where it is weak or absent). Therefore statement d, which denies all of this variation, is the false statement. - e. Incorrect because d is indeed false. (Chapter 15)
📷 Show original page
Q024
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. Both a and b.
Müllerian mimicry — where two or more harmful or distasteful species converge on a shared warning signal (e.g., similar aposematic color patterns) — illustrates both: - a. Convergent evolution (pattern): Unrelated toxic species independently evolve similar appearances. The pattern of similar traits arising in separate lineages is classic convergence. - b. Coevolution (process): In Müllerian mimicry, each species exerts reciprocal selection on the others. As predators learn to avoid a common warning signal, any toxic species close to that signal pattern gains protection; selection favors individuals in each species that converge even more closely. Because each species is an agent of selection on the other, this qualifies as coevolution. Why the other choices are incorrect: - c. Speciation is the origin of new species; Müllerian mimicry is not by itself speciation, though it may affect diversification of color patterns within or among species. - e. Both a and b are correct, so "none of the above" is wrong. (Chapter 15)
📷 Show original page
Q025
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: c. Species are in mutualistic coevolutionary relationships.
Highly specialized mutualistic coevolutionary relationships make species extremely dependent on each other. If one species becomes extinct, its obligate partners are likely to go extinct as well. This cascading loss is how coevolution can amplify extinction and rapidly eliminate a group of interdependent species. Examples include obligate pollinator–plant mutualisms (fig–fig wasp, Yucca–yucca moth) and gut microbe–host mutualisms where loss of either partner dooms the other. Why the other choices are incorrect: - a. Antagonistic coevolution (predator–prey, host–parasite arms races) usually produces variation, maintains diversity, and rarely drives rapid multi-species extinction (though it can drive single-species extinction under extreme conditions). - b. Alleles going to fixation is a normal outcome of directional selection; by itself it does not cause rapid extinction of species. - d. Highly virulent virus strains tend to burn out quickly because they kill hosts too fast to transmit (as the myxoma virus example shows). Selection against excessive virulence usually limits this scenario from causing widespread extinction. - e. Since c is correct, "none of the above" is wrong. (Chapter 15, Section 15.3)
📷 Show original page
Q026
p0ContemplateContemplate (Open-ended Discussion)
✓ Answer: (Open-ended — multiple defensible responses.)
This analogy highlights the key components of the geographic mosaic theory: spatial variation in selection intensity, reciprocal interactions among "competitors," and connectivity between populations. It also shows that the theory's logic can apply to any system with variation, differential success, and heredity/transmission — even cultural evolution.
📷 Show original page
Q027
p0ContemplateContemplate (Open-ended Discussion)
✓ Answer: (Open-ended — multiple defensible responses, but a clear "yes" is the standard answer.)
This question highlights human-mediated effects on coevolution: artificial selection substitutes for natural selection, transport disrupts geographic structure (reshaping the mosaic), and direct intervention (pesticides, irrigation, monocultures) can weaken or rewire selection pressures between partners. Understanding these effects is increasingly important for conservation biology, pollination ecology, and sustainable agriculture.

Q001
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: e. All of the above
Variation in the expression of a trait, phenotypic variation that has a genetic (i.e., heritable) component, and variation in additive alleles that contribute to a phenotypic trait are all necessary for natural selection. Some individuals also must survive and reproduce more effectively than others because of these differences (see Sections 2.3 and 8.2). - (a) Correct, but so are other answers. Variation among individuals in the expression of a trait is one of three conditions necessary for evolution by natural selection. - (b) Correct, but so are other answers. Phenotypic variation with a genetic component is necessary because the genetic component is the basis of heritability. - (c) Correct, but so are other answers. Additive alleles contribute to the heritability of a trait. - (d) Incorrect. All three components (a, b, and c) are needed. - (f) Incorrect. All three are required for natural selection.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: c. The strength of selection and how much of the variation in a phenotypic trait is heritable
The evolutionary response to selection is determined by the phenotypic variation that influences fitness (strength of selection) and the ability to transmit those phenotypic characteristics to offspring (heritability): R = S x h^2. If selection is strong, a population can respond even if a trait is only weakly heritable. If a trait's heritability is high, even weak selection can lead to significant evolutionary change. The most rapid evolutionary responses occur when both selection is strong and heritability is high (Section 7.2). - (a) Incorrect. Differential reproductive success influences the strength of selection, but heritability is also required. - (b) Incorrect. Phenotypic variation matters only if heritable. - (d) Incorrect. Genotypic variation only matters if it affects the phenotype; heritable phenotypic variation is what drives response. - (e) Incorrect. Differential reproductive success is part of selection, but heritability is also essential.

p0Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: d. a and c only
Patterning genes, such as Hox genes and limb-patterning networks, demarcate the geography of developing animals, determining the relative locations and sizes of body parts. Within the toolkit, gene networks act as modules that can be deployed in new developmental contexts, yielding novel traits. For 570 million years, mutations—even those that lead to relatively subtle changes to the toolkit—have been able to generate tremendous diversity in the animal kingdom (Section 10.3). - (a) Correct, but so is (c). The same toolkit gene networks govern development across animals. - (b) Incorrect. The genetic toolkit is NOT all identified genes; it refers to the complex of networks shared by an ancient common ancestor of all animals ~570 million years ago. - (c) Correct, but so is (a). Gene networks act like modules deployable in new contexts. - (e) Incorrect. Because (b) is false, not all statements are true. - (f) Incorrect. Because (a) and (c) are both true.

Q030
p0Interpret the DataShort Answer
✓ Answer: Because the results are correlations, the scientists could not determine that higher learning causes shorter life spans. Each experiment supports the hypothesis that learning negatively affects life span, so the evidence is that much stronger.
Reciprocal artificial selection (selecting for each trait separately) provides converging evidence for a causal tradeoff between learning ability and longevity — a stronger inference than a single-direction correlation.

Q032
p0Overcoming MisconceptionsMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: c. Some mutations can result in alleles that enhance the fitness of a group at the expense of the fitness of the individual with those alleles.
Although natural selection typically favors alleles that increase an individual's fitness (since selection is "shortsighted" and acts on immediate fitness consequences), mutations can produce alleles that benefit the group while being costly to the individual — however, such alleles are generally unstable because any mutation that increases individual fitness will spread quickly through the population. - (a) False: individuals in groups do NOT behave for the good of all members; altruistic behavior is actually "selfish" at the allelic level (directly or indirectly benefits the altruist's own fitness). - (b) False: other species (chimpanzees, crows, dolphins, etc.) also have culturally transmitted traits. - (d) False: evolution is neither moral nor immoral — it has no inherent morality. Natural selection simply favors whatever increases survival and reproduction.

Q036
p0Delve DeeperShort Answer
✓ Answer: Learning can be cultural — individuals can change their behavior based on observing other individuals. So information can be passed on from generation to generation, individuals can vary in what they've learned, and having learned can alter their behavior in ways that influence their fitness. But the learned behavior itself is, by definition, not directly inherited. Learning itself is not genetically controlled. Once you learn math, your offspring don't know math. You may have a better capacity to learn math, however, and that capacity can be transferred to some or all of your offspring — if it has a genetic component.
Learning is an individual-lifetime phenomenon (learned content isn't genetically transmitted), while the evolution of learning ability is a population-level, multi-generational process that operates on heritable variation in the CAPACITY to learn.

But the relationship still holds (and the line actually reflects more of an influence of all the other points anyway). The amount of food each individual dog gets increases with increasing pack size.
Even removing the potentially influential pack-size-20 data point, the positive relationship between pack size and per-capita energy intake remains — the trend is driven by many points, not just the high-end outlier.

Q040
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: c. Group selection favors alleles that contribute to the performance of the group, not necessarily the individual.
Group selection arises from variation in fitness among groups; it can favor alleles that boost group performance even if they are costly to individuals carrying them. Individual selection, by contrast, favors alleles that increase the fitness of the individuals carrying them regardless of group-level consequences. The conflict between these levels is the crux of "selfish" vs. altruistic evolution.

Natural selection is shortsighted. Options (a) and (b) mischaracterize evolution; (c) is partially correct but misses the shortsightedness piece that (d) includes.

Game theory, as applied in behavioral ecology, is a mathematical approach to analyzing situations in which the payoff from a given behavior depends on what other individuals do. It is used to solve for optimal strategies (e.g., evolutionarily stable strategies).

Q045
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: a. Helping behavior would cease because the costs of helping would increase relative to the benefits of helping.
Hamilton's rule states rB > C: helping evolves when (relatedness x benefit to recipient) exceeds (cost to helper). If benefits are already low and the cost of helping rises (predators targeting active adult helpers increases their mortality), then C becomes large enough that rB < C — so helping behavior should decline or cease.
📷 Show original page
Q046
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: b. 0.125
The coefficient of relatedness (r) between first cousins is 1/8 = 0.125. This follows from the probability that any given allele in one cousin is identical by descent to an allele in the other (shared grandparent, two generations of 1/2 inheritance on each side: 1/2 x 1/2 x 1/2 x 2 = 1/8).
📷 Show original page
Q047
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: a. Flies with a higher learning index live longer than flies with a lower learning index.
This is the FALSE statement. In fact, Kawecki's experiment showed the opposite — high-learning flies had SHORTER lifespans than controls. Statements (b), (c), (d), and (e) all accurately describe features of the survival and learning-index curves and the tradeoff between learning and longevity.
📷 Show original page
Q048
p0Test YourselfMultiple Choice
✓ Answer: a. Because complex social cognition may lead to extended interaction between individuals and cultural learning about tools
Complex social cognition enables animals to learn from one another by observation and teaching, and to maintain the prolonged social interactions (e.g., between parent and offspring, or within stable social groups) that allow tool-use techniques to be transmitted culturally. Tool use is confined largely to primates and certain birds — lineages with advanced social learning.

This is a reflection prompt that asks students to connect kin-selection theory and inclusive fitness to their own observable family behaviors.

Ch 17 — Human Evolution: A New Kind of Ape

23 questions
Q001
p0Interpret the Data (Ultra-social hypothesis / Herrmann et al. 2007)Interpret the Data
✓ Answer: No. Children should not do better than chimpanzees and orangutans with tests of skills like math because the ultra-social hypothesis makes no prediction about humans, math skills, and development.
Humans and other apes share a common ancestor, a common ancestor that experienced selection for complex cognition, including tool use and sophisticated forms of communication and perhaps even recognition. But the ultra-social hypothesis predicts that humans are fundamentally different from other apes in social cognition. Children should do better than chimpanzees and orangutans on tests related to observation and learning because selection favored rapid development of social cognition in humans.
📷 Show original page
Q002
p0Interpret the Data (Ultra-social hypothesis / Herrmann et al. 2007)Interpret the Data
✓ Answer: Variation among individuals in test scores.
The gray bars and whiskers in the figure depict the distribution/spread of test scores among individuals within each species group. The whiskers extend to indicate individuals at the tails of each species' distribution.

Q003
p0Interpret the Data (Ultra-social hypothesis / Herrmann et al. 2007)Interpret the Data
✓ Answer: A few individual children had test results that overlapped with a few chimpanzee test results, but the majority of children scored higher than the majority of chimpanzees.
The whiskers mark extreme (tail) individuals whose scores overlap between species, while the central part of the distributions (represented by the gray bars) remains separated--consistent with the ultra-social hypothesis's prediction that selection has favored rapid development of social cognition in humans.
📷 Show original page
Q004
p0Games and Exercises (Stone Tool Technology)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: Older technology (Oldowan) consists of simple chipped stones produced by striking a flake off a core--crude choppers and flakes with rough, irregular edges used for basic tasks such as butchering meat. Newer technology (Acheulean and later) shows bifacial flaking, symmetry, standardization of shape (e.g., teardrop hand axes, polyhedrons), finer retouch along edges, and greater functional specialization (scrapers, points, blades).
The evolution of stone tools reflects the evolution of the hominin brain. Stone technology changed over time as hominin lineages evolved the capacity to control their hands, to plan, and to create elaborate tools. Homo sapiens ancestors produced tools demonstrating self-expression, standardization, and evidence of trade (see Chapter 17 text, p. 273).
📷 Show original page
Q005
p0Games and Exercises (Stone Tool Technology)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: Open-ended. Typical uses include: choppers and core tools for breaking bones and cracking nuts; flakes and sharp-edged scrapers for butchering meat and processing hides; hand axes (Acheulean bifaces) for heavy cutting, chopping wood, and butchery; polyhedrons as hammerstones or cores; points (Levallois/later) for hafting onto spears for hunting; blades for cutting, piercing, and fine craftwork.
Tool function can often be inferred from shape (sharp edge = cutting/butchering, heavy/blunt = pounding), wear patterns on the stone, and context of associated remains (e.g., cut-marked animal bones). The increasing specialization of tools over time parallels increasing cognitive capacity in hominins (p. 273).
📷 Show original page
Q006
p0Games and Exercises (Stone Tool Technology)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: Newer tools are more abundant in the archaeological record, are more visually recognizable as tools, are often aesthetically striking (symmetric, well-crafted), and are easier for non-experts to identify as human-made. Older tools (e.g., Oldowan) resemble naturally broken rocks and are harder to distinguish from unmodified stones.
Preservation bias, greater production volume by later hominins, and the "photogenic" appeal of symmetric, well-finished artifacts all contribute. Additionally, newer technology was made by larger populations over longer periods, producing many more artifacts available for discovery and display (p. 273).
📷 Show original page
Q007
p0Games and Exercises (Stone Tool Technology)Games and Exercises
✓ Answer: The cumulative, progressive improvement of stone tools illustrates cultural evolution by descent with modification: each generation built on and modified the techniques of the last, producing increasing complexity, specialization, and efficiency. Modern technology follows the same pattern--successive generations refine, recombine, and specialize prior inventions, accelerated by social learning, language, and trade networks.
Toolmaking evolution shows that human technology is a product of both biological evolution (brain, hands) and cultural transmission. The capacity for planning, manipulating materials, and teaching others allowed exponential accumulation of knowledge--a process continuing today in science and engineering (p. 273).
📷 Show original page
Q008
p0Overcoming MisconceptionsOvercoming Misconceptions
✓ Answer: d. As scientists discover more and more hominin fossils, they may be able to define the genes that distinguish humans from other species.
(a) is false: defining a species is notoriously difficult, and scientists do not agree on a single species concept; classification is a human artifact. (b) is false: humans are still evolving. Natural selection continues to act; for example, women with a genetic tendency for low cholesterol are reproducing at higher rates in Framingham, Massachusetts than women with higher cholesterol. (c) is false: phylogenetic trees are not ladders. Lineages branching off earlier are not primitive; branches can swing like a mobile, and humans are not the pinnacle of evolution. (d) is correct: transitional fossils that together make up the human phylogenetic tree are being uncovered, helping scientists identify the genes that distinguish Homo sapiens from close relatives.
📷 Show original page
Q009
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance
✓ Answer: Stearns et al. (2010) suggest examining two additional long-term study cohorts: (1) participants of the Atherosclerosis Risk in Communities (ARIC) study, and (2) the cohort of women in the Nurses Health Study. They also discuss other long-term cohorts such as the Long Life Family Study.
These cohorts offer decades of longitudinal medical, demographic, and reproductive data on large numbers of individuals, enabling estimation of lifetime reproductive success and the strength of natural selection on specific traits in contemporary populations. See Stearns et al. 2010, Nature Reviews Genetics 11:611-22.
📷 Show original page
Q010
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance
✓ Answer: These studies currently measure health- and disease-related variables: cardiovascular risk factors (blood pressure, total and LDL cholesterol, triglycerides, glucose), anthropometric measurements (height, weight, BMI), age at menarche and menopause, age at first reproduction, completed fertility (number of children), longevity, and incidence of major diseases. They were designed primarily to track risk factors for cardiovascular and other chronic diseases rather than to measure natural selection directly, but the longitudinal demographic and reproductive data they contain allow researchers to estimate selection differentials on these traits.
Stearns and colleagues re-purposed these medical datasets to calculate fitness (lifetime reproductive success) and selection gradients, showing, for example, that selection in Framingham favors shorter and stouter women, lower total cholesterol, lower systolic blood pressure, later menopause, and earlier age at first birth (Stearns et al. 2010).
📷 Show original page
Q011
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance
✓ Answer: Major unresolved issues include: (1) identifying the specific genetic loci on which contemporary selection is acting and estimating their heritabilities in modern environments; (2) distinguishing genetic responses to selection from cultural/environmental change (cultural evolution can mimic or mask genetic change); (3) measuring selection on men, whose reproductive variance and data are less well characterized than those of women; (4) accounting for the mismatch between modern environments (medicine, contraception, nutrition) and the environments in which our traits evolved; (5) reconciling short-term selection differentials with observed long-term evolutionary trajectories (gene-culture coevolution, antagonistic pleiotropy); and (6) the ethical and technical challenges of long-term, representative, multi-generational sampling.
Stearns et al. (2010) argue that although we can now detect selection in contemporary populations, predicting its evolutionary consequences requires data on heritability in current environments, pleiotropic trade-offs, and the relative contributions of genetic versus cultural transmission, all of which remain difficult to estimate.
📷 Show original page
Q012
p0Go the Distance: Examine the Primary LiteratureGo the Distance
✓ Answer: No. Stearns and colleagues conclude that while current selection pressures on contemporary human populations can be measured, long-term evolutionary outcomes cannot be reliably predicted. Heritabilities, genetic correlations, and the environment itself are all changing, and cultural evolution (technology, medicine, lifestyle) interacts with and can dominate genetic change.
They emphasize that detecting ongoing selection is not the same as forecasting evolutionary trajectories. Future selection will depend on environmental shifts (including climate), cultural change, and gene-culture interactions that cannot be fully anticipated (Stearns et al. 2010).
📷 Show original page
Q013
p0Delve DeeperDelve Deeper
✓ Answer: Dopamine and oxytocin.
Dopamine is a neurotransmitter that arouses an animal attention. Rats and other mammals share this neurotransmitter, although they may respond differently than humans. Oxytocin is a hormone that elicits bonding behavior in mammals, including humans. Smell usually causes the release of this hormone in other mammals, but in humans, release is related to sight, which is consistent with other shifts toward sight in the apes.
📷 Show original page
Q014
p0Delve DeeperDelve Deeper
✓ Answer: Stone tools may have been used as early as 3.4 million years ago, although the strongest evidence indicates that stone tools were in use commonly around 2.6 million years ago. This new technology indicated a major transition in how the brains of our ancestors worked. The capacity to recognize and manipulate our surroundings is a function of complex cognition. The discovery of tools potentially as old as 3.4 million years ago indicates that species that branched off much earlier than our own may have been using tools.
Stone tool technology has also evolved, and the tools discovered in the fossil record provide additional evidence of the course of our evolution, including how our ancestors interacted with the environment as well as other species, and the evolution of components of culture such as trade and migration.
📷 Show original page
Q015
p0Delve DeeperDelve Deeper
✓ Answer: The external environment a gene is exposed to during development, and pleiotropy.
The external environment a developing individual is exposed to can play an important role in gene expression. The temperature an individual develops in, the food available to it, and the infections it suffers can all influence gene expression and development. Even the social environment, such as presence or absence of affection from parents, can alter gene expression and development. Different environments can lead to very different phenotypes from a single genotype. The environment is not restricted to the external conditions around us, however; it can be anything that interacts with the promoter region of a gene in a way that influences whether that gene is expressed. Pleiotropy occurs when a single gene affects the expression of many different phenotypic traits; antagonistic pleiotropy is when the beneficial effects for one trait cause detrimental effects on other traits. So pleiotropic effects can be very important factors influencing gene expression in humans. For example, animals as different from us as insects use the same genetic toolkit to regulate development. Genes at the top of these regulatory hierarchies influence many others, and mutations in a few of these regulatory genes can produce far-reaching changes, including limb loss and the development of novel traits.
📷 Show original page
Q016
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: e. All of the above
All four statements are simultaneously true of the primate phylogeny in Figure 17.3: (a) Humans and lemurs share a deep common ancestor (all primates do). (b) The error bars/shaded bars around nodes represent confidence intervals on the age of each node; they show timing uncertainty, not topology uncertainty. (c) Based on molecular-clock estimates in the figure, the split between the human lineage and the lineage containing the other great apes is dated at approximately 8.4 Ma. (d) The more recent Homo-Pan split is better constrained (narrower confidence interval) than the much older Platyrrhini-Catarrhini split.
📷 Show original page
Q017
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: b. The human branch of our evolutionary tree that includes the genus Homo and its close relatives such as Australopithecus and Paranthropus, but not chimpanzees.
The hominin clade (subtribe Hominina) comprises modern humans plus all extinct species more closely related to us than to chimpanzees: Homo (all species including sapiens), Australopithecus, Paranthropus, Ardipithecus, Sahelanthropus, and other close relatives. It excludes Pan (chimpanzees/bonobos), Gorilla, and Pongo (orangutans), which belong to the broader Hominidae (great apes). (a) is wrong because it includes Pan, Gorilla, and Pongo. (c) and (d) are wrong because they exclude the australopithecines/paranthropines that are members of the hominin clade.
📷 Show original page
Q018
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: a. Acheulean
Homo heidelbergensis is associated with the Acheulean stone tool industry, characterized by symmetrical bifacial hand axes. Oldowan is the older, simpler industry (2.6-1.7 Ma) associated with early Homo and late australopithecines. Levallois (prepared-core) technology is a later Middle Paleolithic technique associated primarily with Neanderthals and early Homo sapiens. Stone is not a named technology.

Q019
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: e. b and c only
The ultra-social hypothesis predicts that humans differ from other apes specifically in social cognition, not in physical-domain cognition. Thus (b) is true: children should outperform chimpanzees and orangutans on social skills tests (observation/learning). (c) is also true: the hypothesis predicts no human advantage on physics/math tests, since those rely on cognition shared with a common ancestor that evolved tool use and basic causal reasoning. (a) is wrong because the ultra-social hypothesis does not predict a human advantage in math.

Q020
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: d. The high levels of clumping that align with the geography of Europe are likely a product of genetic drift and founder events.
Figure 17.35 (Novembre et al. genetic map of Europe) shows that individual genetic ancestry closely tracks country of origin: the principal-components plot of European genomes resembles a map of Europe. This spatial clumping reflects limited gene flow over hundreds of generations, so that genetic drift and founder events have produced modest but detectable differentiation between geographic populations. (a) is too strong; there is overlap between neighboring countries. (b) is false; a clear pattern exists. (c) is false; isolated populations can differ but the strongest signal is geographic distance/proximity. (e) is false; genetic clumping in Europe reflects isolation by distance and drift, not speciation.

Q021
p0Test YourselfTest Yourself (Multiple Choice)
✓ Answer: e. b and c only
Larry Young and colleagues took advantage of the difference between monogamous prairie voles (high vasopressin V1a receptor expression in certain brain regions) and promiscuous meadow voles (low expression). Using a viral vector, they increased V1a receptor expression in the ventral forebrain of male meadow voles. These experimental meadow voles showed pair-bonding (partner preference) similar to that of monogamous prairie voles, unlike control meadow voles (both b and c describe this correctly). (a) is false; Young did not manipulate prairie voles to become more monogamous; the experimental manipulation was in meadow voles.
📷 Show original page
Q022
p0ContemplateContemplate
✓ Answer: No. Human populations worldwide interbreed freely and produce fully fertile offspring, so under the biological species concept all humans belong to a single species, Homo sapiens. Genetic differences among human populations are modest; most genetic variation occurs within, not between, populations, and there is continuous gene flow across geographic regions. No human population is reproductively isolated. Classifying populations as separate species is therefore neither biologically justified nor scientifically useful.
Although populations differ in some allele frequencies (e.g., lactase persistence, skin-pigmentation alleles, altitude adaptations), these are population-level adaptations within one species, not the deep reproductive isolation or divergence that defines species. As emphasized in the Overcoming Misconceptions section, species is not a precisely defined category, and attempting to carve humans into distinct species imposes a false discontinuity on a continuous pattern of variation, an error with well-known harmful social consequences.
📷 Show original page
Q023
p0ContemplateContemplate
✓ Answer: Anthropogenic climate change is likely to act as a strong and novel selective pressure on humans. Possible effects include: (1) selection for heat tolerance and efficient thermoregulation as regions warm; (2) altered disease environments in which range expansion of mosquito- and tick-borne pathogens (malaria, dengue, Lyme) may favor resistance alleles; (3) shifts in food and water availability that may select for metabolic and immune traits suited to new diets and microbiota; (4) large-scale migration driven by sea-level rise, drought, and extreme weather, increasing gene flow among previously separated populations and potentially reducing between-population differentiation; (5) demographic bottlenecks or expansions linked to famine, conflict, or displacement that could amplify genetic drift in some populations. Cultural and technological responses (medicine, agriculture, air conditioning) may buffer or alter these pressures in unpredictable ways.
Human evolution has not stopped. Past climate-driven adaptations (skin pigmentation, altitude tolerance such as EPAS1 in Tibetans, lactase persistence, sickle-cell/thalassemia in malarial zones) illustrate how environmental change shapes human genomes. As the textbook notes (p. 262), the effects we are having on our climate and environment may turn out to be strong selective factors in our future, but the exact trajectory is difficult to predict because culture, technology, and biology interact.
📷 Show original page

Q001
p290Evolving Resistance (Games and Exercises)Data Analysis
Scientists collected data on antibiotic use and the evolution of resistance from a community in Finland from 1978 to 1993. They looked at the annual amount of antibiotics used by members of the community and compared that with samples of bacteria from young children with middle ear infections. They examined the bacterial strains to see if they were susceptible to (killed by) or resistant to (not killed by) these antibiotics. The table below lists the year, the amount of antibiotics used, and the percentage of the bacterial strains that were resistant to the antibiotic (from 0 to 100 percent). Year | Annual Antibiotic Usage | % Resistant Strains 1978 | 0.84 | 0 1979 | 0.92 | 2 1980 | 1.04 | 29 1981 | 0.98 | 46 1982 | 1.02 | 45 Use this data to make two different graphs: (1) antibiotic usage versus year (graph year on the x-axis and annual antibiotic usage on the y-axis); and (2) percent resistant strains versus year (graph year on the x-axis and percent resistant strains on the y-axis). Did the annual antibiotic usage in this community increase, decrease, or stay the same over the course of the study?
✓ Answer: Increased
The annual antibiotic usage in this community increased over the course of the study, rising from 0.84 in 1978 to approximately 1.02 in 1982 and continuing upward over the full 1978–1993 period.

Q002
p291Evolving Resistance (Games and Exercises)Short Answer
Did the percentage of bacterial strains resistant to these antibiotics increase, decrease, or stay the same over this same time period?
✓ Answer: Increased
The percentage of resistant strains increased dramatically — from 0% in 1978 to 45–46% by 1981–1982. This is classic directional selection: antibiotic exposure killed susceptible strains while resistant strains survived and reproduced.
📷 Show original page
Q003
p291Evolving Resistance (Games and Exercises)Short Answer
From these data, do you think antibiotic usage was related to percentage of strains resistant to the antibiotics?
✓ Answer: Yes — positively related
Both variables rose together over time. The strong positive correlation between increasing antibiotic exposure and increasing resistance frequency supports a causal link mediated by natural selection on the bacterial population.
📷 Show original page
Q004
p291Evolving Resistance (Games and Exercises)Short Answer
Do you think one of these factors was the cause of the other factor? If you do, which one?
✓ Answer: Yes — antibiotic usage caused resistance to rise
Antibiotic usage acts as a selective pressure: it kills susceptible bacteria and leaves resistant ones to reproduce. So heavier use → stronger selection → higher resistance frequency in the population.
📷 Show original page
Q005
p291Evolving Resistance (Games and Exercises)Short Answer
How might you be able to tell for sure?
✓ Answer: By controlled experiment — reducing antibiotic use and watching resistance change
To confirm causation, reduce antibiotic use in a comparable community and measure whether resistance drops. If resistance falls when the selective pressure is removed, causation is supported (see next question for the actual follow-up study).
📷 Show original page
Q006
p291Evolving Resistance (Games and Exercises)Short Answer
In another study, also in Finland, researchers investigated the effect of greatly limiting the use of an antibiotic on a community (kids still got ear infections, but they now used a 'wait and see' approach to see if the infection would clear up on its own). After antibiotic usage was greatly decreased, the percentage of bacterial strains that were resistant to this antibiotic was decreased by 50 percent (cut in half). What does this new information tell you?
✓ Answer: Confirms causation: reducing selection pressure reverses resistance
Removing the selective pressure (antibiotic exposure) allowed resistant bacteria — which often carry a fitness cost in the absence of antibiotics — to be outcompeted by susceptible strains. This is real-time evolution, reversible by removing the selective agent. It supports that antibiotic use was driving the resistance increase.
📷 Show original page
Q007
p290Check Your UnderstandingMultiple Choice
Which is NOT one of the three conditions necessary for evolution by natural selection to occur?
  • A)Individuals must differ in the characteristics of a trait.
  • B)The differences among individuals in a trait must be at least partially heritable.
  • C)Some individuals survive and reproduce more than others because of those differences.
  • D)All individuals in the population must carry the same mutation.
✓ Answer: d
The three conditions for natural selection are: (1) phenotypic variation exists, (2) variation is at least partially heritable, and (3) variation affects differential survival/reproduction (fitness). Choice d (all individuals carrying the same mutation) would ELIMINATE the variation needed — so it is NOT a requirement.
📷 Show original page
Q008
p293Delve DeeperShort Answer
How does antibiotic resistance in bacteria illustrate natural selection?
✓ Answer: See explanation
Antibiotic resistance is natural selection in action: (1) bacterial populations contain genetic variation (mutations, plasmid-borne resistance genes); (2) this variation is heritable (passed to daughter cells and via horizontal gene transfer); (3) antibiotics cause differential survival — susceptible strains die, resistant strains reproduce. Over time the resistant phenotype increases in frequency in the population. This is exactly Darwin's three-step mechanism operating on observable timescales.
📷 Show original page
Q009
p293Delve DeeperShort Answer
Explain why evolutionary thinking improves medical diagnosis and treatment.
✓ Answer: See explanation
Evolutionary medicine adds the 'why' to the clinical 'how'. Understanding that fever, pain, and inflammation are evolved defenses prevents inappropriate suppression. Recognizing pathogens as adaptive organisms explains resistance evolution and informs stewardship. Seeing cancer as somatic evolution predicts chemotherapy resistance. Recognizing mismatch between evolved biology and modern environments explains many chronic diseases (obesity, myopia, autoimmunity).
📷 Show original page
Q010
p293Delve DeeperShort Answer
What are some evolutionary reasons we have inherited vulnerabilities (bad backs, wisdom-tooth impaction, birth complications)?
✓ Answer: See explanation
Evolutionary constraints: (1) Design compromises — bipedal spines and hips evolved from quadrupedal ancestors, so back pain and birth difficulties reflect co-opted anatomy. (2) Life-history trade-offs — large neonatal heads benefit cognition but complicate birth. (3) Diet-tooth mismatch — our jaws shrank faster than our teeth under agricultural diets, producing impaction. (4) Slow evolution — cultural/technological change outpaces biological adaptation, creating mismatches with modern environments.
📷 Show original page
Q011
p294Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which of the following is a reason we get sick, according to Nesse and Williams?
  • A)Defenses (like fever) that can feel like illness.
  • B)Infections from rapidly evolving pathogens.
  • C)Novel environments and design trade-offs.
  • D)Slow evolution of humans vs. pathogens.
  • E)All of the above
✓ Answer: e
Nesse & Williams identified six classes of reasons: defenses, infection, novel environments, genes with trade-offs, design compromises, and slow human evolution. All options a–d are valid members of that set.

Q012
p294Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Why is antibiotic resistance considered a textbook example of natural selection?
  • A)Bacteria can learn to resist.
  • B)Antibiotics select pre-existing resistant variants that then reproduce more than susceptibles.
  • C)All bacteria are resistant from birth.
  • D)Resistance appears only after antibiotic use begins.
  • E)Resistance requires human intervention to evolve.
✓ Answer: b
Antibiotics filter existing genetic variation — they don't induce it. Resistant variants (from random mutation or horizontal gene transfer) survive antibiotic exposure and reproduce, while susceptibles die. This is Darwinian selection acting on a bacterial population in real time.
📷 Show original page
Q013
p294Test YourselfMultiple Choice
The sickle-cell allele persists at high frequency in malaria-endemic regions because:
  • A)HbS homozygotes resist malaria.
  • B)Mutations spontaneously re-create HbS.
  • C)HbS is selectively neutral.
  • D)Heterozygotes (HbA/HbS) have higher fitness than either homozygote.
✓ Answer: d
This is balancing selection via heterozygote advantage. HbA/HbS heterozygotes resist malaria without developing full sickle-cell disease, making them fitter than either HbA/HbA (malaria-susceptible) or HbS/HbS (sickle-cell disease).
📷 Show original page
Q014
p294Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Fever during infection is best understood as:
  • A)An evolved host defense that inhibits pathogen growth.
  • B)A malfunction of the immune system.
  • C)Caused by the pathogen for its benefit.
  • D)Always harmful and needing suppression.
✓ Answer: a
Elevated body temperature inhibits replication of many pathogens and enhances immune-cell function. Fever is an evolved defense — not the disease itself — so aggressive fever suppression can sometimes prolong infection.

Q015
p294Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which is an example of a 'mismatch disease'?
  • A)Smallpox
  • B)Type 2 diabetes
  • C)Down syndrome
  • D)Measles
  • E)Sickle-cell anemia
✓ Answer: b
Mismatch diseases arise when traits that evolved under ancestral conditions become maladaptive in modern environments. Type 2 diabetes reflects a mismatch between ancestral metabolism (thrifty under scarcity) and modern diets (chronic caloric excess).

Q016
p295Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Why is cancer particularly difficult to eliminate with chemotherapy?
  • A)Tumors are genetically uniform.
  • B)Cancer cells never divide.
  • C)Chemo is ineffective against all cancers.
  • D)The body rejects chemo.
  • E)Cancers only spread by contact.
✓ Answer: f
Cancers are populations of somatic cells. Heterogeneity provides variation; chemo imposes selection; resistant clones expand. This is evolution by natural selection within an individual's lifetime — the same logic as antibiotic resistance.
📷 Show original page
Q017
p295Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Aging (senescence) is best explained evolutionarily by:
  • A)Genes programmed to kill the organism at a set age.
  • B)Oxidative damage alone.
  • C)Telomere shortening alone.
  • D)Weak selection on late-life traits → antagonistic pleiotropy and mutation accumulation.
  • E)Random chance.
✓ Answer: d
Because most reproduction occurs early, selection is weak against alleles that cause late-life harm. Williams' antagonistic pleiotropy (alleles good early, bad late) and Medawar's mutation accumulation produce senescence as an evolutionary side-effect — not a programmed process.

Q018
p295Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which of the following is/are evolutionary design compromises in humans?
  • A)Narrow birth canal vs. large neonatal head.
  • B)Recurrent laryngeal nerve's long detour.
  • C)Crossed path of air and food in the pharynx.
  • D)Retinal 'blind spot' from inverted vasculature.
  • E)Upright spine prone to back pain.
✓ Answer: f
Each feature is a 'good-enough' legacy of ancestry that couldn't be redesigned: the nerve detours around ancient branchial arches; the pharyngeal crossover came from fish ancestry; the inverted retina came from early chordate eyes; the upright spine came from quadrupeds. Selection modifies existing structures.

Q019
p295Test YourselfMultiple Choice
The hygiene hypothesis proposes that:
  • A)Hand-washing causes allergies.
  • B)Too-clean early environments disrupt immune development, increasing allergies/autoimmunity.
  • C)All infections are beneficial.
  • D)Hygiene is unnecessary.
  • E)Hygiene caused cancer.
✓ Answer: b
The immune system co-evolved with a rich microbial milieu. Modern ultra-hygienic environments may underexpose developing immune systems, increasing rates of allergies, asthma, and autoimmune diseases. It does NOT argue against basic hygiene.
📷 Show original page
Q020
p296Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Pathogens with vector-borne or waterborne transmission tend to evolve:
  • A)Lower virulence than direct-contact pathogens.
  • B)The same virulence as direct-contact pathogens.
  • C)Higher virulence — because hosts don't need to be mobile for transmission.
  • D)No virulence at all.
  • E)Intermittent virulence.
✓ Answer: c
Virulence evolves with transmission mode. When transmission doesn't require the host to move (waterborne, vector-borne), pathogens can exploit hosts heavily without losing transmission. Direct-contact pathogens depend on mobile hosts and usually evolve lower virulence.
📷 Show original page
Q021
p296Test YourselfMultiple Choice
Which of the following best summarizes the evolutionary-medicine perspective?
  • A)Replace clinical medicine with evolutionary theory.
  • B)Treat all diseases as adaptations.
  • C)Ignore proximate mechanisms.
  • D)Complement clinical practice by asking WHY our bodies are vulnerable, not just HOW disease works.
  • E)Treat pathogens as unchanging.
✓ Answer: d
Evolutionary medicine complements — does not replace — mechanistic medicine. It adds the 'ultimate why' (evolutionary history, trade-offs, mismatch) to the clinical 'proximate how'. Understanding both yields better prevention, diagnosis, and treatment.

Q022
p296ContemplateOpen-Ended Reflection
How might an evolutionary perspective change how we approach antibiotic stewardship in clinical practice and agriculture?
✓ Answer: Model Answer
Treat antibiotics as a finite shared resource subject to evolutionary depletion. Clinical: prescribe only when needed, use combination therapy to slow resistance, shorten courses when safe, rotate agents across populations, monitor resistance trends. Agriculture: eliminate growth-promotion uses, restrict prophylactic use, improve animal husbandry. The framing is: resistance is INEVITABLE under selection — plan for it rather than react to it.

Q023
p296ContemplateOpen-Ended Reflection
Consider a disease where evolutionary thinking has NOT yet been applied to treatment. How might it change the clinical approach?
✓ Answer: Model Answer
Example — chronic inflammatory disease (e.g., ulcerative colitis). An evolutionary lens would ask: what ancestral environments shaped our gut-immune system? (rich microbial exposure, high-fiber diet, parasitic helminths). Modern mismatch might explain dysregulated inflammation. Therapeutic implications: restore microbial diversity (diet, FMT, helminth therapy), rather than only suppressing immune response with pharmaceuticals.
